]
.

Chapter 4
Zhelobenko type operators for q-W–algebras

In this chapter we define a quantum analogue
\Pi^{q}
of the operator
\Pi
and apply it to describe q-W–algebras. Observe that the operator
\Pi
is defined using the conjugation action and operators of multiplication by the functions
G_{p}
in the space
\mathbb{C}[N_{-}ZsM_{-}]
. The conjugation action has a natural quantum group analogue, the adjoint action. But multiplication by functions in
\mathbb{C}[G]
is quite far from the multiplication in the algebra
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]
which is used in the definition of q-W–algebras. However, using isomorphism (
3.2.19
) of Ad–modules
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]
and
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
we can try to describe q-W–algebras in terms of the space
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
multiplication in which is more closely related to that of
\mathbb{C}[G]
. Therefore it is natural to expect that a quantum analogue of the operator
\Pi
, if it exists at all, should be defined in terms of the adjoint action and of operators of multiplication in
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
using appropriate quantum analogues of formulas (
3.5.10
), (
3.5.11
) and (
3.5.12
). We shall see that this conjecture is almost correct. In fact
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
should be replaced with a certain localization. More precisely, recall that the operator
\Pi
is defined using the functions
G_{p}
given by (
3.5.10
). Natural analogues of matrix elements which appear in formula (
3.5.10
) can be defined in the algebra
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
. But formula (
3.5.10
) contains some artificial denominators which are canceled in the formula for
\Pi
(see Remark
3.5.3
). It turns out that in the quantum case formulas similar to (
3.5.10
) make sense but the denominators in them are not canceled in the formula for
\Pi^{q}
, and we are forced to replace
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
with a localization containing all such denominators. This will also force us to replace the algebra
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s}(G)
with a certain localization
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)
of it.

The main difficulty in defining a quantum analogue
\Pi^{q}
of the operator
\Pi
is that the proof of the fact that the operator defined by (
3.5.12
) is a projection operator onto
W^{s}(G)
is based on isomorphism (
3.4.29
) a quantum counterpart of which does not make sense. Recall that
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s}(G)
is the space of invariants with respect to the adjoint action of
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]
on
Q_{\mathcal{B}}
. Although quantum analogues of operators (
3.5.11
) can be defined the proof of the fact that their composition similar to (
3.5.12
) is a projection operator on the localization
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)
of
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s}(G)
should only use the algebra structure of
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
, the properties of the adjoint action of
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]
on
Q_{\mathcal{B}}
, and the structure of
Q_{\mathcal{B}}
. These are the only technical tools in our disposal.

Thus our first task is to describe in terms of
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
the
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]
–module
Q_{\mathcal{B}}
originally defined using
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]
. In the classical case this would correspond to describing the vanishing ideal of the closed subvariety
N_{-}ZsM_{-}\simeq N_{-}ZsM_{-}^{s}\subset G
as by Lemma
3.4.1
and by Theorem
3.4.5

Q_{\mathcal{B}}/(q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}-1)Q_{\mathcal{B}}\simeq\mathbb{C}[N_{-}ZsM_{-}^{s}]
. It turns out that not all elements of
\mathbb{C}[G]
generating the vanishing ideal of
N_{-}ZsM_{-}^{s}
have nice quantum counterparts in
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
. Recall that
\mathbb{C}[G]
is
P_{+}\times P_{+}
–graded via the left and the right regular action of
H
on
G
. The subvariety
N_{-}ZsM_{-}^{s}\subset G
is closed and some generators of its vanishing ideal belong to the graded components and some do not. It turns out that at least some of the generators of the latter type have no nice quantum counterparts. But for our purposes it suffices to replace
N_{-}ZsM_{-}^{s}
with a larger variety
\overline{N_{-}ZHsM_{-}^{s}}
the vanishing ideal of which has a nice quantum counterpart
{\overline{J}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}
in
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
. This counterpart is described in Proposition
4.1.2
and its image under the natural map
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]\simeq\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]\rightarrow Q_{\mathcal{B}}
is zero.

After recollecting some facts on the algebra
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
and on the adjoint action in Section
4.2
we study properties of
{\overline{J}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}
in Section
4.3
. For technical reasons related to the non–commutativity of the algebra
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
we also introduce and study a
\mathcal{B}
–submodule
J^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}\subset{\overline{J}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}
which is a quantum counterpart of the vanishing ideal of
\overline{N_{-}sZHN_{-}^{\prime}}\supset\overline{N_{-}ZHsM_{-}^{s}}
.

In order to show that
\Pi^{q}
is a projection operator on
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)
we shall need more relations which resemble relations in the algebras
\mathbb{C}[\overline{N_{[-\beta_{p},-\beta_{D}]}sZHM_{-}^{s}}],\mathbb{C}[\overline{N_{[-\beta_{p},-\beta_{D}]}sZHN_{-}^{\prime}}]
of regular functions on the varieties
\overline{N_{[-\beta_{p},-\beta_{D}]}sZHM_{-}^{s}}\subset\overline{N_{-}sZHM_{-}^{s}}
and
\overline{N_{[-\beta_{p},-\beta_{D}]}sZHN_{-}^{\prime}}\subset\overline{N_{-}sZHN_{-}^{\prime}}
introduced in Corollary
3.5.5
. These algebras are closely related to the algebras of regular functions on the subvarieties
{N_{[-\beta_{p},-\beta_{D}]}sZM_{-}^{s}}\subset{N_{-}sZM_{-}^{s}}
. The use of these subvarieties in our setting comes from that fact that elements of
{N_{[-\beta_{p},-\beta_{D}]}sZM_{-}^{s}}
are obtained from elements
g\in{N_{-}sZM_{-}^{s}}
by conjugation by
n_{p}^{-1}
as in Proposition
3.5.2
. This conjugation corresponds to applying the operator
\Pi_{1}\circ\ldots\circ\Pi_{p-1}
in the algebra
\mathbb{C}[N_{-}ZsM_{-}^{s}]
, so that the vanishing ideal of
{N_{[-\beta_{p},-\beta_{D}]}sZM_{-}^{s}}
belongs to the kernel of this operator. For the purposes of quantization we have to replace the varieties
{N_{[-\beta_{p},-\beta_{D}]}sZM_{-}^{s}}
with the larger varieties
\overline{N_{[-\beta_{p},-\beta_{D}]}sZHM_{-}^{s}}\subset\overline{N_{-}sZHM_{-}^{s}},\overline{N_{[-\beta_{p},-\beta_{D}]}sZHN_{-}^{\prime}}\subset\overline{N_{-}sZHN_{-}^{\prime}}
,
{N_{[-\beta_{p},-\beta_{D}]}sZM_{-}^{s}}\subset\overline{N_{[-\beta_{p},-\beta_{D}]}sZHM_{-}^{s}},\overline{N_{[-\beta_{p},-\beta_{D}]}sZHN_{-}^{\prime}}
due to the reasons mentioned above, and the vanishing ideals of these extended varieties have well–defined quantum analogues
\overline{J}^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}
and
J^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}
defined and studied in Section
4.4
. Note that since we define all our algebras over the ring
\mathcal{B}
, for technical reasons, we shall have to consider a priori slightly larger ideals
\overline{I}^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}=(\overline{J}^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}\otimes\mathbb{C}(q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}))\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
and
I^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}=(J^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}\otimes\mathbb{C}(q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}))\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
.

In Section
4.5
we introduce the localizations mentioned above and study their properties and the relevant properties of the adjoint action. The results obtained in Sections
4.1
,
4.2
,
4.3
,
4.4
and
4.5
are prerequisites for the study of the properties of the quantum analogues
P_{p}
of the operators
\Pi_{p}
and of their compositions in Section
4.6
, the main properties being summarized in Proposition
4.6.1
. In Proposition
4.6.7
we also define quantum analogues of monomials in variables
G_{p}
,
p=1,\ldots,c
which play a crucial role in the study of equivariant modules over a quantum group and in the proof of the De Concini–Kac–Procesi conjecture.

Finally in Section
4.7
we prove that the image of the operator
\Pi^{q}
almost coincides with the localization
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)
of the algebra
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s}(G)
.

4.1
A quantum analogue of the level surface of the moment map for q-W–algebras

In this section we describe a quantum counterpart
{\overline{J}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}\subset\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
of the vanishing ideal of the variety
\overline{N_{-}ZHsM_{-}^{s}}\supset{N_{-}ZsM_{-}^{s}}
containing the closure
\overline{q(\mu_{M_{-}}^{-1}(u))}=\mathbb{C}[N_{-}ZsM_{-}^{s}]
of the level surface of the moment map
\mu_{M_{-}}
corresponding to the element
u\in M_{+}
. As we mentioned in the introduction to this chapter isomorphism (
3.2.19
) of Ad–modules
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]
and
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
plays a central role in the description of
{\overline{J}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}
.
However, for technical reasons we replace the adjoint action of
U_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,res}(\mathfrak{g})
on
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
with the twisted adjoint action defined by

({\rm Ad}_{0}xf)(w)=f(\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1}({\rm Ad}^{\prime}x(S_{s}\omega_{0}w)))=f((\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})(x^{1})w\omega_{0}(x^{2})),

(4.1.1)

where
f\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G],x,w\in U_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,res}(\mathfrak{g})
, and consider isomorphism (
3.2.19
) twisted by
\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1}
,

\varphi:\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[{G}_{*}],f\mapsto(id\otimes f)(id\otimes\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})(\mathcal{R}^{s}_{21}\mathcal{R}^{s}).

(4.1.2)

If
\kappa=1
it induces a homomorphism of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]
–modules

\phi:\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]\rightarrow Q_{\mathcal{B}},\phi(f)=\varphi(f)1,

(4.1.3)

where
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
is equipped with the restriction of action (
4.1.1
) to
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]
and
Q_{\mathcal{B}}
with the action induced by the adjoint action
\rm Ad
of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]
and
1
is the image of
1\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]
in
Q_{\mathcal{B}}
under the natural map
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]\rightarrow Q_{\mathcal{B}}
.

The following proposition is a quantum counterpart of Proposition
3.4.11
. The proof of Proposition
3.4.11
was based on the Chevalley commutation relations between one–parameter subgroups in
G
as described in Lemma
1.3.1
and on formula (
3.4.9
) for representatives of Weyl group elements in
G
. In the quantum case instead of the Chevalley commutation relations we have commutation relations between quantum root vectors, and the Weyl group is replaced with the corresponding braid group generators of which are also expressed in terms on generators of the quantum group by formula (
2.2.3
). However, in the quantum case the generators of the braid group do not square to identity automorphisms of the quantum group and we are only allowed to use the braid group relations. The action of the braid group on quantum root vectors is also very difficult to control. It is much more complicated than the conjugation action of representatives in
G
of Weyl group elements on root vectors in
\mathfrak{g}
. All this brings additional complications to the proof of Proposition
4.1.2
.

As before we assume that a Weyl group element
s\in W
and an ordered system of positive roots
\Delta_{+}
associated to
s
are fixed, and denote by
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{D}
the ordered roots in
\Delta_{+}
as in Section
1.6
.

Let
U_{q}^{res}(w^{\prime}(\mathfrak{b}_{+}))
be the subalgebra in
U_{q}^{res}(\mathfrak{g})

generated by the elements

({X}^{-}_{\beta_{k_{l^{\prime}}}})^{(n_{k_{l^{\prime}}})},\ldots,({X}^{-}_{\beta_{1}})^{(n_{1})},(X^{+}_{\beta_{k_{l^{\prime}}+1}})^{(n_{k_{l^{\prime}}+1})},\ldots,(X^{+}_{\beta_{D}})^{(n_{D})},n_{i}\in\mathbb{N},i=1,\ldots,D,

where
\beta_{k_{l^{\prime}}}=\gamma_{l^{\prime}}
, and by
U_{q}^{res}(H)
.

Below we denote the multiplication in the algebra
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
by
\otimes
. We shall also use the following notation for elements of
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
. Let
V^{res}
be a
U_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,res}(\mathfrak{g})
–lattice in a finite rank
U_{h}(\mathfrak{g})
–module
V
. Recall that there is a contravariant non–degenerate bilinear form
(\cdot,\cdot)
on
V
such that
(u,xv)=(\omega(x)u,v)
for any
u,v\in V
,
x\in U_{h}(\mathfrak{g})
. Assume that
u
is such that
(u,w)\in\mathcal{B}
for any
w\in V^{res}
. Then
(u,\cdot)
is an element of the dual module
{V^{res}}^{*}
. Since
V
and
V^{res}
are of finite ranks and
(\cdot,\cdot)
is non–degenerate all elements of
{V^{res}}^{*}
can be obtained this way. Clearly, for any
v\in V^{res}

(u,\cdot v)\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
, and by the definition
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
is generated by such elements.

Let
c_{\beta}\in\mathcal{B}
,
\beta\in\Delta_{\mathfrak{m}_{+}}
be elements such that

c_{\beta}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}k_{i}\in\mathcal{B}&{\rm if}~\beta=\gamma_{i},i=1,\ldots,l^{\prime}\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{array}\right..

As we observed in the proof of Proposition
3.2.6
the elements
\tilde{e}_{\beta_{1}}^{n_{1}}\ldots\tilde{e}_{\beta_{D}}^{n_{D}}V_{i}\tilde{f}_{\beta_{D}}^{k_{D}}\ldots\tilde{f}_{\beta_{1}}^{k_{1}}
with
n_{j},k_{j},i\in\mathbb{N}
,
j=1,\ldots,D
form a
\mathcal{B}
–basis in
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]
.

Clearly, the elements
\tilde{e}_{\beta_{1}}^{n_{1}}\ldots\tilde{e}_{\beta_{D}}^{n_{D}}V_{i}\tilde{f}_{\beta_{D}}^{k_{D}}\ldots\tilde{f}_{\beta_{c+1}}^{k_{c+1}}(\tilde{f}_{\beta_{c}}-c_{\beta_{c}})^{k_{c}}\ldots(\tilde{f}_{\beta_{1}}-c_{\beta_{1}})^{k_{1}}
with
n_{j},k_{j},i\in\mathbb{N}
,
j=1,\ldots,D
also form a
\mathcal{B}
–basis in
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]
.
Let
I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}
be the
\mathcal{B}
–submodule in
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]
generated by the elements

\tilde{e}_{\beta_{1}}^{n_{1}}\ldots\tilde{e}_{\beta_{D}}^{n_{D}}V_{i}\tilde{f}_{\beta_{D}}^{k_{D}}\ldots\tilde{f}_{\beta_{c+1}}^{k_{c+1}}(\tilde{f}_{\beta_{c}}-c_{\beta_{c}})^{k_{c}}\ldots(\tilde{f}_{\beta_{1}}-c_{\beta_{1}})^{k_{1}}

with
n_{j},k_{j},i\in\mathbb{N}
,
j=1,\ldots,D
, and where at least one
k_{j}>0
for
j<c+1
. Since these elements are linearly independent they form a
\mathcal{B}
–basis in
I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}
.

Proposition 4.1.1
.

Let
{\overline{J}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}
be the left ideal in
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
generated by the elements
(u,\cdot v)\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
, where
u
is a highest weight vector in a finite rank representation
V
of
U_{h}(\mathfrak{g})
, and
v\in V^{res}
is such that
(u,T_{s}xv)=0
for any
x\in U_{q}^{res}(w^{\prime}(\mathfrak{b}_{+}))
. Denote
{\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}=({\overline{J}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{C}(q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}))\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
.
Then
\varphi({\overline{J}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}})\subset I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]
and
\varphi({\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}})\subset I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]
.

Let
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}
is the image of
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]\subset\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]
under the canonical projection
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]/I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}
. Denote by
1\in Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}
the image of
1\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]
in
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}
.

If
u
is a highest weight vector in a finite rank indecomposable representation
V_{\lambda}
of
U_{h}(\mathfrak{g})
of highest weight
\lambda
such that
(u,u)=1
then for any
f\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
we have in
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}

\varphi(f\otimes(u,\cdot T_{s}^{-1}u))=\varphi({\rm Ad}_{0}(q^{-(\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+id)\lambda^{\vee}})(f))q^{(s^{-1}+id)(id-\kappa P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}1=

(4.1.4)

=q^{(s^{-1}+id)(id-\kappa P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}\varphi({\rm Ad}_{0}(q^{(-\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}s^{-1}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+s^{-1})\lambda^{\vee}})(f))1,

where the classes in the quotient
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]/I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}
of the elements in the right hand side of (
4.1.15
) belong to
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}
.
In particular,

\phi((u,\cdot T_{s}^{-1}u))=q^{(s^{-1}+id)(id-\kappa P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}1,

and
q^{(s^{-1}+id)(id-\kappa P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}1
should be understood as the class of the element
q^{(s^{-1}+id)(id-\kappa P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]
in the quotient
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]/I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}
. This class belongs to
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}
.

Proof.

The proof of this proposition is based on Lemma
4.1.7
which will be proved in the end of this section.

First we obtain a useful expression for
(id\otimes\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})(\mathcal{R}^{s}_{21}\mathcal{R}^{s})
.
In order to do that we recall some properties of universal R-matrices,

(S_{s}\otimes id)\mathcal{R}^{s}=(id\otimes S_{s}^{-1})\mathcal{R}^{s}={\mathcal{R}^{s}}^{-1},(S_{s}\otimes S_{s})\mathcal{R}^{s}=\mathcal{R}^{s},

Using the first identity above we can write

\mathcal{R}^{s}_{21}\mathcal{R}^{s}=\mathcal{R}^{s}_{21}(id\otimes S_{s})({\mathcal{R}^{s}}^{-1})=(id\otimes S_{s}^{-1})({\mathcal{R}^{s}_{21}}^{-1})(id\otimes S_{s})({\mathcal{R}^{s}}^{-1})=(id\otimes S_{s})((id\otimes S_{s}^{-2})({\mathcal{R}^{s}_{21}}^{-1})\circ{\mathcal{R}^{s}}^{-1}),

where

(a\otimes b)\circ(c\otimes d)=ac\otimes db.

Now since
\omega_{0}
an algebra antiautomorphism we have

(id\otimes\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})(\mathcal{R}^{s}_{21}\mathcal{R}^{s})=(id\otimes\omega_{0})((id\otimes S_{s}^{-2})({\mathcal{R}^{s}_{21}}^{-1})\circ{\mathcal{R}^{s}}^{-1})=(id\otimes\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-2})({\mathcal{R}^{s}_{21}}^{-1})(id\otimes\omega_{0})({\mathcal{R}^{s}}^{-1}).

Recalling the definition of
{\mathcal{R}}^{s}
we obtain

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{R}}^{s}}^{-1}=exp\left[-h(\sum_{i=1}^{l}Y_{i}\otimes H_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{l}\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}H_{i}\otimes Y_{i})\right]\times

(4.1.5)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\beta\in\Delta_{+}}exp_{q_{\beta}^{-1}}[(1-q_{\beta}^{2})f_{\beta}\otimes e_{\beta}e^{-h\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}\beta^{\vee}}]=

\displaystyle=\prod_{\beta\in\Delta_{+}}exp_{q_{\beta}^{-1}}[(1-q_{\beta}^{2})e^{h(\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}-id)\beta^{\vee}}f_{\beta}\otimes e_{\beta}q^{\beta^{\vee}}]\times

\displaystyle\times exp\left[-h(\sum_{i=1}^{l}Y_{i}\otimes H_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{l}\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}H_{i}\otimes Y_{i})\right]

and using the fact that

S_{s}^{-2}={\rm Ad}~q^{2\rho^{\vee}},

where
\rho
is a half of the sum of the positive roots, we also deduce

\displaystyle(id\otimes S_{s}^{-2})({\mathcal{R}^{s}_{21}}^{-1})=\prod_{\beta\in\Delta_{+}}exp_{q_{\beta}^{-1}}[(1-q_{\beta}^{2})q^{-2\beta(\rho^{\vee})}e_{\beta}q^{\beta^{\vee}}\otimes e^{h\left(\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}-id\right)\beta^{\vee}}f_{\beta}]\times

(4.1.6)

\displaystyle exp\left[-h(\sum_{i=1}^{l}(Y_{i}\otimes H_{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{l}\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}H_{i}\otimes Y_{i})\right].

The order of the terms in the products in the formulas above is such that
the
\alpha
–term appears to the left of the
\beta
–term if
\alpha>\beta
with respect to the normal
ordering of
\Delta_{+}
.

Combining (
4.1.5
) and (
4.1.6
) we arrive at the following expression for
(id\otimes\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})(\mathcal{R}^{s}_{21}\mathcal{R}^{s})

\displaystyle(id\otimes\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})(\mathcal{R}^{s}_{21}\mathcal{R}^{s})=\prod^{\leftarrow}_{\rightarrow}exp_{q_{\beta}^{-1}}[(1-q_{\beta}^{2})q^{-2\beta(\rho^{\vee})}e_{\beta}q^{\beta^{\vee}}\otimes\omega_{0}(e^{h\left(\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}-id\right)\beta^{\vee}}f_{\beta})]\times

\displaystyle\times exp\left[h(\sum_{i=1}^{l}(Y_{i}\otimes H_{i}^{f})+\sum_{i=1}^{l}\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}H_{i}\otimes Y_{i}^{f})\right]\times

(4.1.7)

\displaystyle\times exp\left[h(\sum_{i=1}^{l}(Y_{i}\otimes H_{i}^{r})-\sum_{i=1}^{l}\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}H_{i}\otimes Y_{i}^{r})\right]\times

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\rightarrow}^{\leftarrow}exp_{q_{\beta}^{-1}}[(1-q_{\beta}^{2})f_{\beta}\otimes\omega_{0}(e_{\beta}e^{-h\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}\beta^{\vee}})],

where in the product

\prod^{\leftarrow}_{\rightarrow}

the upper (the lower) arrow indicates the order of the terms in the first (the second) factor of the tensor product relative to the normal
ordering of
\Delta_{+}
, and superscripts
f(r)
indicate that the corresponding term appears in the front (in the rear) of all the other terms in the product.

Assume now that
u\in V
has highest weight
\lambda
and
v\in V^{res}
is any vector of weight
\mu
such that
(u,\cdot v)\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
. Observe that the elements of the subalgebra
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]
appear in the first factor of the tensor product in formula (
4.1.7
) on the right. Then, since
v
is a highest weight vector, we have in
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}
using (
4.1.7
), the definitions of
I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}
and of
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}={\rm Im}({\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]/I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k})
, and the definition of the homomorphism
\varphi

\displaystyle\varphi((u,\cdot v))=q^{\lambda^{\vee}+\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}\lambda^{\vee}+\mu^{\vee}-\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}\mu^{\vee}}\times

\displaystyle\times(id\otimes f)(\prod_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}\rightarrow\\
i=1\end{array}}^{l^{\prime}}exp_{q_{\gamma_{i}}^{-1}}[-q_{\gamma_{i}}^{-2}k_{i}\otimes\omega_{0}(e_{\gamma_{i}}e^{-h\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}\gamma_{i}^{\vee}})]\times

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{l}\hskip 8.36171pt\rightarrow\\
\beta>\gamma_{l^{\prime}}\end{array}}^{\leftarrow}exp_{q_{\beta}^{-1}}[(1-q_{\beta}^{2})f_{\beta}\otimes\omega_{0}(e_{\beta}e^{-h\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}\beta^{\vee}})]),

where
f=(u,\cdot v)
.

Now formula (
4.1
), the definitions of the subalgebra
U_{q}^{res}(w^{\prime}(\mathfrak{b}_{+}))
and of the elements
e_{\beta}=X_{\beta}^{+}q^{K\beta^{\vee}}
, and Lemma
4.1.7
imply that in
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}

\phi((u,\cdot v))=\sum_{i}x_{i}(u,T_{s}y_{i}v)1,

(4.1.14)

where
x_{i}\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]
,
y_{i}\in U_{q}^{res}(w^{\prime}(\mathfrak{b}_{+}))
. Since every element of
V^{res}
is the sum of its weight components a formula of type (
4.1.14
) holds for arbitrary
(u,\cdot v)\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
, where
u
is a highest weight vector.

If
v
is chosen in such a way that
(u,T_{s}xv)=0
for any
x\in U_{q}^{res}(w^{\prime}(\mathfrak{b}_{+}))
we deduce from (
4.1.14
) that
(u,T_{s}y_{i}v)=0
. Thus
\phi((u,\cdot v))=0
in
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}
.

Now using the properties

(\Delta_{s}\otimes id)\mathcal{R}^{s}=\mathcal{R}^{s}_{13}\mathcal{R}^{s}_{23},(id\otimes\Delta_{s})\mathcal{R}^{s}=\mathcal{R}^{s}_{13}\mathcal{R}^{s}_{12},

and the fact that
\omega_{0}
is a coautomorphism and
S_{s}
is an anti-coautomorphism we get

(id\otimes\Delta_{s})(id\otimes\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})(\mathcal{R}^{s}_{21}\mathcal{R}^{s})=(id\otimes\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1}\otimes\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})(id\otimes\Delta_{s}^{opp})(\mathcal{R}^{s}_{21}\mathcal{R}^{s})=

=(id\otimes\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-2}\otimes\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-2})({\mathcal{R}^{s}_{31}}^{-1}{\mathcal{R}^{s}_{21}}^{-1})(id\otimes\omega_{0}\otimes\omega_{0})({\mathcal{R}^{s}_{12}}^{-1}{\mathcal{R}^{s}_{13}}^{-1}).

From this identity we obtain, similarly to (
4.1.14
), that for any
f\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
in
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}

\varphi(f\otimes(u,\cdot v))=\sum_{i}x_{i}^{\prime}\varphi(f)x_{i}^{\prime\prime}(u,T_{s}y_{i}^{\prime}v)1,

where
x_{i}^{\prime},x_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]
,
y_{i}^{\prime}\in U_{q}^{res}(w^{\prime}(\mathfrak{b}_{+}))
. Hence
\varphi(f\otimes(u,\cdot v))=0
in
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}
by the choice of
v
, i.e.
\varphi({\overline{J}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}})\subset I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]
.

In order to show that
\varphi({\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}})\subset I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]
we naturally extend
\varphi
to and Ad–module isomorphism
\varphi:\mathbb{C}_{q}[G]\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}_{q}[{G}_{*}]
, where
\mathbb{C}_{q}[G]=\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{C}(q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}})
,
\mathbb{C}_{q}[G_{*}]=\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{C}(q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}})
. By the definition of
{\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}
we have

\varphi({\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}})\subset(I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{C}(q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}))\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]
as obviously
\varphi({\overline{J}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{C}(q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}))\subset I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{C}(q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}})
by the first part of the proof and
\varphi(\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G])\subset\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]
.

We also have
(I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{C}(q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}))\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]\subset(I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{C}(q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}))\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]
as
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]\subset\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]
.

Recall that by the definition the elements
\tilde{e}_{\beta_{1}}^{n_{1}}\ldots\tilde{e}_{\beta_{D}}^{n_{D}}V_{i}\tilde{f}_{\beta_{D}}^{k_{D}}\ldots\tilde{f}_{\beta_{c+1}}^{k_{c+1}}(\tilde{f}_{\beta_{c}}-c_{\beta_{c}})^{k_{c}}\ldots(\tilde{f}_{\beta_{1}}-c_{\beta_{1}})^{k_{1}}
with
n_{j},k_{j},i\in\mathbb{N}
,
j=1,\ldots,D
, and where at least one
k_{j}>0
for
j<c+1
form a
\mathcal{B}
–basis in
I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}
, and this basis can be completed to a
\mathcal{B}
–basis of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]
which consists of the elements

\tilde{e}_{\beta_{1}}^{n_{1}}\ldots\tilde{e}_{\beta_{D}}^{n_{D}}V_{i}\tilde{f}_{\beta_{D}}^{k_{D}}\ldots\tilde{f}_{\beta_{c+1}}^{k_{c+1}}(\tilde{f}_{\beta_{c}}-c_{\beta_{c}})^{k_{c}}\ldots(\tilde{f}_{\beta_{1}}-c_{\beta_{1}})^{k_{1}}
with
n_{j},k_{j},i\in\mathbb{N}
,
j=1,\ldots,D
.

This implies
(I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{C}(q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}))\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]=I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}
, and hence
\varphi({\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}})\subset(I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{C}(q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}))\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]=((I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{C}(q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}))\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}])\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]=I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}\cap\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]
.

Arguments similar to those in the first part of the proof show that if
u
is a highest weight vector in a finite rank indecomposable representation
V_{\lambda}
of
U_{h}(\mathfrak{g})
of highest weight
\lambda
then in
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}

\varphi(f\otimes(u,\cdot T_{s}^{-1}u))=q^{(\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+id)\lambda^{\vee}}\varphi(f)q^{s^{-1}(-\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+id)\lambda^{\vee}}1=

=q^{(s^{-1}+id)(id-\kappa P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}{\rm Ad}(q^{(-\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}s^{-1}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+s^{-1})\lambda^{\vee}})(\varphi(f))1=

=q^{(s^{-1}+id)(id-\kappa P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}\varphi({\rm Ad}_{0}(q^{(-\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}s^{-1}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+s^{-1})\lambda^{\vee}})(f))1=

=q^{(s^{-1}+id)(id-\kappa P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}\varphi({\rm Ad}_{0}(q^{(-\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}s^{-1}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+s^{-1})\lambda^{\vee}})(f)).

The formula above can also be rewritten as

\varphi(f\otimes(u,\cdot T_{s}^{-1}u))={\rm Ad}(q^{-(\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+id)\lambda^{\vee}})(\varphi(f))q^{(s^{-1}+id)(id-\kappa P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}1=

=\varphi({\rm Ad}_{0}(q^{-(\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+id)\lambda^{\vee}})(f))q^{(s^{-1}+id)(id-\kappa P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}1.

In particular, in
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}

\varphi((u,\cdot T_{s}^{-1}u))=q^{(s^{-1}+id)(id-\kappa P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}1.

Note that the classes in the quotient
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]/I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}
of the elements

q^{(s^{-1}+id)(id-\kappa P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}\varphi({\rm Ad}_{0}(q^{(-\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}s^{-1}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+s^{-1})\lambda^{\vee}})(f)),\varphi({\rm Ad}_{0}(q^{-(\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+id)\lambda^{\vee}})(f))q^{(s^{-1}+id)(id-\kappa P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}

and
q^{(s^{-1}+id)(id-\kappa P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}
in the right hand sides of the formulas above a priori belong to
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}
.
This completes the proof.

∎

Now consider the case when
\kappa=1
and
k_{i}\in\mathcal{B}
,
i=1,\ldots,l^{\prime}
are defined in (
3.2.11
). Recall that the elements
\tilde{e}_{\beta_{1}}^{n_{1}}\ldots\tilde{e}_{\beta_{D}}^{n_{D}}V_{i}\tilde{f}_{\beta_{D}}^{k_{D}}\ldots\tilde{f}_{\beta_{c+1}}^{k_{c+1}}(\tilde{f}_{\beta_{c}}-c_{\beta_{c}})^{k_{c}}\ldots(\tilde{f}_{\beta_{1}}-c_{\beta_{1}})^{k_{1}}
with
n_{j},k_{j},i\in\mathbb{N}
,
j=1,\ldots,D
form a
\mathcal{B}
–basis in
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]
and observe that in the considered case the elements
(\tilde{f}_{\beta_{c}}-c_{\beta_{c}})^{k_{c}}\ldots(\tilde{f}_{\beta_{1}}-c_{\beta_{1}})^{k_{1}}
with
n_{j},k_{j},i\in\mathbb{N}
,
j=1,\ldots,D
, and where at least one
k_{j}>0
for
j<c+1
, form a
\mathcal{B}
–basis of
{\rm Ker}\chi_{q}^{s}
. Therefore in the considered case
I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}=I_{\mathcal{B}}
and
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}=Q_{\mathcal{B}}
, and we can apply the previous proposition to get the following statement.

Proposition 4.1.2
.

Assume that
\kappa=1
and
k_{i}\in\mathcal{B}
are defined in (
3.2.11
). Then
{\overline{J}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}
and
{\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}
lie in the kernel of
\phi
.

Moreover, if
u
is a highest weight vector in a finite rank indecomposable representation
V_{\lambda}
of
U_{h}(\mathfrak{g})
of highest weight
\lambda
such that
(u,u)=1
then for any
f\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]

\phi(f\otimes(u,\cdot T_{s}^{-1}u))=\varphi({\rm Ad}_{0}(q^{-({1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+id)\lambda^{\vee}})(f))q^{2P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\perp}}\lambda^{\vee}}1=

(4.1.15)

=q^{2P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\perp}}\lambda^{\vee}}\phi({\rm Ad}_{0}(q^{(-{1+s\over 1-s}s^{-1}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+s^{-1})\lambda^{\vee}})(f)),

where the classes in the quotient
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]/I_{\mathcal{B}}
of the elements in the right hand side of (
4.1.15
) belong to
Q_{\mathcal{B}}
.
In particular,

\phi((u,\cdot T_{s}^{-1}u))=q^{2P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\perp}}\lambda^{\vee}}1,

and
q^{2P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\perp}}\lambda^{\vee}}1
should be understood as the class of the element
q^{2P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\perp}}\lambda^{\vee}}\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]
in the quotient
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]/I_{\mathcal{B}}
. This class belongs to
Q_{\mathcal{B}}
.

The rest of this section will be devoted to the proof of Lemma
4.1.7
. This proof is in turn split into several other lemmas.

Lemma 4.1.3
.

Let
V
be a finite rank representation of
U_{h}(\mathfrak{g})
,
u,v\in V
. Let
\overline{w}=s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{D}}
be a reduced decomposition of the longest element of the Weyl group
W
. Then for any
\beta=s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{k-1}}\alpha_{i_{k}}\in\Delta_{+}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}

(u,(X_{\beta}^{+})^{(k)}v)=\sum_{p,p^{\prime}}(u,K_{p,p^{\prime}}(X_{\beta}^{-})^{(p)}(X_{\beta}^{+})^{(p^{\prime})}T_{\beta}v),

(4.1.16)

where the sum in the right hand side is finite,
X_{\beta}^{\pm}=T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{k-1}}X_{i_{k}}^{\pm}
,
K_{p,p^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{C}[q,q^{-1}]^{*}
are integer powers of
q
, and

T_{\beta}=T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{k-1}}T_{i_{k}}^{-1}T_{i_{k-1}}^{-1}\ldots T_{i_{1}}^{-1}.

Proof.

First we assume that
v
is a weight vector of weight
\lambda
. Then the only nontrivial contribution to the left hand side of (
4.1.16
) comes from the component of
u
of weight
\lambda+k\beta
, so we can assume that
u
has weight
\lambda+k\beta
.

Conjugating (
2.2.5
) by
T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{k-1}}
we get

exp_{q_{i}}^{\prime}(-X_{\beta}^{+})=exp_{q_{i}^{-1}}^{\prime}(-q_{i}X_{\beta}^{-}K_{\beta}^{-1})q_{i}^{\frac{H_{\beta}(H_{\beta}+1)}{2}}exp_{q_{i}^{-1}}^{\prime}(q_{i}^{-1}X_{\beta}^{+})T_{\beta},

(4.1.17)

where
K_{\beta}=q^{\beta^{\vee}}
,
H_{\beta}=T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{k-1}}H_{i_{k}}
.

Evaluating this identity on the matrix element
(u,\cdot v)
and using the commutation relations of the quantum group we obtain (
4.1.16
) for
v
of weight
\lambda
, where the sum in the right hand side is such that all terms
K_{p,p^{\prime}}(X_{\beta}^{-})^{(p)}(X_{\beta}^{+})^{(p^{\prime})}T_{\beta}v
have weight
\lambda+k\beta
, i.e.
p^{\prime}-p-\beta^{\vee}(\lambda)=k
. Adding identities of type (
4.1.16
) for weight vectors
v
we obtain identities of the same type for arbitrary
v
.

∎

Next we obtain some useful relations in the Weyl group which lead to important formulas for the action of braid group elements on quantum root vectors.
Recall that according to the arguments in the proof of Proposition 5.1 in
[
104
]

s^{1}
is the longest element in the Weyl group
W_{1}
of the semisimple part
\mathfrak{m}^{1}
of the standard Levi subalgebra the positive roots of which in
\Delta_{+}
form the set

\displaystyle\quad\Delta_{+}^{1}=\{\gamma_{1},\beta_{t+\frac{p-n}{2}+2}^{1},\ldots,\beta_{t+\frac{p-n}{2}+n_{1}}^{1},\gamma_{2},\beta_{t+\frac{p-n}{2}+n_{1}+2}^{1}\ldots,\beta_{t+\frac{p-n}{2}+n_{2}}^{1},\gamma_{3},\ldots,\gamma_{n},

(4.1.18)

\displaystyle-\beta_{t+1}^{1},\ldots,-\beta_{t+\frac{p-n}{2}}^{1}\}

and
s^{1}
acts on them by multiplication by
-1
. The roots in (
4.1.18
) are ordered as in the normal ordering of
\Delta_{+}
associated to
s
.

Let

s^{1}=s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{p}}=s_{i_{k_{1}}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{2}}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{n}}}s_{i_{k_{n}+1}}\ldots s_{i_{p}},i_{k_{1}}=i_{1}

be the corresponding reduced decomposition of
s^{1}
, where
\gamma_{m}=s_{i_{k_{1}}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{2}}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{m}-1}}\alpha_{i_{k_{m}}}
,
m=1,\ldots,n
. Since
s^{1}
is an involution we also have the following reduced decomposition

s^{1}=s_{i_{p}}\ldots s_{i_{1}}=s_{i_{p}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{n}+1}}s_{i_{k_{n}}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{2}}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{1}}}.

(4.1.19)

Let
\gamma_{1}\leq\beta_{q}\leq\gamma_{n}
,
\beta_{q}=s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{q-1}}\alpha_{i_{q}}
.
Since
s^{1}=-1
in
W_{1}

s^{1}=s_{i_{q}}\ldots s_{i_{1}}s^{1}s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{q}}=s_{i_{q}}\ldots s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{p}}s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{q}}=s_{i_{q+1}}\ldots s_{i_{p}}s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{q}},

and in the right hand side we obtain a reduced decomposition of
s^{1}
.
Now

s^{1}\alpha_{i_{q}}=-\alpha_{i_{q}}=s_{i_{q+1}}\ldots s_{i_{p}}s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{q}}\alpha_{i_{q}}=-s_{i_{q+1}}\ldots s_{i_{p}}s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{q-1}}\alpha_{i_{q}},

and hence

s_{i_{q+1}}\ldots s_{i_{p}}s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{q-1}}\alpha_{i_{q}}=\alpha_{i_{q}}.

(4.1.20)

Form the expressions
\gamma_{m}=s_{i_{k_{1}}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{2}}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{m}-1}}\alpha_{i_{k_{m}}}
,
m=1,\ldots,n
we deduce

s_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots s_{\gamma_{q-1}}=s_{i_{2}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{2}-1}}s_{i_{k_{2}+1}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{q-1}-1}}s_{i_{k_{q-1}}}s_{i_{k_{q-1}-1}}\ldots s_{i_{1}}.

In particular, we have reduced decompositions

s^{1}=s_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots s_{\gamma_{n}}=s_{i_{2}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{2}-1}}s_{i_{k_{2}+1}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{n}-1}}s_{i_{k_{n}}}s_{i_{k_{n}-1}}\ldots s_{i_{1}}

(4.1.21)

and

s^{1}=s_{i_{k_{n}}}\ldots s_{i_{1}}s^{1}s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{n}}}=s_{i_{k_{n}}}s_{i_{k_{n}-1}}\ldots s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{2}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{2}-1}}s_{i_{k_{2}+1}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{n}-1}}.

(4.1.22)

Comparing the first expression above with (
4.1.19
) we obtain the following identity for reduced decompositions

s_{i_{2}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{2}-1}}s_{i_{k_{2}+1}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{n}-1}}=s_{i_{p}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{n}+1}}.

(4.1.23)

As the roots
\gamma_{m}
,
m=1,\ldots,n
are mutually orthogonal we deduce

\displaystyle s_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots s_{\gamma_{q-1}}\gamma_{q}=s_{i_{2}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{2}-1}}s_{i_{k_{2}+1}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{q-1}-1}}s_{i_{k_{q-1}}}s_{i_{k_{q-1}-1}}\ldots s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{q}-1}}\alpha_{i_{k_{q}}}=

\displaystyle=s_{i_{2}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{2}-1}}s_{i_{k_{2}+1}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{q-1}-1}}s_{i_{k_{q-1}+1}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{q}-1}}\alpha_{i_{k_{q}}}=\gamma_{q}=s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{q}-1}}\alpha_{i_{k_{q}}}.

Therefore

s_{i_{k_{q}-1}}\ldots s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{2}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{2}-1}}s_{i_{k_{2}+1}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{q-1}-1}}s_{i_{k_{q-1}+1}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{q}-1}}\alpha_{i_{k_{q}}}=\alpha_{i_{k_{q}}},

and

s_{i_{k_{q}-1}}\ldots s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{2}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{2}-1}}s_{i_{k_{2}+1}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{q-1}-1}}s_{i_{k_{q-1}+1}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{q}-1}}

is a reduced decomposition since it is a part of reduced decomposition (
4.1.22
).

The last two properties and (
2.2.10
) imply

T_{i_{k_{q}-1}}\ldots T_{i_{1}}T_{i_{2}}\ldots T_{i_{k_{2}-1}}T_{i_{k_{2}+1}}\ldots T_{i_{k_{q-1}-1}}T_{i_{k_{q-1}+1}}\ldots T_{i_{k_{q}-1}}X_{i_{k_{q}}}^{\pm}=X_{i_{k_{q}}}^{\pm},

and hence

\displaystyle T_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots T_{\gamma_{q-1}}X_{\gamma_{q}}^{\pm}=T_{i_{2}}\ldots T_{i_{k_{2}-1}}T_{i_{k_{2}+1}}\ldots T_{i_{k_{q-1}-1}}T_{i_{k_{q-1}}}^{-1}T_{i_{k_{q-1}-1}}^{-1}\ldots T_{i_{1}}^{-1}T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{k_{q}-1}}X_{i_{k_{q}}}^{\pm}=

\displaystyle=T_{i_{2}}\ldots T_{i_{k_{2}-1}}T_{i_{k_{2}+1}}\ldots T_{i_{k_{q-1}-1}}T_{i_{k_{q-1}+1}}\ldots T_{i_{k_{q}-1}}X_{i_{k_{q}}}^{\pm}=T_{i_{1}}^{-1}\ldots T_{i_{k_{q}-1}}^{-1}X_{i_{k_{q}}}^{\pm}=\overline{X}_{\gamma_{q}}^{\pm}.

(4.1.24)

Lemma 4.1.4
.

Let
V
be a finite rank representation of
U_{h}(\mathfrak{g})
,
u,v\in V
.
Then for any
m_{1},\ldots,m_{n}\in\mathbb{N}

(u,(X_{\gamma_{1}}^{+})^{(m_{1})}\ldots(X_{\gamma_{n}}^{+})^{(m_{n})}v)=(u,KT_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots T_{\gamma_{n}}v),

(4.1.25)

where
K
is of the form

K=\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{l}p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\\
p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{m}^{\prime}\end{array}}K^{p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}}_{p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{m}^{\prime}}(\overline{X}_{\delta_{1}}^{-})^{(p_{1})}\ldots(\overline{X}_{\delta_{k}}^{-})^{(p_{k})}(\overline{X}_{\beta_{1}}^{+})^{(p_{1}^{\prime})}\ldots(\overline{X}_{\beta_{m}}^{+})^{(p_{m}^{\prime})},

(4.1.26)

the finite sum in the right hand side is over all
\delta_{i},\beta_{j}\in\Delta_{+}^{1}
such that
\delta_{1}<\delta_{2}<\ldots<\delta_{k}
,
\gamma_{1}\leq\beta_{1}<\beta_{2}<\ldots<\beta_{m}\leq\gamma_{n}
, and

\overline{X}_{\delta_{i}}^{-}
,
\overline{X}_{\beta_{j}}^{+}
are the quantum root vectors of
U_{h}(\mathfrak{m}^{1})\subset U_{h}(\mathfrak{g})
defined with the help of normal ordering (
4.1.18
) of
\Delta_{+}^{1}
,
K^{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}_{p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{m}^{\prime}}\in U_{q}^{res}(H)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.1.3

(u,(X_{\gamma_{1}}^{+})^{(m_{1})}\ldots(X_{\gamma_{n}}^{+})^{(m_{n})}v)=

=\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{l}c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\\
c_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,c_{n}^{\prime}\end{array}}K^{c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}}_{c_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,c_{n}^{\prime}}(u,(X_{\gamma_{1}}^{-})^{(c_{1})}(X_{\gamma_{1}}^{+})^{(c_{1}^{\prime})}T_{\gamma_{1}}(X_{\gamma_{2}}^{-})^{(c_{2})}(X_{\gamma_{2}}^{+})^{(c_{2}^{\prime})}T_{\gamma_{2}}\ldots(X_{\gamma_{n}}^{-})^{(c_{n})}(X_{\gamma_{n}}^{+})^{(c_{n}^{\prime})}T_{\gamma_{n}}v),

where the sum in the right hand side is finite and
K^{c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}}_{c_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,c_{n}^{\prime}}
are integer powers of
q
.

Using (
4.1.24
) and observing that
X_{\gamma_{1}}^{\pm}=\overline{X}_{\gamma_{1}}^{\pm}
as
\gamma_{1}
the first simple root in the normal ordering of
\Delta_{+}
associated to
s
we obtain

(u,(X_{\gamma_{1}}^{+})^{(k_{1})}\ldots(X_{\gamma_{n}}^{+})^{(k_{n})}v)=

=\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{l}c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\\
c_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,c_{n}^{\prime}\end{array}}K^{c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}}_{c_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,c_{n}^{\prime}}(u,(\overline{X}_{\gamma_{1}}^{-})^{(c_{1})}(\overline{X}_{\gamma_{1}}^{+})^{(c_{1}^{\prime})}(\overline{X}_{\gamma_{2}}^{-})^{(c_{2})}(\overline{X}_{\gamma_{2}}^{+})^{(c_{2}^{\prime})}\ldots(\overline{X}_{\gamma_{n}}^{-})^{(c_{n})}(\overline{X}_{\gamma_{n}}^{+})^{(c_{n}^{\prime})}T_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots T_{\gamma_{n}}v).

Now (
4.1.25
) follows from the previous identity by bringing the monomials

(\overline{X}_{\gamma_{1}}^{-})^{(c_{1})}(\overline{X}_{\gamma_{1}}^{+})^{(c_{1}^{\prime})}(\overline{X}_{\gamma_{2}}^{-})^{(c_{2})}(\overline{X}_{\gamma_{2}}^{+})^{(c_{2}^{\prime})}\ldots(\overline{X}_{\gamma_{n}}^{-})^{(c_{n})}(\overline{X}_{\gamma_{n}}^{+})^{(c_{n}^{\prime})}

in the right hand side to the form as in the right hand side of (
4.1.26
) with the help of Lemma
2.4.2
.

∎

Lemma 4.1.5
.

Let
V
be a finite rank representation of
U_{h}(\mathfrak{g})
,
u,v\in V
. Suppose that
u
is a highest weight vector.
Then for any
m_{1},\ldots,m_{n}\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle(u,(X_{\gamma_{1}}^{+})^{(m_{1})}\ldots(X_{\gamma_{n}}^{+})^{(m_{n})}v)=\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle=(u,\sum_{q_{1},\ldots,q_{k_{n}}}C_{q_{1},\ldots,q_{k_{n}}}T_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots T_{\gamma_{n}}(X_{\beta_{1}}^{-})^{(q_{1})}\ldots(X_{\beta_{k_{n}}}^{-})^{(q_{k_{n}})}v),

where
C_{q_{1},\ldots,q_{k_{n}}}\in\mathbb{C}[q,q^{-1}]
.

Proof.

Denote
T^{1}=T_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots T_{\gamma_{n}}
.
From the definition of
T_{\gamma_{1}},\ldots,T_{\gamma_{n}}
and (
4.1.23
) we obtain

T^{1}=T_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots T_{\gamma_{n}}=T_{i_{2}}\ldots T_{i_{k_{2}-1}}T_{i_{k_{2}+1}}\ldots T_{i_{k_{n}-1}}T_{i_{k_{n}}}^{-1}T_{i_{k_{n}-1}}^{-1}\ldots T_{i_{1}}^{-1}=

(4.1.27)

=T_{i_{p}}\ldots T_{i_{k_{n}+1}}T_{i_{k_{n}}}^{-1}T_{i_{k_{n}-1}}^{-1}\ldots T_{i_{1}}^{-1}.

Therefore for
\gamma_{1}\leq\beta_{q}\leq\gamma_{n}
,
\beta_{q}=s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{q-1}}\alpha_{i_{q}}
we have

(T^{1})^{-1}\overline{X}_{\beta_{q}}^{+}=T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{k_{n}}}T_{i_{k_{n}+1}}^{-1}\ldots T_{i_{p}}^{-1}T_{i_{1}}^{-1}\ldots T_{i_{q-1}}^{-1}X_{i_{q}}^{+}.

(4.1.28)

By (
4.1.20
), (
2.2.10
)

T_{i_{q+1}}^{-1}\ldots T_{i_{p}}^{-1}T_{i_{1}}^{-1}\ldots T_{i_{q-1}}^{-1}X_{i_{q}}^{+}=X_{i_{q}}^{+},

and hence

T_{i_{k_{n}+1}}^{-1}\ldots T_{i_{p}}^{-1}T_{i_{1}}^{-1}\ldots T_{i_{q-1}}^{-1}X_{i_{q}}^{+}=T_{i_{k_{n}}}\ldots T_{i_{q+1}}X_{i_{q}}^{+}.

Combining this with (
4.1.28
) we infer

(T^{1})^{-1}\overline{X}_{\beta_{q}}^{+}=T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{k_{n}}}T_{i_{k_{n}}}\ldots T_{i_{q+1}}X_{i_{q}}^{+}

(4.1.29)

Since
u
is a highest weight vector, Lemma
4.1.4
and the definition of the bilinear form on
V
imply

(u,(X_{\gamma_{1}}^{+})^{(m_{1})}\ldots(X_{\gamma_{n}}^{+})^{(m_{n})}v)=(u,\sum_{p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{m}^{\prime}}K^{0,\ldots,0}_{p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{m}^{\prime}}(\overline{X}_{\beta_{1}}^{+})^{(p_{1}^{\prime})}\ldots(\overline{X}_{\beta_{m}}^{+})^{(p_{m}^{\prime})}T_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots T_{\gamma_{n}}v).

Combining this with (
4.1.29
) we get

(u,(X_{\gamma_{1}}^{+})^{(m_{1})}\ldots(X_{\gamma_{n}}^{+})^{(m_{n})}v)=

(4.1.30)

=(u,\sum_{p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{m}^{\prime}}K^{0,\ldots,0}_{p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{k_{n}}^{\prime}}T_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots T_{\gamma_{n}}(T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{k_{n}}})(({X}_{\delta_{1}}^{+})^{(p_{1}^{\prime})}\ldots({X}_{\delta_{k_{n}}}^{+})^{(p_{m}^{\prime})})v),

where for
q=1,\ldots,k_{n}
we denote
\delta_{q}=s_{i_{k_{n}}}\ldots s_{i_{q+1}}\alpha_{i_{q}}
, and
{X}_{\delta_{q}}^{+}=T_{i_{k_{n}}}\ldots T_{i_{q+1}}X_{i_{q}}^{+}
.

Let
U_{q}^{res}(\mathfrak{m}^{1})
be the restricted specialization of
U_{h}(\mathfrak{m}^{1})
,
U_{q}^{res}(\mathfrak{m}^{1}_{+})
the subalgebra of
U_{q}^{res}(\mathfrak{m}^{1})
generated by
({X}_{\beta}^{+})^{(k)}
for simple roots
\beta\in\Delta_{+}^{1}
and
k\geq 0
. Then for
q=1,\ldots,k_{n}
and
k\geq 0
we have
({X}_{\delta_{q}}^{+})^{(k)}\in U_{q}^{res}(\mathfrak{m}^{1}_{+})
.

Since

s^{1}=s_{i_{k_{n}}}\ldots s_{i_{1}}s^{1}s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{n}}}=s_{i_{k_{n}}}\ldots s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{p}}\ldots s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{n}}}=s_{i_{k_{n}}}\ldots s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{p}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{n}+1}},

and in the right hand side we have a reduced decomposition, the elements

\displaystyle Y_{\phi_{k_{n}+1}}=T_{i_{k_{n}}}^{-1}\ldots T_{i_{1}}^{-1}T_{i_{p}}^{-1}\ldots T_{i_{k_{n}+2}}^{-1}X_{i_{k_{n}+1}}^{+},

\displaystyle Y_{\phi_{k_{n}+2}}=T_{i_{k_{n}}}^{-1}\ldots T_{i_{1}}^{-1}T_{i_{p}}^{-1}\ldots T_{i_{k_{n}+3}}^{-1}X_{i_{k_{n}+2}}^{+},\ldots,

\displaystyle Y_{\phi_{p}}=T_{i_{k_{n}}}^{-1}\ldots T_{i_{1}}^{-1}X_{i_{p}}^{+},

\displaystyle Y_{\phi_{1}}=T_{i_{k_{n}}}^{-1}\ldots T_{i_{2}}^{-1}X_{i_{1}}^{+},\ldots,

\displaystyle Y_{\phi_{k_{n}}}=X_{i_{k_{n}}}^{+}

belong to
U_{q}^{res}(\mathfrak{m}^{1}_{+})
and the products

(Y_{\phi_{k_{n}+1}})^{(q_{k_{n}+1})}(Y_{\phi_{k_{n}+2}})^{(q_{k_{n}+2})}\ldots(Y_{\phi_{p}})^{(q_{p})}(Y_{\phi_{1}})^{(q_{1})}\ldots(Y_{\phi_{k_{n}}})^{(q_{k_{n}})}

form a
\mathbb{C}[q,q^{-1}]
–basis of
U_{q}^{res}(\mathfrak{m}^{1}_{+})
. Expanding each monomial
({X}_{\delta_{1}}^{+})^{(p_{1}^{\prime})}\ldots({X}_{\delta_{m}}^{+})^{(p_{k_{n}}^{\prime})}
in (
4.1.30
) with respect to this basis we get

(u,(X_{\gamma_{1}}^{+})^{(m_{1})}\ldots(X_{\gamma_{n}}^{+})^{(m_{n})}v)=(u,\sum_{q_{1},\ldots,q_{p}}K_{q_{1},\ldots,q_{p}}T_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots T_{\gamma_{n}}(T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{k_{n}}})((Y_{\phi_{k_{n}+1}})^{(q_{k_{n}+1})}(Y_{\phi_{k_{n}+2}})^{(q_{k_{n}+2})}\ldots

\ldots(Y_{\phi_{p}})^{(q_{p})}(Y_{\phi_{1}})^{(q_{1})}\ldots(Y_{\phi_{k_{n}}})^{(q_{k_{n}})})v),

(4.1.31)

where
K_{q_{1},\ldots,q_{p}}\in\mathbb{C}[q,q^{-1}]
.

Now for
r=k_{n}+1,\ldots p

(T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{k_{n}}})(Y_{\phi_{r}})=T_{i_{p}}^{-1}\ldots T_{i_{r+1}}^{-1}X_{i_{r}}^{+},

(4.1.32)

and for
r=1,\ldots k_{n}

(T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{k_{n}}})(Y_{\phi_{r}})=T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{r}}X_{i_{r}}^{+}=-T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{r-1}}X_{i_{r}}^{-}K_{i_{r}}=-X_{\beta_{r}}^{-}K_{\beta_{r}},

(4.1.33)

where
K_{\beta_{r}}=T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{r-1}}K_{i_{r}}
.

Since
u
is a highest weight vector, by Lemma
2.7.4
we have
T_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots T_{\gamma_{1}}u=\omega(c)T_{i_{p}}\ldots T_{i_{1}}u
, where
c\in\mathbb{C}[q,q^{-1}]^{*}
. Therefore for any element
w\in V
,
x\in U_{h}(\mathfrak{g})
we can write

(u,T_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots T_{\gamma_{n}}xw)=c(u,T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{p}}xw).

Combining this with (
4.1.32
) yields for
r=k_{n}+1,\ldots p

\displaystyle(u,T_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots T_{\gamma_{n}}(T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{k_{n}}})(Y_{\phi_{r}})xw)=c(u,T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{p}}(T_{i_{p}}^{-1}\ldots T_{i_{r+1}}^{-1})(X_{i_{r}}^{+})xw)=

\displaystyle=c(u,(T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{r}})(X_{i_{r}}^{+})T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{p}}xw)=-c(u,(T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{r-1}})(X_{i_{r}}^{-}K_{i_{r}})T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{p}}xw)=

\displaystyle=-c(u,X_{\beta_{r}}^{-}K_{\beta_{r}}T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{p}}xw)=0.

From this formula, (
4.1.31
), (
4.1.33
) and the commutation relations between the elements
K_{\beta_{r}}
and the quantum root vectors we deduce

(u,(X_{\gamma_{1}}^{+})^{(m_{1})}\ldots(X_{\gamma_{n}}^{+})^{(m_{n})}v)=

=(u,\sum_{q_{1},\ldots,q_{k_{n}}}C_{q_{1},\ldots,q_{k_{n}}}T_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots T_{\gamma_{n}}(X_{\beta_{1}}^{-})^{(q_{1})}\ldots(X_{\beta_{k_{n}}}^{-})^{(q_{k_{n}})}v),

where
C_{q_{1},\ldots,q_{k_{n}}}\in\mathbb{C}[q,q^{-1}]
.

∎

Recall now that according to the results obtained in the proof of Proposition 5.1 in
[
104
]

s^{2}
is the longest element in the Weyl group
W_{2}
of the semisimple part
\mathfrak{m}^{2}
of the Levi subalgebra the positive roots of which in
\Delta_{+}
form the set

\displaystyle\Delta_{+}^{2}=\{\gamma_{n+1},\beta_{q+2}^{2},\ldots,\beta_{q+m_{1}}^{2},\gamma_{n+2},\beta_{q+m_{1}+2}^{2},\ldots,\beta_{q+m_{2}}^{2},\gamma_{n+3},\ldots,

(4.1.34)

\displaystyle\gamma_{l^{\prime}},\beta_{q+m_{l(s^{2})}+1}^{2},\ldots,\beta_{2q+2m_{l(s^{2})}-(l^{\prime}-n)}^{2}\},

and
s^{2}
acts on them by multiplication by
-1
. The roots in (
4.1.34
) are ordered as in the normal ordering of
\Delta_{+}
associated to
s
.

As above we denote
\gamma_{m}=s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{n+1}}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{m}-1}}\alpha_{i_{k_{m}}}
,
m=n+1,\ldots,l^{\prime}
.

Let
w=s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{k_{n+1}-1}}
,
T_{w}=T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{k_{n+1}-1}}
. Then
\widetilde{\Delta}_{+}^{2}=w^{-1}(\Delta_{+}^{2})
is the set of positive roots of the semisimple part
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}
of a standard Levi subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
,
\widetilde{s}^{2}=w^{-1}s^{2}w
is the longest element in the Weyl group
\widetilde{W}_{2}
of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}
.

For any root
\beta\in\Delta_{+}
denote

\widetilde{X}_{\beta}^{\pm}=T_{w}^{-1}(X_{\beta}^{\pm}),~~\widetilde{\overline{X}}_{\beta}^{\pm}=\overline{T}_{w}^{-1}(\overline{X}_{\beta}^{\pm}),~~\widetilde{T}_{\beta}=T_{w}^{-1}T_{\beta}T_{w}.

Similarly to Lemma
4.1.4
we have

Lemma 4.1.6
.

Let
V
be a finite rank representation of
U_{h}(\mathfrak{g})
,
u,v\in V
.
Then for any
m_{n+1},\ldots,m_{l^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{N}

(u,(\widetilde{X}_{\gamma_{n+1}}^{+})^{(m_{n+1})}\ldots(\widetilde{X}_{\gamma_{l^{\prime}}}^{+})^{(m_{l^{\prime}})}v)=(u,\widetilde{K}\widetilde{T}_{\gamma_{n+1}}\ldots\widetilde{T}_{\gamma_{l^{\prime}}}v),

(4.1.35)

where
\widetilde{K}
is of the form

\widetilde{K}=\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{l}p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\\
p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{m}^{\prime}\end{array}}M^{p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}}_{p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{m}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{{X}}_{\delta_{1}}^{-})^{(p_{1})}\ldots(\widetilde{{X}}_{\delta_{k}}^{-})^{(p_{k})}(\widetilde{\overline{X}}_{\beta_{1}^{\prime}}^{+})^{(p_{1}^{\prime})}\ldots(\widetilde{\overline{X}}_{\beta_{m}^{\prime}}^{+})^{(p_{m}^{\prime})},

(4.1.36)

the finite sum in the right hand side is over all
\delta_{i},\beta_{j}\in\Delta_{+}^{2}
such that
\delta_{1}<\delta_{2}<\ldots<\delta_{k}
,
\gamma_{n+1}\leq\beta_{1}^{\prime}<\beta_{2}^{\prime}<\ldots<\beta_{m}^{\prime}\leq\gamma_{l^{\prime}}
,
M^{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}_{p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{m}^{\prime}}\in U_{q}^{res}(H)
.

Proof.

Similarly to Lemma
4.1.4
we deduce that formula (
4.1.35
) holds with

\widetilde{K}=\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{l}p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\\
p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{m}^{\prime}\end{array}}L^{p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}}_{p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{m}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\overline{X}}_{\delta_{1}}^{-})^{(p_{1})}\ldots(\widetilde{\overline{X}}_{\delta_{k}}^{-})^{(p_{k})}(\widetilde{\overline{X}}_{\beta_{1}^{\prime}}^{+})^{(p_{1}^{\prime})}\ldots(\widetilde{\overline{X}}_{\beta_{m}^{\prime}}^{+})^{(p_{m}^{\prime})},

(4.1.37)

where
L^{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}_{p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{m}^{\prime}}\in U_{q}^{res}(H)
.

Let
U_{q}^{res}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}^{2})
be the restricted specialization of
U_{h}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}^{2})
,
U_{q}^{res}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}_{+})
the subalgebra of
U_{q}^{res}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}^{2})
generated by
({X}_{\beta}^{+})^{(k)}
for simple roots
\beta\in\widetilde{\Delta}_{+}^{2}
and
k\geq 0
. Then the monomials
(\widetilde{\overline{X}}_{\delta_{1}}^{-})^{(p_{1})}\ldots(\widetilde{\overline{X}}_{\delta_{k}}^{-})^{(p_{k})}
,
\delta_{i},\in\Delta_{+}^{2}
,
\delta_{1}<\delta_{2}<\ldots<\delta_{k}
form a linear basis of
U_{q}^{res}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}_{+})
and the monomials

(\widetilde{{X}}_{\delta_{1}}^{-})^{(p_{1})}\ldots(\widetilde{{X}}_{\delta_{k}}^{-})^{(p_{k})}

\delta_{i},\in\Delta_{+}^{2}
,
\delta_{1}<\delta_{2}<\ldots<\delta_{k}
form a linear basis of
U_{q}^{res}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}_{+})
as well. Thus
\widetilde{K}
can be rewritten in form (
4.1.36
) using the latter basis.

∎

Lemma 4.1.7
.

Let
V
be a finite rank representation of
U_{h}(\mathfrak{g})
,
u,v\in V
. Suppose that
u
is a highest weight vector.
Then for any
m_{1},\ldots,m_{l^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{N}

(u,(X_{\gamma_{1}}^{+})^{(m_{1})}\ldots(X_{\gamma_{n}}^{+})^{(m_{n})}({X}_{\gamma_{n+1}}^{+})^{(m_{n+1})}\ldots({X}_{\gamma_{l^{\prime}}}^{+})^{(m_{l^{\prime}})}v)=

=\sum_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{D}}F_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{D}}(u,T_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots T_{\gamma_{n}}{T}_{\gamma_{n+1}}\ldots{T}_{\gamma_{l^{\prime}}}({X}^{-}_{\beta_{k_{l^{\prime}}}})^{(n_{k_{l^{\prime}}})}\ldots({X}^{-}_{\beta_{1}})^{(n_{1})}(X^{+}_{\beta_{k_{l^{\prime}}+1}})^{(n_{k_{l^{\prime}}+1})}\ldots(X^{+}_{\beta_{D}})^{(n_{D})}v)=

=c\sum_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{D}}F_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{D}}(u,T_{s}({X}^{-}_{\beta_{k_{l^{\prime}}}})^{(n_{k_{l^{\prime}}})}\ldots({X}^{-}_{\beta_{1}})^{(n_{1})}(X^{+}_{\beta_{k_{l^{\prime}}+1}})^{(n_{k_{l^{\prime}}+1})}\ldots(X^{+}_{\beta_{D}})^{(n_{D})}v)=

=(u,\sum_{q_{1},\ldots,q_{k_{n}}}C_{q_{1},\ldots,q_{k_{n}}}T_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots T_{\gamma_{n}}(X_{\beta_{1}}^{-})^{(q_{1})}\ldots(X_{\beta_{k_{n}}}^{-})^{(q_{k_{n}})}\times

(X_{\beta}^{-})^{(k)}
,

(4.1.38)

(4.1.39)
