{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}(f\otimes\Delta_{\mu}^{-1})=q^{-n((\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}-id)\beta_{p}^{\vee},\mu^{\vee})}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}(f)\otimes\Delta_{\mu}^{-1},f\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{I^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}.

(4.5.13)

Proof.

The proof of the first part follows from formula (
4.2.4
) applied to
g=\Delta_{\mu}
. All terms in the r.h.s. of (
4.2.4
), except for the ones with
p=n-k
in the first sum, vanish since
v_{\mu}
is a highest weight vector. Lemma
4.3.4
implies vanishing all the remaining terms in the first sum in the r.h.s. of (
4.2.4
) modulo
{\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}
, except for
k=0
. The only non-vanishing term in the first sum in the r.h.s. of (
4.2.4
) corresponds to
p=n
,
k=0
. It gives the r.h.s. of (
4.5.10
).

Local finiteness of the action of
U_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,res}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
on
\mathbb{C}_{1}^{loc}[G]
follows from the local finiteness of the action of
U_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,res}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
on
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]/{\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}
and from formula (
4.5.11
).

The second part is proved similarly using Lemma
4.3.7
.

∎

Lemma 4.5.5
.

For any
r\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
p\leq r
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
the left ideal
{\overline{I}^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}}\subset\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
is
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{r}}^{(n)}
–stable, and, if in addition
\beta_{r}\not\in\Delta_{s^{1}}^{s}
, then
{I^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}}
is
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{r}}^{(n)}
–stable.

For any
r\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
p\leq r
and
n\in\mathbb{N}

{\overline{I}^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}/{\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}\subset\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}\simeq\mathbb{C}_{1}^{loc}[G]
is
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{r}}^{(n)}
–stable, and, if in addition
\beta_{r}\not\in\Delta_{s^{1}}^{s}
, then
{I^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}/{I^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}\subset\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{I^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}
is
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{r}}^{(n)}
–stable.

Thus for any
r\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
p\leq r
and
n\in\mathbb{N}

\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}
is naturally equipped with an
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{r}}^{(n)}
–action, and, if in addition
\beta_{r}\not\in\Delta_{s^{1}}^{s}

\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{I^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}
is naturally equipped with an
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{r}}^{(n)}
–action.

In particular,
\mathbb{C}_{p}^{loc}[G]
is naturally equipped with an
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{r}}^{(n)}
–action.

Proof.

By (
4.5.11
) and (
4.5.13
) to establish the first two claims it suffices to show that
{\overline{J}^{p}_{q}}
is
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{r}}^{(n)}
–stable for any
r\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
p\leq r
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
{J^{p}_{q}}
is
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{r}}^{(n)}
–stable for any
r\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
with
\beta_{r}\not\in\Delta_{s^{1}}^{s}
,
p\leq r
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

We show that
{\overline{J}^{p}_{q}}
is
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{r}}^{(n)}
–stable for any
r\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
p\leq r
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
{\overline{J}^{p}_{q}}
is generated by
{\overline{J}^{1}_{q}}
and by
A_{1},\ldots,A_{p-1}
and by Lemma
4.3.2

{\overline{J}^{1}_{q}}
is
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{r}}^{(n)}
–stable it suffices to prove that
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{r}}^{(n)}(f\otimes A_{t})\in{\overline{J}^{p}_{q}}
, for any
f\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
,
t=1,\ldots,p-1
. By (
4.2.3
) we have

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{r}}^{(n)}(f\otimes A_{t})=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\sum_{p=0}^{n-k}q_{{\beta_{r}}}^{-k(n-k)-p(n-k-p)}{\rm Ad}_{0}(G_{\beta_{r}}^{-k}f_{\beta_{r}}^{(p)})(f)\otimes A_{t}(\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1}(G_{\beta_{r}}^{-k-p}f_{\beta_{r}}^{(n-k-p)})\cdot\omega_{0}(f_{\beta_{r}}^{(k)}))+

+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\sum_{i}q_{\beta_{r}}^{-k(n-k)}{\rm Ad}_{0}(G_{\beta_{r}}^{-k}x_{i}^{(n-k)})(f)\otimes A_{t}((\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})(G_{\beta_{r}}^{-k}y_{i}^{(n-k)})\cdot\omega_{0}(f_{\beta_{r}}^{(k)}))+

(4.5.14)

+\sum_{i}{\rm Ad}_{0}({y_{i}^{(n)}}^{2})(f)\otimes A_{t}((\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})({y_{i}^{(n)}}^{1})\cdot\omega_{0}(x_{i}^{(n)}))=

=\sum_{k=0}^{n}q_{{\beta_{r}}}^{-k(n-k)}q^{n(\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}\beta_{r}-\beta_{r},\mu_{t}-\beta_{t})}{\rm Ad}_{0}(G_{\beta_{r}}^{-k}f_{\beta_{r}}^{(n-k)})(f)\otimes A_{t}(\cdot\omega_{0}(f_{\beta_{r}}^{(k)}))+

+\sum_{k=0}^{n}\sum_{p=0}^{n-k-1}q_{{\beta_{r}}}^{-k(n-k)-p(n-k-p)}{\rm Ad}_{0}(G_{\beta_{r}}^{-k}f_{\beta_{r}}^{(p)})(f)\otimes A_{t}(\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1}(G_{\beta_{r}}^{-k-p}f_{\beta_{r}}^{(n-k-p)})\cdot\omega_{0}(f_{\beta_{r}}^{(k)}))+

+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\sum_{i}q_{\beta_{r}}^{-k(n-k)}{\rm Ad}_{0}(G_{\beta_{r}}^{-k}x_{i}^{(n-k)})(f)\otimes A_{t}((\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})(G_{\beta_{r}}^{-k}y_{i}^{(n-k)})\cdot\omega_{0}(f_{\beta_{r}}^{(k)}))+

+\sum_{i}{\rm Ad}_{0}({y_{i}^{(n)}}^{2})(f)\otimes A_{t}((\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})({y_{i}^{(n)}}^{1})\cdot\omega_{0}(x_{i}^{(n)})).

We claim that all terms in the right hand side of the formula above belong to
{\overline{J}^{p}_{q}}
. Indeed,
by Lemma
4.4.3

q_{{\beta_{r}}}^{-k(n-k)}q^{n(\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}\beta_{r}-\beta_{r},\mu_{t}-\beta_{t})}{\rm Ad}_{0}(G_{\beta_{r}}^{-k}f_{\beta_{r}}^{(n-k)})(f)\otimes A_{t}(\cdot\omega_{0}(f_{\beta_{r}}^{(k)}))\in\overline{J}^{t}_{q}\subset\overline{J}^{p}_{q}

for
k>0
. If
k=0

q^{n(\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}\beta_{r}-\beta_{r},\mu_{t}-\beta_{t})}{\rm Ad}_{0}(f_{\beta_{r}}^{(n)})(f)\otimes A_{t}\in\overline{J}^{t+1}_{q}\subset\overline{J}^{p}_{q}.

To establish the same claim for all the other terms in the right hand side of (
4.5.14
) we shall apply Lemma
4.4.1
. The arguments are similar for all these terms. Consider, for instance, the terms in the last sum. For
xT_{s}hz_{+}x^{\prime}
with arbitrary
x=\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1}(f_{\beta_{D}}^{(n_{D})}\ldots f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n_{p})}),z_{+}\in U_{q}^{res}(\Delta_{0}^{+}),x^{\prime}\in U_{q}^{res}(-\Delta_{\mathfrak{m}_{+}}^{s}),h\in U_{q}^{res}(H)
,
n_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we have

A_{t}((\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})({y_{i}^{(n)}}^{1})xT_{s}hz_{+}x^{\prime}\omega_{0}x_{i}^{(n)})=(v_{t},(\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})({y_{i}^{(n)}}^{1})xT_{s}hz_{+}x^{\prime}\omega_{0}x_{i}^{(n)}T_{s^{-1}}v_{\mu_{t}})=

=c(v_{t},(\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})({\overline{y}_{i}^{(n)}}^{1}f_{\beta_{D}}^{(n_{D})}\ldots f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n_{p})})T_{s}hz_{+}x^{\prime}\omega_{0}x_{i}^{(n)}T_{s^{-1}}v_{\mu_{t}})=

=c(v_{t},(\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})({\overline{y}_{i}^{(n)}}^{1}f_{\beta_{D}}^{(n_{D})}\ldots f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n_{p})})T_{s}hz_{+}x^{\prime}\omega_{0}x_{i}^{(n)}T_{s^{-1}}v_{\mu_{t}})=(v_{t},(\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})({\overline{y}_{i}^{(n)}}^{1}f_{\beta_{D}}^{(n_{D})}\ldots f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n_{p})}y)v_{\mu_{t}}),

where
c\in\mathcal{B}
,
y\in U_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,res}(\mathfrak{n}_{-})
and for the last implication we used the fact that
v_{t}
is a weight vector and
v_{\mu_{t}}
is a highest weight vector. Since
{\overline{y}_{i}^{(n)}}^{1}\in I_{r}^{>}
,
r\geq p
and the weight of
{\overline{y}_{i}^{(n)}}^{1}
is not zero, Lemma
2.7.2
applied to
{\overline{y}_{i}^{(n)}}^{1}f_{\beta_{D}}^{(n_{D})}\ldots f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n_{p})}y
implies that this element belongs to the right ideal in
U_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,res}(\mathfrak{n}_{-})
generated by the elements
f_{\beta_{D}}^{(n_{D})}\ldots f_{\beta_{r+1}}^{(n_{r+1})}
, where at least one
n_{i}>0
. This ideal does not contain elements proportional to
f_{\beta_{t}}
as
1\leq t<p\leq r
. Therefore by the definition of
A_{t}
(see (
4.4.1
))
A_{t}((\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})({y_{i}^{(n)}}^{1})xT_{s}hz_{+}x^{\prime}\omega_{0}x_{i}^{(n)})=0
and hence by Lemma
4.4.1

f\otimes A_{t}((\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})({y_{i}^{(n)}}^{1})\cdot\omega_{0}x_{i}^{(n)})\in{\overline{J}^{p}_{q}}
. Note that this statement holds for
t=p
as well. This proves that
{\overline{J}^{p}_{q}}
is
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{r}}
–stable for
r\geq p
.

To show that
{J^{p}_{q}}
is
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{r}}
–stable for any
r\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
with
\beta_{r}\not\in\Delta_{s^{1}}^{s}
,
p\leq r
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
one has to apply similar arguments in conjunction with Lemmas
4.3.5
,
4.4.2
and
4.4.3
.

∎

4.6
Zhelobenko type operators for q-W–algebras

This section is central in this part and in the whole book. We are going to introduce and study some quantum analogues of the operators
\Pi_{p}
and
\Pi
defined in (
3.5.11
) and (
3.5.12
). It turns out that the analogues
P_{p}
of
\Pi_{p}
can be obtained by proper extrapolation of the expansion of the conjugation operator in (
3.5.11
) in terms of the adjoint action operator and by replacing the coefficients
G_{p}
with their quantum counterparts
B_{p}
introduced in (
4.5.5
). However, the proof of Proposition
4.6.1
which asserts that the image of their composition
P
consists of invariant elements with respect to the adjoint action of
U_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,res}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
is rather complicated. It entirely relies on the properties of the subspaces
{\overline{I}^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc},{I^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}\subset\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]
, of the quotients
\mathbb{C}_{p}^{loc}[G]
and of the adjoint action, which were obtained in the previous sections of this chapter.

The point is that in the quantum case we do not have in our disposal the isomorphism (
3.4.29
) which plays a crucial role in the proof of a similar property for the operator
\Pi
given by (
3.5.11
), (
3.5.12
) as one can see from the proofs of Propositions
3.5.1
and
3.5.2
.

We start with the definition of quantum analogues
P_{p}
of the operators
\Pi_{p}
. For technical reasons we shall also need more general operators
P_{p}^{k}
. More precisely, by Lemmas
4.5.3
and
4.5.5

for
\beta_{p}\not\in\Delta_{s^{1}}^{s}
,
f\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{I_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
,
p=1,\ldots,c
,
n,k=0,1,\ldots
,
n\geq k
, we have
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-k)}(f)\otimes B_{p}^{n}\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{I_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
. Note that by Lemma
4.5.4
for each
f\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{I_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-k)}(f)=0
for
n>N(f)
, where
N(f)\in\mathbb{N}
depends on
f
. Therefore we can define define an element
P_{p}^{k}(f)\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{I_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
by

P_{p}^{k}(f)=\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}q_{\beta_{p}}^{\frac{(n-1)(n-2k)}{2}}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-k)}(f)\otimes B_{p}^{n}.

(4.6.1)

Similarly, for
\beta_{p}\in\Delta_{s^{-1}}^{s}
,
f\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
,
p=1,\ldots,c
,
n,k=0,1,\ldots
,
n\geq k
, we have
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-k)}(f)\otimes B_{p}^{n}\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
. Note that by Lemma
4.5.4
for each
f\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-k)}(f)=0
for
n>N(f)
, where
N(f)\in\mathbb{N}
depends on
f
. Therefore we can define define an element
P_{p}^{k}(f)\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
by formula (
4.6.1
).

Let
\beta_{e}
be the first root of
\Delta_{s^{-1}}^{s}
in normal ordering (
1.6.3
) which does not belong to
\Delta_{s^{1}}^{s}
. Then, using the imbedding
{I_{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}^{loc}\subset{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}^{loc}
we can define the canonical projection
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{I_{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}^{loc}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}^{loc}
, and hence in this case for
f\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{I_{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}^{loc}=\mathbb{C}_{e}^{loc}[G]
projecting
P_{e}^{k}(f)\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{I_{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}^{loc}
defined in the former situation above onto
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}^{loc}
we can introduce an operator

P_{e}^{k}:\mathbb{C}_{e}^{loc}[G]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}^{loc}.

From the discussion above it follows that for all other indexes
p=1,\ldots,c
,
p\neq e
formula (
4.6.1
) can be used to define operators

P_{p}^{k}:\mathbb{C}_{p}^{loc}[G]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}_{p}^{loc}[G].

Denote
P_{p}^{0}=P_{p}
.

The following proposition summarizes the main properties of the operators
P_{p}
.

Proposition 4.6.1
.

For any
p=1,\ldots,c
the composition
P_{\leq p}=P_{1}\ldots P_{p}:\mathbb{C}_{p}^{loc}[G]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}_{1}^{loc}[G]
is well–defined. In fact for
p\neq e-1

P_{\leq p}
is well–defined as an operator with the domain
\mathbb{C}_{p+1}^{loc}[G]
and
P_{\leq e-1}
is well–defined as an operator with the domain
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}^{loc}
.

For
q\leq p
,
n>0
and for any
f\in\mathbb{C}_{p}^{loc}[G]
we have

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{q}}^{(n)}(P_{\leq p}(f))=0.

(4.6.2)

Moreover,

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p+1}}^{(n)}(P_{\leq p}(f))=P_{\leq p}({\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p+1}}^{(n)}f).

(4.6.3)

For
\beta_{p}\in\Delta_{s^{1}}^{s}
and any
f\in\mathbb{C}_{1}^{loc}[G]
such that
{\rm Ad}_{0}x(f)=\varepsilon_{s}(x)f~~\forall x\in U_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,res}([-\beta_{1},-\beta_{p}])
we have in
\mathbb{C}_{1}^{loc}[G]

P_{\leq p}(\bar{f})=f,

(4.6.4)

where
\bar{f}
is the class of
f
in
\mathbb{C}_{p}^{loc}[G]
.

For
\beta_{p}\not\in\Delta_{s^{1}}^{s}
and any
f\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{I^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}
such that
{\rm Ad}_{0}x(f)=\varepsilon_{s}(x)f~~\forall x\in U_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,res}([-\beta_{1},-\beta_{p}])
we have

P_{\leq p}(\bar{f})=f~(\text{mod}~{\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}),

(4.6.5)

where
\bar{f}
is the class of
f
in
\mathbb{C}_{p}^{loc}[G]
.

In particular, if
P=P_{\leq c}
then for any
f\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{I^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}
such that
{\rm Ad}_{0}x(f)=\varepsilon_{s}(x)f~~\forall x\in U_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,res}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
we have

P(\bar{f})=f~(\text{mod}~{\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}),

(4.6.6)

where
\bar{f}
is the class of
f
in
\mathbb{C}_{c}^{loc}[G]
.

The proof of Proposition
4.6.1
is quite long. It will be split into several lemmas. The strategy of the proof can be explained by looking at a similar situation in the classical case.

Namely, from the definition of the operators
\Pi_{p}
in (
3.5.11
), Proposition
3.5.2
and Corollary
3.5.4
it follows that the kernel of the operator
\Pi_{1}\ldots\Pi_{p}:\mathbb{C}[N_{-}sZM_{-}^{s}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[N_{-}sZM_{-}^{s}]
coincides with the vanishing ideal of
{N_{[-\beta_{p+1},-\beta_{D}]}sZM_{-}^{s}}\subset{N_{-}sZM_{-}^{s}}
. Similarly, in the quantum case we shall show that
P_{\leq p}
is well–defined as an operator with the domain of definition
\mathbb{C}_{p+1}^{loc}[G]
, i.e.
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}^{p+1}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}
or
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{I^{p+1}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}
depending on
p
.

Denote by
L_{p}
the the derivative of the action induced by the conjugation action by the elements of the one–parameter subgroup
\exp(-tX_{-\beta_{p}})
at
t=0
. From Propositions
3.5.1
,
3.5.2
and Corollary
3.5.4
one can immediately deduce that
L_{p}\circ\Pi_{p}:\mathbb{C}[N_{-}sZM_{-}^{s}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[N_{-}sZM_{-}^{s}]
is the zero operator modulo the vanishing ideal of
{N_{[-\beta_{p},-\beta_{D}]}sZM_{-}^{s}}\subset{N_{-}sZM_{-}^{s}}
. In the quantum case we shall show that for
n>0

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}P_{p}
is the zero operator modulo
{\overline{I}^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}
or
{I^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}
depending on
p
. This is done using an explicit calculation and observing that
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}
has some properties analogues to properties of derivatives of order
n
.

Finally to show that for
q\leq p
and
n>0

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{q}}^{(n)}(P_{\leq p}(f))=0
modulo
{\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}
we shall use the above mentioned property of the images of operators
P_{p}
and commutation relations between
P_{p}
and
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{q}}^{(n)}
for
q>p
which, in particular, lead to commutation relations (
4.6.3
).

Firstly, we are going to show by an explicit calculation that for
n>0

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}P_{p}
is the zero operator modulo
{\overline{I}^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}
(or
{I^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}
).
Since the right hand side of formula (
4.6.1
) contains products of elements from
\mathbb{C}_{q}^{loc}[G]
and of
B_{p}^{n}
, we have to study the adjoint action on such products.

Lemma 4.6.2
.

Let
B_{p}={A_{p}^{0}}^{-1}\otimes A_{p}\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]_{0}
,
f\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]
,
p=1,\ldots,c
. Let either
\beta_{p}\in\Delta_{s^{-1}}^{s}
or
\beta_{p}\not\in\Delta_{s^{1}}^{s}
. Then the identity

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}(\bar{f}\otimes B_{p}^{k})=\sum_{r=0}^{{\rm min}(n,k)}q_{\beta_{p}}^{(2n-r)k+\frac{r(r-1)}{2}-rn}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}k\\
r\end{array}\right]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-r)}(\bar{f})\otimes B_{p}^{k-r}

(4.6.7)

holds in
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
in the former case or in
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{I_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
in the latter case, where
\bar{f}
is the class of
f
in
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
or in
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{I_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
, respectively.

For any
f\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]
,
p,q=1,\ldots,c
,
q>p
let either
\beta_{p}\in\Delta_{s^{-1}}^{s}
or
\beta_{q}\not\in\Delta_{s^{1}}^{s}
. For any two roots
\alpha,\beta\in\Delta
we denote

c_{\alpha\beta}=q^{((\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+id)\alpha,\beta)}.

Then the identity

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{q}}^{(m)}(\bar{f}\otimes B_{p}^{n})=c_{\beta_{p}\beta_{q}}^{mn}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{q}}^{(m)}(\bar{f})\otimes B_{p}^{n}

(4.6.8)

holds in
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
in the former case or in
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{I_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
in the latter case, where
\bar{f}
is the class of
f
in
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
or in
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{I_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
, respectively.

Proof.

We prove (
4.6.7
) by induction over
k
. Firstly by (
4.2.4
), similarly to (
4.5.14
), we have for any
f\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}(f\otimes A_{p})=q^{n(\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}\beta_{p}-\beta_{p},\mu_{p}-\beta_{p})}{\rm Ad}_{0}(f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)})(f)\otimes A_{p}+

+q^{(n-1)(\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}\beta_{p}-\beta_{p},\mu_{p}-\beta_{p})}q_{{\beta_{p}}}^{-(n-1)}{\rm Ad}_{0}(f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-1)})(f)\otimes A_{p}(\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1}(f_{\beta_{p}})\cdot)+

+\sum_{k=1}^{n}q_{{\beta_{p}}}^{-k(n-k)}q^{n(\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}\beta_{p}-\beta_{p},\mu_{p}-\beta_{p})}{\rm Ad}_{0}(G_{\beta_{p}}^{-k}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-k)})(f)\otimes A_{p}(\cdot\omega_{0}(f_{\beta_{p}}^{(k)}))+

+\sum_{k=1}^{n}q_{{\beta_{p}}}^{-k(n-k)-(n-k-1)}{\rm Ad}_{0}(G_{\beta_{p}}^{-k}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-k-1)})(f)\otimes A_{p}(\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1}(G_{\beta_{p}}^{-n+1}f_{\beta_{p}})\cdot\omega_{0}(f_{\beta_{p}}^{(k)}))+

(4.6.9)

+\sum_{k=0}^{n}\sum_{p=0}^{n-k-2}q_{{\beta_{p}}}^{-k(n-k)-p(n-k-p)}{\rm Ad}_{0}(G_{\beta_{p}}^{-k}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(p)})(f)\otimes A_{p}(\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1}(G_{\beta_{p}}^{-k-p}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-k-p)})\cdot\omega_{0}(f_{\beta_{p}}^{(k)}))+

+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\sum_{i}q_{\beta_{p}}^{-k(n-k)}{\rm Ad}_{0}(G_{\beta_{p}}^{-k}x_{i}^{(n-k)})(f)\otimes A_{p}((\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})(G_{\beta_{p}}^{-k}y_{i}^{(n-k)})\cdot\omega_{0}(f_{\beta_{p}}^{(k)}))+

+\sum_{i}{\rm Ad}_{0}({y_{i}^{(n)}}^{2})(f)\otimes A_{p}((\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})({y_{i}^{(n)}}^{1})\cdot\omega_{0}(x_{i}^{(n)})).

Using arguments similar to those in the proof of Lemma
4.5.5
one can show that all terms in the right hand side of (
4.6.9
), except for the first two, vanish in
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
in the former case or in
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{I_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
in the latter case mentioned in the statement. Also by (
4.4.2
) we have
A_{p}((\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})(f_{\beta_{p}})\cdot)=A_{p}^{0}
, and hence in both cases we have in the corresponding quotient

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}(f\otimes A_{p})=q^{n(\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}\beta_{p}-\beta_{p},\mu_{p}-\beta_{p})}{\rm Ad}_{0}(f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)})(f)\otimes A_{p}+

+q^{(n-1)(\kappa{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}\beta_{p}-\beta_{p},\mu_{p}-\beta_{p})}q_{{\beta_{p}}}^{-(n-1)}{\rm Ad}_{0}(f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-1)})(f)\otimes A_{p}^{0}.

Since by definition elements of
S^{-1}
naturally act on
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
in the former case or on
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{I_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
in the latter case by multiplication from the right one can multiply the last identity by
(A_{p}^{0})^{-1}
from the right. Using commutation relations (
4.2.2
), formulas (
4.5.11
), (
4.5.13
) and recalling the definition of
B_{p}
we finally obtain in in the corresponding quotient

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}(f\otimes B_{p})=q_{\beta_{p}}^{n-1}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-1)}(f)+q_{\beta_{p}}^{2n}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}(f)\otimes B_{p}.

(4.6.10)

Since by definition elements of
S^{-1}
naturally act on both
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
in the former case and on
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{I_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
in the latter case by multiplication from the right one can multiply the last identity by
\Delta_{\mu}^{-1}
from the right. Using commutation relations (
4.2.2
), formulas (
4.5.11
), (
4.5.13
) and recalling the definition of
B_{p}
we finally obtain in the corresponding quotient

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}(\overline{f\otimes\Delta_{\mu}^{-1}}\otimes B_{p})=q_{\beta_{p}}^{n-1}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-1)}(\overline{f\otimes\Delta_{\mu}^{-1}})+q_{\beta_{p}}^{2n}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}(\overline{f\otimes\Delta_{\mu}^{-1}})\otimes B_{p},

where
\overline{f\otimes\Delta_{\mu}^{-1}}
should be regarded now as the class of
f\otimes\Delta_{\mu}^{-1}\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]
in the corresponding quotient. Since such classes generate these quotients formula (
4.6.10
) holds for any
f\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]

This establishes the base of induction.

Now we assume that (
4.6.7
) holds for some natural
k
and for all natural
n
and prove that it holds for
k+1
and all natural
n
. The arguments given below are the same for both cases, and we consider them simultaneously.
Since by Lemma
4.5.3
right multiplication gives rise to a well–defined action of
B_{p}
on the corresponding quotient introduced in the statement, we have in this quotient for any
f\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]
using the base of induction and the induction assumption

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}(\bar{f}\otimes B_{p}^{k+1})=q_{\beta_{p}}^{n-1}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-1)}(\bar{f}\otimes B_{p}^{k})+q_{\beta_{p}}^{2n}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}(\bar{f}\otimes B_{p}^{k})\otimes B_{p}=

=q_{\beta_{p}}^{n-1}\sum_{r=0}^{{\rm min}(n-1,k)}q_{\beta_{p}}^{(2(n-1)-r)k+\frac{r(r-1)}{2}-r(n-1)}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}k\\
r\end{array}\right]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-r-1)}(\bar{f})\otimes B_{p}^{k-r}+

+q_{\beta_{p}}^{2n}\sum_{r=0}^{{\rm min}(n,k)}q_{\beta_{p}}^{(2n-r)k+\frac{r(r-1)}{2}-rn}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}k\\
r\end{array}\right]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-r)}(\bar{f})\otimes B_{p}^{k-r+1}=

=\sum_{r=0}^{{\rm min}(n,k+1)}q_{\beta_{p}}^{(2n-r)(k+1)+\frac{r(r-1)}{2}-rn}\left(q_{\beta_{p}}^{r}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}k\\
r\end{array}\right]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}+q_{\beta_{p}}^{r-(k+1)}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}k\\
r-1\end{array}\right]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}\right){\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-r)}(\bar{f})\otimes B_{p}^{k-r+1}=

=\sum_{r=0}^{{\rm min}(n,k+1)}q_{\beta_{p}}^{(2n-r)(k+1)+\frac{r(r-1)}{2}-rn}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}k+1\\
r\end{array}\right]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-r)}(\bar{f})\otimes B_{p}^{k+1-r},

where we used the identity

q_{\beta_{p}}^{r}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}k\\
r\end{array}\right]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}+q_{\beta_{p}}^{r-(k+1)}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}k\\
r-1\end{array}\right]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}k+1\\
r\end{array}\right]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}

which can be found e.g. in
[
58
]
, Proposition 6.1.
This establishes the induction step and completes the proof of (
4.6.7
).

Formula (
4.6.8
) is proved in a similar way by induction using (
4.2.4
), (
4.4.2
), the definition of
B_{p}
, (
4.4.1
) and Lemmas
4.4.3
and
4.5.3
.

∎

The previous lemma shows that in an appropriate quotient the operator
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}
acts on
f\otimes B_{p}^{k}
as a quantum analogue of a derivation of order
n
such that the derivative of
B_{p}
by
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}
is equal to one. Recalling the definition of the classical counterpart
G_{p}
of
B_{p}
in (
3.5.10
) one can observe that similar formula can be obtained in the classical case as well. It will be given by specializing (
4.6.7
) at
q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}=1
, and the
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}
–action will be replaced with
\frac{1}{n!}L_{p}^{n}
.

In the next lemma we show that for
n>0
the composition
{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}P_{p}
is the zero operator modulo
{\overline{I}^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}
(or
{I^{p}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}
). At the same time this lemma lays a basis for the proof of the fact that the kernel of
P_{\leq p}
contains
{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p+1}}^{loc}
(or
{I_{\mathcal{B}}^{p+1}}^{loc}
). For
k=0
formula (
4.6.11
) is a quantum analogue of the statement in the line before (
3.5.8
).

Lemma 4.6.3
.

For any
f\in\mathbb{C}_{p}^{loc}[G]
,
p=1,\ldots,c
,
k=0,1,\ldots
we have

P_{p}^{k}(f\otimes B_{p})=0,

(4.6.11)

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}(P_{p}(f))=0,~n>0.

(4.6.12)

Proof.

Since by Lemma
4.5.3
right multiplication by
B_{p}
gives rise to an action of
B_{p}
on
\mathbb{C}_{p}^{loc}[G]
and on the image of
P_{p}^{k}
we have by (
4.6.7
) in the image of
P_{p}^{k}

P_{p}^{k}(f\otimes B_{p})=\sum_{n=k+1}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}q_{\beta_{p}}^{\frac{(n-1)(n-2k)}{2}}q_{\beta_{p}}^{n-k-1}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-k-1)}(f)\otimes B_{p}^{n}+

+\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}q_{\beta_{p}}^{\frac{(n-1)(n-2k)}{2}}q_{\beta_{p}}^{2(n-k)}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-k)}(f)\otimes B_{p}^{n+1}=

=-\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}{(-1)^{n}q_{\beta_{p}}^{\frac{n(n+1-2k)}{2}+n-k}}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-k)}(f)\otimes B_{p}^{n+1}+

+\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}{(-1)^{n}q_{\beta_{p}}^{\frac{(n-1)(n-2k)}{2}+2n-2k}}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-k)}(f)\otimes B_{p}^{n+1}=0

which proves (
4.6.11
).

Similarly by (
4.6.7
) we obtain in in the image of
P_{p}^{k}

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}(P_{p}(f))={\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}q_{\beta_{p}}^{\frac{(k-1)k}{2}}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(k)}(f)\otimes B_{p}^{k}\right)=

=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{t=0}^{{\rm min}(n,k)}(-1)^{k}q_{\beta_{p}}^{\frac{(k-1)k}{2}+(2n-t)k+\frac{t(t-1)}{2}-tn}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}k\\
t\end{array}\right]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}{\rm Ad}_{0}(f_{\beta_{p}}^{(k)}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-t)})(f)\otimes B_{p}^{k-t}.

Introducing a new variable of summation
k-t=r
and using the identity

f_{\beta_{p}}^{(k)}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-t)}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}n+k-t\\
k\end{array}\right]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n+k-t)}

we get

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}(P_{p}(f))=

=\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\sum_{t=0}^{n}(-1)^{r+t}q_{\beta_{p}}^{\frac{(r+t-1)(r+t)}{2}+(2n-t)(r+t)+\frac{t(t-1)}{2}-tn}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}r+t\\
t\end{array}\right]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}r+n\\
r+t\end{array}\right]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}{\rm Ad}_{0}(f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n+r)})(f)\otimes B_{p}^{r}.

Now recalling that

\left[\begin{array}[]{c}r+t\\
t\end{array}\right]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}r+n\\
r+t\end{array}\right]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}n\\
t\end{array}\right]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}r+n\\
r\end{array}\right]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}

we obtain

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}(P_{p}(f))=

=\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{r}q_{\beta_{p}}^{\frac{(r-1)r}{2}+2nr}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}r+n\\
r\end{array}\right]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}\left(\sum_{t=0}^{n}(-1)^{t}q_{\beta_{p}}^{-t+tn}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}n\\
t\end{array}\right]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}\right){\rm Ad}_{0}(f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n+r)})(f)\otimes B_{p}^{r}=0,

where we used the identity

\sum_{t=0}^{n}(-1)^{t}q_{\beta_{p}}^{-t+tn}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}n\\
t\end{array}\right]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}=0

which follows from the q–binomial theorem (see e.g.
[
40
]
, Ch. 1).

This proves (
4.6.12
).

∎

As we observed above, the definition of the operators
\Pi_{p}
in (
3.5.11
), Proposition
3.5.2
and Corollary
3.5.4
imply that the kernel of the operator
\Pi_{1}\ldots\Pi_{p}:\mathbb{C}[N_{-}sZM_{-}^{s}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[N_{-}sZM_{-}^{s}]
coincides with the vanishing ideal of
{N_{[-\beta_{p+1},-\beta_{D}]}sZM_{-}^{s}}\subset{N_{-}sZM_{-}^{s}}
. The following lemma is a quantum counterpart of this property.

Lemma 4.6.4
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. If
\beta_{p}\not\in\Delta_{s^{1}}^{s}
then
P_{p}^{k}({I_{\mathcal{B}}^{p+1}}^{loc}/{I_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc})=0
, if
\beta_{p}\in\Delta_{s^{1}}^{s}
then
P_{p}^{k}({\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p+1}}^{loc}/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc})=0
. Thus for
p\neq e-1

P_{p}^{k}
is well–defined as an operator with the domain
\mathbb{C}_{p+1}^{loc}[G]
and
P_{e-1}^{k}
is well–defined as an operator with the domain
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}^{loc}
.

Moreover, for any
p,q=1,\ldots,c

q\leq p
and any
k_{q},\ldots,k_{p}\in\mathbb{N}
the composition
P_{q}^{k_{q}}\ldots P_{p}^{k_{p}}
is well–defined and gives rise to an operator with domain
\mathbb{C}_{p+1}^{loc}[G]
if
p\neq e-1
and
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}^{loc}
if
p=e-1
, and the target space being the target space of
P_{q}^{k_{q}}

In particular, the composition
P_{\leq p}=P_{1}\ldots P_{p}
is well–defined and gives rise to an operator with domain
\mathbb{C}_{p+1}^{loc}[G]
if
p\neq e-1
and
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}^{loc}
if
p=e-1
, and the target space being
\mathbb{C}_{1}^{loc}[G]
.

Proof.

Let us show, for instance, that if
\beta_{p}\in\Delta_{s^{1}}^{s}
then
P_{p}^{k}({\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p+1}}^{loc}/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc})=0
. The other case is considered in a similar way.

Indeed, by the definition of
{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p+1}}
and by commutation relations (
4.2.2
) for any element
f\in{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p+1}}^{loc}
there exists an element
u\in\mathcal{B}
,
u\neq 0
and
g,h\in{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
such that
uf=g+h\otimes B_{p}
. Let
\bar{f},\bar{g}
,
\overline{h\otimes B_{p}}
and
\bar{h}
be the classes of the corresponding elements in
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]/{\overline{I}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}^{loc}
. Note that
\overline{h\otimes B_{p}}=\bar{h}\otimes B_{p}
and
\bar{g}=0
, and by the previous Lemma
P_{p}^{k_{p}}(\overline{h\otimes B_{p}})=P_{p}^{k_{p}}(\bar{h}\otimes B_{p})=0
. Thus
P_{p}^{k_{p}}(\overline{uf})=uP_{p}^{k_{p}}(\bar{f})=0
. Since
u\neq 0
this implies
P_{p}^{k_{p}}(\bar{f})=0
.
This completes the proof in the considered case.

The remaining statements of the lemma are simple corollaries of the first assertion.

∎

Next, we are going to study how the adjoint action of quantum root vectors commutes with the operators
P_{p}
. For this purpose we shall need some commutation relations between quantum root vectors stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.6.5
.

For any
\alpha<\beta
,
\alpha,\beta\in\Delta_{+}
and any
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

f_{\alpha}^{(m)}f_{\beta}^{(n)}=c_{\alpha\beta}^{mn}\sum_{p=0}^{m}q_{\alpha}^{p(m-1)}\sum_{\alpha<\delta_{1}<\ldots<\delta_{n}\leq\beta}d_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}^{p}f_{\delta_{1}}^{(p_{1})}\ldots f_{\delta_{n}}^{(p_{n})}f_{\alpha}^{(m-p)},

(4.6.13)

where the coefficients
d_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}^{p}\in\mathcal{B}
do not depend on
m
.

Proof.

To prove this lemma it suffices to show that

f_{\alpha}^{m}f_{\beta}^{n}=c_{\alpha\beta}^{mn}\sum_{p=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha<\delta_{1}<\ldots<\delta_{n}\leq\beta}c_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}^{p}S_{m}^{p}f_{\delta_{1}}^{p_{1}}\ldots f_{\delta_{n}}^{p_{n}}f_{\alpha}^{m-p},

(4.6.14)

where

S_{m}^{p}=q_{\alpha}^{p(m-1)}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}m\\
p\end{array}\right]_{q_{\alpha}},

and the coefficients
c_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}^{p}\in\mathcal{A}
do not depend on
m
.

Indeed, dividing (
4.6.14
) by
[n]_{q_{\alpha}}![m]_{q_{\beta}}!
we arrive at an identity of the form (
4.6.13
) where the coefficients
d_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}^{p}
a priori belong to
\mathbb{C}(q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}})
.
But by the uniqueness of the Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt decomposition in
U_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,res}(\mathfrak{n}_{-})
(see Lemma
2.7.2
) we have
d_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}^{p}\in\mathcal{B}
.

Now we establish (
4.6.14
). Firstly we consider the case
n=1
.
By commutation relations (
2.7.11
) we have

{f}_{\alpha}^{m}{f}_{\beta}=c_{\alpha\beta}^{m}{f}_{\beta}{f}_{\alpha}^{m}+\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{m-1}c_{\alpha\beta}^{m-k_{1}-1}{f}_{\alpha}^{k_{1}}\sum_{\alpha<\delta_{1}<\ldots<\delta_{n}<\beta}C(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}){f}_{\delta_{1}}^{p_{1}}{f}_{\delta_{2}}^{p_{2}}\ldots{f}_{\delta_{n}}^{p_{n}}{f}_{\alpha}^{m-k_{1}-1},

(4.6.15)

where
C(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\in\mathcal{A}
.
The first term in the right hand side of this formula agrees with the term in the right hand side of (
4.6.14
) corresponding to
p=0
.
The other terms in the right hand side of (
4.6.15
) will contribute to the terms in the right hand side of (
4.6.14
) with
p>0
.

Denote

{D}_{\beta}{f}_{\alpha}=\sum_{\alpha<\delta_{1}<\ldots<\delta_{n}<\beta}C(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}){f}_{\delta_{1}}^{p_{1}}{f}_{\delta_{2}}^{p_{2}}\ldots{f}_{\delta_{n}}^{p_{n}}.

Then (
4.6.15
) takes the form

{f}_{\alpha}^{m}{f}_{\beta}=c_{\alpha\beta}^{m}{f}_{\beta}{f}_{\alpha}^{m}+\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{m-1}c_{\alpha\beta}^{m-k_{1}-1}{f}_{\alpha}^{k_{1}}{D}_{\beta}{f}_{\alpha}{f}_{\alpha}^{m-k_{1}-1}.

(4.6.16)

To get the term in the right hand side of (
4.6.14
) corresponding to
p=1
we have to move
{f}_{\alpha}^{k_{1}}
to the right in (
4.6.15
) using commutation relations (
2.7.11
),

{f}_{\alpha}{f}_{\delta}=c_{\alpha,\delta}{f}_{\delta}{f}_{\alpha}+\sum_{\alpha<\delta_{1}<\ldots<\delta_{n}<\delta}C(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}){f}_{\delta_{1}}^{p_{1}}{f}_{\delta_{2}}^{p_{2}}\ldots{f}_{\delta_{n}}^{p_{n}}=c_{\alpha,\delta}{f}_{\delta}{f}_{\alpha}+{D}_{\delta}{f}_{\alpha},~\alpha<\delta,

(4.6.17)

and keep the leading term
c_{\alpha,\delta}{f}_{\delta}{f}_{\alpha}
for each
\delta=\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{n}
.
Since for weight reasons
\delta_{1}p_{1}+\ldots+\delta_{n}p_{n}=\alpha+\beta
, so the weight of
{D}_{\beta}{f}_{\alpha}
is
\alpha+\beta
, this yields

{f}_{\alpha}^{m}{f}_{\beta}=c_{\alpha\beta}^{m}{f}_{\beta}{f}_{\alpha}^{m}+\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{m-1}c_{\alpha\beta}^{m-k_{1}-1}c_{\alpha\alpha+\beta}^{k_{1}}{D}_{\beta}{f}_{\alpha}{f}_{\alpha}^{m-1}+R,

(4.6.18)

where
R
stands for the terms contributing to the terms in the right hand side of (
4.6.14
) with
p>1
.
Now

\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{m-1}c_{\alpha\beta}^{m-k_{1}-1}c_{\alpha\alpha+\beta}^{k_{1}}=c_{\alpha\beta}^{m-1}\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{m-1}c_{\alpha\beta}^{-k_{1}}c_{\alpha\beta}^{k_{1}}c_{\alpha\alpha}^{k_{1}}=c_{\alpha\beta}^{m-1}\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{m-1}q_{\alpha}^{2k_{1}}=c_{\alpha\beta}^{m-1}\frac{q_{\alpha}^{2m}-1}{q_{\alpha}^{2}-1}=c_{\alpha\beta}^{m-1}q_{\alpha}^{m-1}[m]_{q_{\alpha}}=c_{\alpha\beta}^{m-1}S_{m}^{1},

and (
4.6.18
) takes the form

{f}_{\alpha}^{m}{f}_{\beta}=c_{\alpha\beta}^{m}{f}_{\beta}{f}_{\alpha}^{m}+c_{\alpha\beta}^{m-1}S_{m}^{1}\sum_{\alpha<\delta_{1}<\ldots<\delta_{n}<\beta}C(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}){f}_{\delta_{1}}^{p_{1}}{f}_{\delta_{2}}^{p_{2}}\ldots{f}_{\delta_{n}}^{p_{n}}{f}_{\alpha}^{m-1}+R.

(4.6.19)

The second term in the right hand side of this formula agrees with the term in the right hand side of (
4.6.14
) corresponding to
p=1
.

To get the term in the right hand side of (
4.6.14
) corresponding to
p=2
we have to move
{f}_{\alpha}^{k_{1}}
to the right in (
4.6.15
) using commutation relations (
2.7.11
),

{f}_{\alpha}^{k_{1}}{f}_{\delta_{l}}=c_{\alpha,\delta}^{k}{f}_{\delta_{l}}{f}_{\alpha}^{k_{1}}+\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}-1}c_{\alpha\beta}^{k_{1}-k_{2}-1}{f}_{\alpha}^{k_{2}}D_{\delta_{l}}f_{\alpha}{f}_{\alpha}^{k_{1}-k_{2}-1},

(4.6.20)

and keep the terms containing one “differentiation”
{D}_{\delta_{l}}{f}_{\alpha}
for some
l=1,\ldots,n
.
Since the weight of
{D}_{\delta_{l}}{f}_{\alpha}
is
\alpha+\delta_{l}
, this yields

R=\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{m-1}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}-1}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{q=1}^{p_{l}}c_{\alpha\beta}^{m-k_{1}-1}c_{\alpha\delta_{1}}^{k_{1}p_{1}}\ldots c_{\alpha\delta_{l-1}}^{k_{1}p_{l-1}}c_{\alpha\delta_{l}}^{k_{1}(p_{l}-q)}c_{\alpha\delta_{l}}^{k_{1}-k_{2}-1}c_{\alpha\alpha+\delta_{l}}^{k_{2}}c_{\alpha\delta_{l}}^{(k_{1}-1)(q-1)}c_{\alpha\delta_{l+1}}^{(k_{1}-1)p_{l+1}}\ldots c_{\alpha\delta_{n}}^{(k_{1}-1)p_{n}}\times

(4.6.21)

\times C(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}){f}_{\delta_{1}}^{p_{1}}\ldots{f}_{\delta_{l-1}}^{p_{l-1}}{f}_{\delta_{l}}^{p_{l}-q}D_{\delta_{l}}f_{\alpha}{f}_{\delta_{l}}^{q-1}{f}_{\delta_{l+1}}^{p_{l+1}}\ldots{f}_{\delta_{n}}^{p_{n}}f_{\alpha}^{m-2}+R_{1},

where
R_{1}
contains only terms with more than one “differentiation”
{D}_{\delta_{l}}{f}_{\alpha}
for some
l=1,\ldots,n
.

Now by the definition of the coefficients
c_{\alpha\beta}
and using the identity
\delta_{1}p_{1}+\ldots+\delta_{n}p_{n}=\alpha+\beta
we have

c_{\alpha\beta}^{m-k_{1}-1}c_{\alpha\delta_{1}}^{k_{1}p_{1}}\ldots c_{\alpha\delta_{l-1}}^{k_{1}p_{l-1}}c_{\alpha\delta_{l}}^{k_{1}(p_{l}-q)}c_{\alpha\delta_{l}}^{k_{1}-k_{2}-1}c_{\alpha\alpha+\delta_{l}}^{k_{2}}c_{\alpha\delta_{l}}^{(k_{1}-1)(q-1)}c_{\alpha\delta_{l+1}}^{(k_{1}-1)p_{l+1}}\ldots c_{\alpha\delta_{n}}^{(k_{1}-1)p_{n}}=

=c_{\alpha\beta}^{m-1}c_{\alpha\alpha}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}c_{\alpha\delta_{l}}^{-q}c_{\alpha\delta_{l+1}}^{-p_{l+1}}\ldots c_{\alpha\delta_{n}}^{-p_{n}},

so

\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{m-1}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}-1}c_{\alpha\beta}^{m-k_{1}-1}c_{\alpha\delta_{1}}^{k_{1}p_{1}}\ldots c_{\alpha\delta_{l-1}}^{k_{1}p_{l-1}}c_{\alpha\delta_{l}}^{k_{1}(p_{l}-q)}c_{\alpha\delta_{l}}^{k_{1}-k_{2}-1}c_{\alpha\alpha+\delta_{l}}^{k_{2}}c_{\alpha\delta_{l}}^{(k_{1}-1)(q-1)}c_{\alpha\delta_{l+1}}^{(k_{1}-1)p_{l+1}}\ldots c_{\alpha\delta_{n}}^{(k_{1}-1)p_{n}}=

=c_{\alpha\beta}^{m-1}c_{\alpha\delta_{l}}^{-q}c_{\alpha\delta_{l+1}}^{-p_{l+1}}\ldots c_{\alpha\delta_{n}}^{-p_{n}}\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{m-1}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}-1}c_{\alpha\alpha}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}=

=c_{\alpha\beta}^{m-1}c_{\alpha\delta_{l}}^{-q}c_{\alpha\delta_{l+1}}^{-p_{l+1}}\ldots c_{\alpha\delta_{n}}^{-p_{n}}\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{m-1}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}-1}q_{\alpha}^{2(k_{1}+k_{2})},

and, after combining terms containing the same monomials
{f}_{\delta_{1}}^{p_{1}}{f}_{\delta_{2}}^{p_{2}}\ldots{f}_{\delta_{n}}^{p_{n}}
, equation (
4.6.21
) takes the form

R=c_{\alpha\beta}^{m}\sum_{\alpha<\delta_{1}<\ldots<\delta_{n}\leq\beta}c_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}^{2}S_{m}^{2}f_{\delta_{1}}^{p_{1}}\ldots f_{\delta_{n}}^{p_{n}}f_{\alpha}^{m-2}+R_{1},

(4.6.22)

where

S_{m}^{2}=\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{m-1}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}-1}q_{\alpha}^{2(k_{1}+k_{2})},

and the coefficients
c_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}^{2}\in\mathcal{A}
do not depend on
m
.
The first term in (
4.6.22
) will agree with with the term in the right hand side of (
4.6.14
) corresponding to
p=2
if we show that
S_{m}^{2}=q_{\alpha}^{2(m-1)}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}m\\
2\end{array}\right]_{q_{\alpha}}
.

Now we can continue in the same way taking into account more “derivatives”
{D}_{\delta_{l}}{f}_{\alpha}
to obtain formula (
4.6.14
) with
n=1
, where

S_{m}^{p}=\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{m-1}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}-1}\ldots\sum_{k_{p}=0}^{k_{p-1}-1}q_{\alpha}^{2(k_{1}+k_{2}+\ldots+k_{p})}.

From this formula it follows that the coefficients
S_{m}^{p}
satisfy the following relations

S_{m}^{p}=S_{m-1}^{p}+q_{\alpha}^{2(m-1)}S_{m-1}^{p-1}.

(4.6.23)

On the other hand for the q-binomial coefficients we have

\left[\begin{array}[]{c}m\\
p\end{array}\right]_{q_{\alpha}}=q_{\alpha}^{-p}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}m-1\\
p\end{array}\right]_{q_{\alpha}}+q_{\alpha}^{m-p}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}m-1\\
p-1\end{array}\right]_{q_{\alpha}}.

If we denote

C_{m}^{p}=q_{\alpha}^{p(m-1)}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}m\\
p\end{array}\right]_{q_{\alpha}}

this implies

C_{m}^{p}=C_{m-1}^{p}+q_{\alpha}^{2(m-1)}C_{m-1}^{p-1},

i.e. the coefficients
C_{m}^{p}
satisfy the same relations (
4.6.23
) as the coefficients
S_{m}^{p}
. Since these relations completely determine them once
S_{2}^{p}
are known and
C_{2}^{0}=S_{2}^{0}=1
,
C_{2}^{2}=S_{2}^{2}=q_{\alpha}^{2}
,
C_{2}^{1}=S_{2}^{1}=q_{\alpha}[2]_{q_{\alpha}}
, we deduce that
C_{m}^{p}=S_{m}^{p}
for all
m
and
p
(compare with Theorem 6.1 in
[
58
]
). Thus formula (
4.6.14
) is established for
n=1
.

For
n>1
we argue by induction over
n
. The base of the induction is already established. Now assuming that the statement is true for
n-1
we show that it holds for
n
. Indeed,

f_{\alpha}^{m}f_{\beta}^{n}=c_{\alpha\beta}^{m(n-1)}\sum_{q=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha<\delta_{1}<\ldots<\delta_{n}\leq\beta}c_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}^{q}S_{m}^{q}f_{\delta_{1}}^{p_{1}}\ldots f_{\delta_{n}}^{p_{n}}f_{\alpha}^{m-q}f_{\beta}=

(4.6.24)

=c_{\alpha\beta}^{m(n-1)}\sum_{q=0}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m-q}\sum_{\alpha<\delta_{1}<\ldots<\delta_{n}\leq\beta}c_{\alpha\beta}^{m-p}c_{p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{n}^{\prime}}^{\prime}S_{m}^{q}S_{m-q}^{k}f_{\delta_{1}}^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\ldots f_{\delta_{n}}^{p_{n}^{\prime}}f_{\alpha}^{m-q-k},

where
c_{p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{n}^{\prime}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
do not depend on
m
.

Now

S_{m}^{q}S_{m-q}^{k}=\frac{q_{\alpha}^{-kq}}{[k]_{q_{\alpha}}!}S_{m}^{k+q}.

Introducing new summation variable
p=k+q
in (
4.6.24
) we can rewrite (
4.6.24
) in the form

f_{\alpha}^{m}f_{\beta}^{n}=c_{\alpha\beta}^{mn}\sum_{p=0}^{m}\sum_{q=0}^{p}\sum_{\alpha<\delta_{1}<\ldots<\delta_{n}\leq\beta}c_{\alpha\beta}^{-p}\frac{q_{\alpha}^{-(p-q)q}}{[p-q]_{q_{\alpha}}!}S_{m}^{p}c_{p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{n}^{\prime}}^{\prime}f_{\delta_{1}}^{p_{1}^{\prime}}\ldots f_{\delta_{n}}^{p_{n}^{\prime}}f_{\alpha}^{m-p}.

Combining terms containing the same monomials
{f}_{\delta_{1}}^{p_{1}^{\prime}}{f}_{\delta_{2}}^{p_{2}^{\prime}}\ldots{f}_{\delta_{n}}^{p_{n}^{\prime}}
we arrive at an identity of the form (
4.6.14
) where the coefficients
c_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}^{p}
a priori belong to
\mathbb{C}(q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}})
.
But by the uniqueness of the Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt decomposition in
U_{\mathcal{A}}^{s}(\mathfrak{n}_{-})
(see Lemma
2.7.2
) we have
c_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}^{p}\in\mathcal{A}
. This completes the proof of (
4.6.14
).

∎

The next lemma shows how the adjoint action of the quantum root vectors commutes with the operators
P_{p}
.

Lemma 4.6.6
.

For any
p,q=1,\ldots,c
,
q>p
and any
f
from the domain of
P_{p}
we have

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{q}}^{(m)}(P_{p}(f))=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}P_{p}^{k}({\rm Ad}_{0}(\sum_{p_{p+1},\ldots p_{q}}d_{p_{p+1},\ldots,p_{q}}^{k}f_{\beta_{p+1}}^{(p_{p+1})}\ldots f_{\beta_{q}}^{(p_{q})})(f)),

(4.6.25)

where only a finite number of terms in the sum are non-zero,
d_{p_{p+1},\ldots,p_{q}}^{k}\in\mathcal{B}
,

d_{p_{p+1},\ldots,p_{q-1},m}^{k}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}1~~{\rm if}~~k=p_{p+1}=\ldots=p_{q-1}=0\\
0~~{\rm otherwise}\end{array}\right.,

and for
n<m
and any
k

d_{0,\ldots,0,n}^{k}=0.

In particular,

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p+1}}^{(m)}(P_{p}(f))=P_{p}({\rm Ad}_{0}(f_{\beta_{p+1}}^{(m)})(f)).

(4.6.26)

Proof.

By formula (
4.6.8
) and by the definition of
P_{p}
we have

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{q}}^{(m)}(P_{p}(f))=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}q_{\beta_{p}}^{\frac{(n-1)n}{2}}d_{\beta_{p}\beta_{q}}^{mn}{\rm Ad}_{0}(f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}f_{\beta_{q}}^{(m)})(f)\otimes B_{p}^{n}.

Using (
4.6.13
) we can rewrite this formula as follows

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{q}}^{(m)}(P_{p}(f))=

=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{n}q_{\beta_{p}}^{\frac{(n-1)n}{2}}q_{\beta_{p}}^{-k(n-1)}c_{\beta_{p}\beta_{q}}^{mn}\omega_{0}(c_{\beta_{p}\beta_{q}}^{mn}){\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-k)}({\rm Ad}_{0}(\sum_{\beta_{p}<\delta_{1}<\ldots<\delta_{n}\leq\beta_{q}}d_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}^{k}f_{\delta_{1}}^{(p_{1})}\ldots f_{\delta_{n}}^{(p_{n})})(f)).

Now recalling that
\omega_{0}(q)=q^{-1}
and swapping the order of summation in the last formula we get

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{q}}^{(m)}(P_{p}(f))=

=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{n}q_{\beta_{p}}^{\frac{(n-1)n}{2}}q_{\beta_{p}}^{-k(n-1)}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-k)}({\rm Ad}_{0}(\sum_{\beta_{p}<\delta_{1}<\ldots<\delta_{n}\leq\beta_{q}}d_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}^{k}f_{\delta_{1}}^{(p_{1})}\ldots f_{\delta_{n}}^{(p_{n})})(f))=

=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}q_{\beta_{p}}^{\frac{(n-1)(n-2k)}{2}}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n-k)}({\rm Ad}_{0}(\sum_{\beta_{p}<\delta_{1}<\ldots<\delta_{n}\leq\beta_{q}}d_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}^{k}f_{\delta_{1}}^{(p_{1})}\ldots f_{\delta_{n}}^{(p_{n})})(f))=

=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}P_{p}^{k}({\rm Ad}_{0}(\sum_{\beta_{p}<\delta_{1}<\ldots<\delta_{n}\leq\beta_{q}}d_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}^{k}f_{\delta_{1}}^{(p_{1})}\ldots f_{\delta_{n}}^{(p_{n})})(f)).

(
4.6.26
) is obtained in a similar way using the relation
f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}f_{\beta_{p+1}}^{(m)}=c_{\beta_{p}\beta_{p+1}}^{mn}f_{\beta_{p+1}}^{(m)}f_{\beta_{p}}^{(n)}
.

∎

Now we have all prerequisites to prove Proposition
4.6.1
.

Proof of Proposition
4.6.1

By Lemmas
4.6.4
,
4.6.6
, the first claim in Lemma
4.5.5
and formula (
4.6.12
) we have for any
f
from the domain of
P_{p}

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{q}}^{(n)}(P_{\leq p}(f))=\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}P_{1}^{k_{1}}\left({\rm Ad}_{0}(\sum_{p_{2},\ldots,p_{q}}d_{p_{2},\ldots,p_{n}}^{k_{1}}f_{\beta_{2}}^{(p_{2})}\ldots f_{\beta_{q}}^{(p_{q})})(P_{2}\ldots P_{p}(f))\right)=

=\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}P_{1}^{k_{1}}\left(\sum_{p_{2},\ldots,p_{q}}{\rm Ad}_{0}(d_{p_{2},\ldots,p_{n}}^{k_{1}}f_{\beta_{3}}^{(p_{3})}\ldots f_{\beta_{q}}^{(p_{q})}){\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{2}}^{(p_{2})}(P_{2}\ldots P_{p}(f))\right)=

\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}P_{1}^{k_{1}}\left(\sum_{p_{3},\ldots,p_{q}}{\rm Ad}_{0}(d_{0,p_{3},\ldots,p_{n}}^{k_{1}}f_{\beta_{3}}^{(p_{3})}\ldots f_{\beta_{q}}^{(p_{q})})(P_{2}\ldots P_{p}(f))\right),

where, by weight counting in the left hand side and in the right hand side, for each
k_{1}

d_{0,0\ldots,0}^{k_{1}}=0
.

Similarly,

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{q}}^{(n)}(P_{\leq p}(f))=\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}P_{1}^{k_{1}}\left(\sum_{p_{3},\ldots,p_{q}}{\rm Ad}_{0}(d_{0,p_{3},\ldots,p_{n}}^{k_{1}}f_{\beta_{3}}^{(p_{3})}\ldots f_{\beta_{q}}^{(p_{q})})(P_{2}\ldots P_{p}(f))\right)=

=\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}P_{1}^{k_{1}}\left(\sum_{p_{3},\ldots,p_{q}}{\rm Ad}_{0}(d_{0,p_{3},\ldots,p_{n}}^{k_{1}}f_{\beta_{4}}^{(p_{4})}\ldots f_{\beta_{q}}^{(p_{q})})(P_{2}{\rm Ad}_{0}(f_{\beta_{3}}^{(p_{3})})P_{3}\ldots P_{p}(f))\right)=

=\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}P_{1}^{k_{1}}\left(\sum_{p_{4},\ldots,p_{q}}{\rm Ad}_{0}(d_{0,0,p_{4},\ldots,p_{n}}^{k_{1}}f_{\beta_{4}}^{(p_{4})}\ldots f_{\beta_{q}}^{(p_{q})})(P_{2}P_{3}\ldots P_{p}(f))\right).

Now we proceed along the same line,

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{q}}^{(n)}(P_{\leq p}(f))=

=\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{\infty}P_{1}^{k_{1}}\left(\sum_{p_{4},\ldots,p_{q}}{\rm Ad}_{0}(d_{0,0,p_{4},\ldots,p_{n}}^{k_{1}}f_{\beta_{5}}^{(p_{5})}\ldots f_{\beta_{q}}^{(p_{q})})(P_{2}^{k_{2}}\sum_{p_{3}^{\prime},p_{4}^{\prime}}{\rm Ad}_{0}(d_{p_{3}^{\prime},p_{4}^{\prime}}^{k_{2}}f_{\beta_{3}}^{(p_{3}^{\prime})}f_{\beta_{4}}^{(p_{4}^{\prime})})P_{3}\ldots P_{p}(f))\right)=

=\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{\infty}P_{1}^{k_{1}}\left(\sum_{p_{4},\ldots,p_{q}}{\rm Ad}_{0}(d_{0,0,p_{4},\ldots,p_{n}}^{k_{1}}f_{\beta_{5}}^{(p_{5})}\ldots f_{\beta_{q}}^{(p_{q})})(P_{2}^{k_{2}}\sum_{p_{4}^{\prime}}{\rm Ad}_{0}(d_{0,p_{4}^{\prime}}^{k_{2}}f_{\beta_{4}}^{(p_{4}^{\prime})})P_{3}\ldots P_{p}(f))\right)=

=\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{\infty}P_{1}^{k_{1}}\left(\sum_{p_{4},\ldots,p_{q}}{\rm Ad}_{0}(d_{0,0,p_{4},\ldots,p_{n}}^{k_{1}}f_{\beta_{5}}^{(p_{5})}\ldots f_{\beta_{q}}^{(p_{q})})(P_{2}^{k_{2}}\sum_{p_{4}^{\prime}}P_{3}{\rm Ad}_{0}(d_{0,p_{4}^{\prime}}^{k_{2}}f_{\beta_{4}}^{(p_{4}^{\prime})})P_{4}\ldots P_{p}(f))\right)=

=\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{\infty}P_{1}^{k_{1}}\left(\sum_{p_{4},\ldots,p_{q}}\omega_{0}(d_{0,0}^{k_{2}}){\rm Ad}_{0}(d_{0,0,p_{4},\ldots,p_{n}}^{k_{1}}f_{\beta_{5}}^{(p_{5})}\ldots f_{\beta_{q}}^{(p_{q})})(P_{2}^{k_{2}}P_{3}P_{4}\ldots P_{p}(f))\right).

But
d_{0,0}^{k_{2}}
is not zero only if
p_{4}=0
and we infer

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{q}}^{(n)}(P_{\leq p}(f))=

=\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}P_{1}^{k_{1}}\left(\sum_{p_{5},\ldots,p_{q}}{\rm Ad}_{0}(d_{0,0,0,p_{5},\ldots,p_{n}}^{k_{1}}f_{\beta_{5}}^{(p_{5})}\ldots f_{\beta_{q}}^{(p_{q})})(P_{2}P_{3}\ldots P_{p}(f))\right).

Repeating the same arguments we arrive at

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{q}}^{(n)}(P_{\leq p}(f))=\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}\omega_{0}(d_{0,0,\ldots,0}^{k_{1}})P_{1}^{k_{1}}P_{2}\ldots P_{p}(f)=0

as
d_{0,0,\ldots,0}^{k_{1}}=0
for all
k_{1}
. This proves (
4.6.2
).

(
4.6.3
) is established in a similar way using (
4.6.25
), Lemma
4.6.4
, the first claim in Lemma
4.5.5
and formula (
4.6.12
).

Formulas (
4.6.4
) and (
4.6.5
) follow from the definition of
P_{\leq p}
, Lemma
4.5.3
, the first part of Lemma
4.5.5
and Lemma
4.6.4
.

∎

In conclusion, using the operators
P_{p}
and the elements
B_{p}
we define proper quantum analogues of monomials in functions
G_{p}
introduced in (
3.5.10
) (they were called
t_{i}(x)
in the introduction). They will play a crucial rule in establishing a quantum group version of the Skryabin equivalence in Section
5.2
and in the proof of the De–Concini–Kac–Procesi conjecture.

Proposition 4.6.7
.

Denote the class of
B_{c}^{n_{c}}
in the domain of definition of
P_{c-1}
by the same letter. Then for
n_{1},\ldots,n_{c},k_{1},\ldots,k_{c}\in\mathbb{N}
the elements

B_{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}}=P_{1}(P_{2}(\ldots P_{c-2}(P_{c-1}(B_{c}^{n_{c}})\otimes B_{c-1}^{n_{c-1}})\ldots)\otimes B_{2}^{n_{2}})\otimes B_{1}^{n_{1}}\in\mathbb{C}_{1}^{loc}[G]

satisfy

{\rm Ad}_{0}(f_{\beta_{1}}^{(k_{1})}\ldots f_{\beta_{c}}^{(k_{c})})(B_{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\prod_{p=1}^{c}q_{\beta_{p}}^{\frac{n_{p}(n_{p}-1)}{2}}~~{\rm if}~~n_{p}=k_{p}~~{\rm for}~~p=1,\ldots,c\\
0~~{\rm if}~~k_{i}=n_{i},i=1,\ldots,p-1~~{\rm and}~~k_{p}>n_{p}~~{\rm for~~some}~~p\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\end{array}\right.,

and hence

{\rm Ad}_{0}(f_{\beta_{1}}^{k_{1}}\ldots f_{\beta_{c}}^{k_{c}})(B_{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\prod_{p=1}^{c}q_{\beta_{p}}^{\frac{n_{p}(n_{p}-1)}{2}}[n_{p}]_{q_{\beta_{p}}}!~~{\rm if}~~n_{p}=k_{p}~~{\rm for}~~p=1,\ldots,d\\
0~~{\rm if}~~k_{i}=n_{i},i=1,\ldots,p-1~~{\rm and}~~k_{p}>n_{p}~~{\rm for~~some}~~p\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\end{array}\right..

In particular, the elements
B_{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}}
are linearly independent.

Proof.

The proof follows from Lemmas
4.5.3
,
4.5.5
,
4.6.4
,
4.6.6
, formula (
4.6.12
) and Proposition
4.6.1
. We shall prove the statement by induction.

First observe that by Proposition
4.6.1

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{1}}^{(k)}(P_{1}(P_{2}(\ldots P_{c-2}(P_{c-1}(B_{d}^{n_{c}})\otimes B_{c-1}^{n_{c-1}})\ldots)\otimes B_{2}^{k_{2}}))=0
for any
k>0
and recall that right multiplication by
B_{1}
gives rise to an operator on
\mathbb{C}_{1}^{loc}[G]
by Lemma
4.5.3
. Therefore from (
4.6.7
) for
p=1
we have

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{1}}^{(k_{1})}(B_{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}})=

=q_{\beta_{1}}^{\frac{k_{1}(k_{1}-1)}{2}}P_{\leq 1}(P_{2}(P_{3}(\ldots P_{c-2}(P_{c-1}(B_{c}^{n_{c}})\otimes B_{c-1}^{n_{c-1}})\ldots)\otimes B_{3}^{n_{3}})\otimes B_{2}^{n_{2}}).

if
k_{1}=n_{1}
and

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{1}}^{(k_{1})}(B_{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}})=0

if
k_{1}>n_{1}
.

Now assume that for some
p

{\rm Ad}_{0}(f_{\beta_{1}}^{(k_{1})}\ldots f_{\beta_{p}}^{(k_{p})})(B_{k_{1}\ldots k_{p}n_{p+1}\ldots n_{c}})=

(4.6.27)

=\prod_{m=1}^{p}q_{\beta_{m}}^{\frac{k_{m}(k_{m}-1)}{2}}P_{\leq p}(P_{p+1}(P_{p+2}(\ldots P_{c-2}(P_{c-1}(B_{d}^{n_{c}})\otimes B_{c-1}^{n_{c-1}})\ldots)\otimes B_{p+2}^{n_{p+2}})\otimes B_{p+1}^{n_{p+1}}).

Then by (
4.6.3
) and by the induction assumption

{\rm Ad}_{0}(f_{\beta_{1}}^{(k_{1})}\ldots f_{\beta_{p+1}}^{(k_{p+1})})(B_{k_{1}\ldots k_{p}n_{p+1}\ldots n_{c}})=

=\prod_{m=1}^{p}q_{\beta_{m}}^{\frac{k_{m}(k_{m}-1)}{2}}P_{\leq p}{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p+1}}^{(k_{p+1})}(P_{p+1}(P_{p+2}(\ldots P_{c-2}(P_{c-1}(B_{c}^{n_{c}})\otimes B_{c-1}^{n_{c-1}})\ldots)\otimes B_{p+2}^{n_{p+2}})\otimes B_{p+1}^{n_{p+1}}).

The last formula can be simplified using (
4.6.7
), the fact that

{\rm Ad}_{0}f_{\beta_{p+1}}^{(k)}P_{p+1}(P_{p+2}(\ldots P_{c-2}(P_{c-1}(B_{c}^{n_{c}})\otimes B_{c-1}^{n_{c-1}})\ldots)\otimes B_{p+2}^{n_{p+2}})=0

for any
k>0
, Lemma
4.6.4
and recalling that right multiplication by
B_{p+1}
gives rise to an operator on the image of
P_{p+1}
by Lemma
4.5.3
. This yields

{\rm Ad}_{0}(f_{\beta_{1}}^{(k_{1})}\ldots f_{\beta_{p+1}}^{(k_{p+1})})(B_{k_{1}\ldots k_{p}n_{p+1}\ldots n_{c}})=

=\prod_{m=1}^{p+1}q_{\beta_{m}}^{\frac{k_{m}(k_{m}-1)}{2}}P_{\leq p+1}(P_{p+2}(\ldots P_{c-2}(P_{c-1}(B_{d}^{n_{c}})\otimes B_{c-1}^{n_{c-1}})\ldots)\otimes B_{p+2}^{n_{p+2}})

if
k_{p+1}=n_{p+1}
and

{\rm Ad}_{0}(f_{\beta_{1}}^{(k_{1})}\ldots f_{\beta_{p+1}}^{(k_{p+1})})(B_{k_{1}\ldots k_{p}n_{p+1}\ldots n_{c}})=0

if
k_{p+1}>n_{p+1}
. This establishes the induction step and completes the proof of the proposition.

∎

4.7
A description of q-W–algebras in terms of Zhelobenko type operators

Now we are in a position to describe q-W–algebras in terms of the operator
P
introduced in the previous section. Recall that q-W–algebras are only defined when the value of the parameter
\kappa
is equal to one. Therefore in this section we always assume that
\kappa=1
. As a
\mathcal{B}
–module the q-W–algebra
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s}(G)
is the space of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]
–invariants in
Q_{\mathcal{B}}
with respect to the adjoint action. In order to use the operator
P
for the description of this space we shall transfer the results of Proposition
4.6.1
from
\mathbb{C}_{1}^{loc}[G]
to a localization
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}
of
Q_{\mathcal{B}}
using a natural extension of the
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]
–module homomorphism
\phi:\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]\rightarrow Q_{\mathcal{B}}
to a homomorphism
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]\rightarrow Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}
. Recall that according to Proposition
4.1.2

{\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}
belongs to the kernel of the homomorphism
\phi
, and as we shall see
{\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}
belongs to the kernel of the extension of
\phi
to
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]
. Therefore one can compose this extension with the operator
P
, and by Proposition
4.6.1
the image of this composition is invariant with respect to the natural extension of the
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]
–adjoint action to
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}
. The operator
\Pi
is a classical counterpart of this composition and using the description of
\Pi
given in Theorem
3.5.6
we shall show that the image of the composition is a localization of the algebra
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s}(G)
.

More precisely, formula (
4.1.15
) and the surjectivity of the map
\phi
imply that one can define a natural action of the algebra generated by the elements
q^{2P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\perp}}\lambda^{\vee}}\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G^{*}]
,
\lambda\in P_{+}
on
Q_{\mathcal{B}}
as follows

q^{2P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\perp}}\lambda^{\vee}}\phi(f)=\varphi({\rm Ad}_{0}(q^{-2P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\perp}}\lambda^{\vee}})(f))q^{2P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\perp}}\lambda^{\vee}}1=

(4.7.1)

={\rm Ad}(q^{-2P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\perp}}\lambda^{\vee}})(\varphi(f))q^{2P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\perp}}\lambda^{\vee}}1,

where the last identity follows from Proposition
3.2.5
.
Let
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}
be the localization of
Q_{\mathcal{B}}
by the elements
q^{2P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\perp}}\lambda^{\vee}}
,
\lambda\in P_{+}
.

Now consider the subalgebra
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G_{*}]\subset\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]
generated by
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]
and by the elements
q^{2P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\perp}}\lambda^{\vee}}
,
\lambda\in P
. Note that the adjoint action of these elements normalizes
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]
in
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]
as
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G_{*}]
is the direct sum of its weight components.
Therefore
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}
is the image of
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G_{*}]
under the natural projection
\rho_{\chi_{q}^{s}}:\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]/I_{\mathcal{B}}
, and hence the adjoint action of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]
on
Q_{\mathcal{B}}
naturally extends to
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}
.

Lemma 4.7.1
.

Let
\kappa=1
. Then

\phi
extends to a
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]
–module homomorphism
\phi:\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]\rightarrow Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}
,

\phi(f\otimes\Delta_{\lambda}^{-1})=q^{2(P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}\lambda^{\vee},\lambda^{\vee})-2P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\perp}}\lambda^{\vee}}\phi({\rm Ad}_{0}(q^{({1+s\over 1-s}s^{-1}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}-s^{-1})\lambda^{\vee}})(f)),f\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G],

(4.7.2)

and
{\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}
belongs to the kernel of this homomorphism, so

\phi:\mathbb{C}_{1}^{loc}[G]\rightarrow Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}.

Proof.

From formula (
4.1.15
) it follows that
\phi
extends to a
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]
–module homomorphism
\phi:\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]\rightarrow Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}

which is defined by (
4.7.2
), and by Proposition
4.1.2

{\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}
belongs to the kernel of this homomorphism.

∎

By (
4.5.10
) for
\lambda\in P_{+}

\Delta_{\lambda}
is
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]
–invariant with respect to the
{\rm Ad}_{0}
–action on
\mathbb{C}_{1}^{loc}[G]
, and hence
\phi(\Delta_{\lambda})=q^{2P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\perp}}\lambda^{\vee}}1
is
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]
–invariant with respect to the
{\rm Ad}
–action on
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}
. Thus by (
4.1.15
) and (
3.2.24
) we have for
\beta\in\Delta_{\mathfrak{m}_{+}}

\phi({\rm Ad}_{0}(\tilde{f}_{\beta})(f\otimes\Delta_{\lambda}))={\rm Ad}(\tilde{f}_{\beta})(\varphi({\rm Ad}_{0}(q^{-({1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+id)\lambda^{\vee}})(f)))q^{2P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\perp}}\lambda^{\vee}}1,

and

\phi({\rm Ad}_{0}(\tilde{f}_{\beta})(f\otimes\Delta_{\lambda}^{-1}))={\rm Ad}(\tilde{f}_{\beta})(\varphi({\rm Ad}_{0}(q^{({1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+id)\lambda^{\vee}})(f)))q^{-2P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\perp}}\lambda^{\vee}}1.

The last formula completely determines the adjoint action of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]
on
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}
.

Now we can describe q-W–algebras in terms of the operator
P
.

Theorem 4.7.2
.

Suppose that
\kappa=1
and for
i=1,\ldots l^{\prime}

k_{i}\in\mathcal{B}
are defined in (
3.2.11
). Then the composition
\phi P
gives rise to a well–defined operator

\Pi^{q}=\phi P:\mathbb{C}_{c+1}^{loc}[G]\rightarrow W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G),P=P_{1}\ldots P_{c}

(4.7.3)

where
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)={\rm Hom}_{{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]}(\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}},Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc})
.

Moreover, we have an imbedding of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]
–modules,
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}\subset\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]/I_{\mathcal{B}}
, multiplication in
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]
induces a multiplication in
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)
and we have an imbedding of algebras
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s}(G)\subset W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)
.

Assume furthermore that
k_{i}\neq 0
mod
(q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}-1)
for
i=1,\ldots l^{\prime}
.
Let
W_{q}^{s,loc}(G)=W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{C}(q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}})
,
{\rm Im}\Pi^{q}
the image of
\Pi^{q}
and denote
{\rm Im}_{q}\Pi^{q}={\rm Im}\Pi^{q}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{C}(q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}})
.

Then
W_{q}^{s,loc}(G)={\rm Im}_{q}\Pi^{q}
and
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)=W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)\cap{\rm Im}_{q}\Pi^{q}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.7.1
and by Proposition
4.6.1
the composition
\Pi^{q}=\phi P:\mathbb{C}_{c}^{loc}[G]\rightarrow W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)
is well–defined. All other claims of this theorem, except for the isomorphisms
W_{q}^{s,loc}(G)={\rm Im}_{q}\Pi^{q}
and
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)=W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)\cap{\rm Im}_{q}\Pi^{q}
follow from the definitions and are established similarly to Proposition
3.2.8
.

Using Lemma
4.3.1
and arguments similar to those in the last part of the proof of Lemma
4.3.2
one sees that in order to establish these isomorphisms it suffices to verify that the specialization of
\Pi^{q}
at
q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}=1
is surjective.

In order to do that we observe that by the definition and by Theorem
3.4.5

the specialization of
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}
at
q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}=1
is isomorphic to the localization
\mathbb{C}^{loc}[N_{-}Z{s}M_{-}]
of the algebra

\mathbb{C}[N_{-}Z{s}M_{-}]
by the classical counterparts of the elements
\Delta_{\lambda}
which we denote by the same symbols,
\Delta_{\lambda}=(v_{\lambda},\cdot s^{-1}v_{\lambda})
.

By (
4.5.10
) for
\lambda\in P_{+}

\Delta_{\lambda}
regarded as elements of
\mathbb{C}_{1}^{loc}[G]
are
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]
–invariant with respect to the
{\rm Ad}_{0}
–action on
\mathbb{C}_{1}^{loc}[G]
, and hence
\phi(\Delta_{\lambda})=q^{2P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\perp}}\lambda^{\vee}}1
is
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]
–invariant with respect to the
{\rm Ad}
–action on
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}
. Therefore their classical counterparts
\Delta_{\lambda}=(v_{\lambda},\cdot s^{-1}v_{\lambda})\in\mathbb{C}[N_{-}Z{s}M_{-}]
are
M_{-}
–invariant, and hence
\mathbb{C}^{loc}[N_{-}Z{s}M_{-}]^{M-}
is the localization of
\mathbb{C}[N_{-}Z{s}M_{-}]^{M-}
by the elements
\Delta_{\lambda}
,
\lambda\in P_{+}
.

This result and explicit formulas (
3.5.11
), (
3.5.12
), (
3.5.6
) for the operator
\Pi
, formulas (
4.6.1
) with
k=0
and the definition of the operator
P
imply that the specialization of the operator
\Pi^{q}
at
q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}=1
gives rise to a natural extension of the projection operator
\Pi:\mathbb{C}[N_{-}Z{s}M_{-}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[N_{-}Z{s}M_{-}]^{M_{-}}
to a projection operator
\Pi^{loc}:\mathbb{C}^{loc}[N_{-}Z{s}M_{-}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{loc}[N_{-}Z{s}M_{-}]^{M-}
given by

\Pi^{loc}(f\Delta_{\lambda}^{-1})=\Pi(f)\Delta_{\lambda}^{-1},f\in\mathbb{C}[N_{-}Z{s}M_{-}].

Since the operator
\Pi
is surjective,
\Pi^{loc}
is also surjective.

From the surjectivity of the specialization of the operator
\Pi^{q}
at
q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}=1
proved above it follows that
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)={\rm Im}\Pi^{q}
mod
(q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}-1)W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)
.
Note also that
{\rm Im}\Pi^{q}\subset W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)
are submodules of the
\mathcal{B}
–module
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}
, and
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}
is a
\mathcal{B}
–submodule of the
\mathcal{B}
–module
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]/I_{\mathcal{B}}
which is free over
\mathcal{B}
by Proposition
3.2.6
.
Since
\mathcal{B}
is a principal ideal domain
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}
is
\mathcal{B}
–free by Theorem 6.5 in
[
92
]
.

The properties mentioned in the previous paragraph and Lemma
4.3.1
imply that
W_{q}^{s,loc}(G)={\rm Im}_{q}\Pi^{q}
and
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)=W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)\cap{\rm Im}_{q}\Pi^{q}
. This completes the proof.

∎

4.8
Bibliographic comments

The results presented in this chapter are entirely new.

Commutation relations in the algebra
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
which appear in Section
4.2
can be found, for instance, in
[
12
]
, Theorem I.8.16.

The definition of the Zhelobenko type operators for q-W–algebras was inspired by the construction of extremal projection operators and of the Zhelobenko operators due to Zhelobenko. The definitions and the statements in this chapter are conceptually close to the definition and the properties of the Zhelobenko operators introduced and studied in
[
120
]
–
[
128
]
(see also
[
62
]
). Below, for the convenience of the reader who is familiar with these papers, we give references to similar statements from them. However, the results of
[
120
]
–
[
128
]
and
[
62
]
are not used in this book and not directly related to it.

For
k=0
the operators
P_{p}
are counterparts of the Zhelobenko operators
q_{\alpha}
introduced in
[
120
]
, §2 and §5, in
[
128
]
, Definition 5.2.1, and for
k>0
the operators
P_{p}^{k}
are counterparts of the operators
q^{(k)}_{\alpha,\mathfrak{m}}
defined in
[
62
]
, formula (4.9).

Properties of the Zhelobenko operators similar to those of the operators
P_{p}
mentioned in Proposition
4.6.1
can be found in
[
120
]
, §5, Proposition 1 (iii) and (iv),
[
128
]
, Proposition 5.2.4 (b) and (c),
[
62
]
, Lemma 4.5 (ii), (iv) and (v).

Zhelobenko operators
q_{w}
conceptually analogues to
P_{\leq p}
were defined in
[
120
]
, §5, Definition 1,
[
128
]
, Definition 5.2.4.

Properties (
4.6.11
) and (
4.6.12
) are counterparts of Proposition 3, parts (i) and (iii) in
[
120
]
, §2, and of properties (
\alpha
) and (
\beta
) in Section 5.2.4 in
[
128
]
.

Lemma
4.6.4
is analogous to a similar property for the Zhelobenko operators stated in
[
128
]
, Proposition and Corollary 5.2.3, and in
[
62
]
, Lemmas 4.3 and 4.5 (iii).

Formulas similar to (
4.6.25
) are used in the proof of Proposition 5.2.4 in
[
128
]
and in Proposition 4.4 in
[
62
]
.

Theorem
4.7.2
is analogous to Theorem 2 in

[
120
]
, §6 and to Theorem 5.5.1 in
[
128
]
for the Zhelobenko operators.

Chapter 5
Application of q-W–algebras to the description of the category of equivariant modules over a quantum group

In this rather short chapter we apply Proposition
4.6.7
to establish an equivalence between the category of finitely generated representations of a q-W–algebra and a category
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}]-{\rm mod}_{{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]}^{\chi_{\varepsilon}^{s}}
of equivariant modules over a quantum group. Categories of this kind were denoted
A-{\rm mod}_{B}^{\chi}
in the introduction.
The structure of modules from the category
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}]-{\rm mod}_{{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]}^{\chi_{\varepsilon}^{s}}
is similar to that of
\mathfrak{g}-K
–modules or of principal series representations over complex semisimple Lie algebras.

The proof of the main theorem of this chapter, Theorem
5.2.1
, is based on Proposition
4.6.7
.
In this framework one can give precise values of
\varepsilon
for which the categorical equivalence holds. Remarkably, with slight modifications this method is also applicable to the study of the structure of finite–dimensional representations over quantum groups at roots of unity. This will be done in the next chapter.

5.1
A category of equivariant modules over a quantum group

In this section we define a category of equivariant representations over a quantum group.

Suppose that
\kappa=1
and let
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{C}
. Fix a root
\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}
of
\varepsilon
of order
\frac{1}{dr^{2}}
. Let
U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{g})
,
U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
,
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}]
,
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
,
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[B_{+}]
,
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[G^{*}]
,
Q_{\varepsilon}^{loc}
,
W_{\varepsilon}^{s,loc}(G)
,
\chi_{\varepsilon}^{s}
,
\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon_{s}}
,
I_{\varepsilon}
,
\phi_{\varepsilon}
,
B_{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}}^{\varepsilon}
,
{\mathbb{C}}_{1}^{loc}[G]_{\varepsilon}
be the natural specializations at
q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}=\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}
of
U_{\mathcal{A}}^{s}(\mathfrak{g})
,
U_{\mathcal{A}}^{s}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
,
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G_{*}]
,
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[M_{+}]
,
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[B_{+}]
,
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]
,
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}
,
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)
,
\chi_{q}^{s}
,
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{s}}
,
I_{\mathcal{B}}
,
\phi
,
B_{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}}
,
{\mathbb{C}}_{1}^{loc}[G]
, respectively.

We shall always assume that
[n]_{\varepsilon_{\alpha_{i}}}\neq 0
and
\varepsilon^{2d_{i}}\neq 1
for
i=1,\ldots,l
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]=U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
=
U_{\varepsilon}^{s,res}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
,
U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{g})=U_{\varepsilon}^{s,res}(\mathfrak{g})
and
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[G^{*}]
is a subalgebra in
U_{\varepsilon}^{s,res}(\mathfrak{g})
as
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]
is a subalgebra in
U_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,res}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Let
J={\rm Ker}~\varepsilon_{s}|_{{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]}
be the augmentation ideal of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
related to the counit
\varepsilon_{s}
, and

\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon_{s}}
the trivial representation of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
given by the counit.
Let
V
be a finitely generated
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}]
–module which satisfies the following conditions:

1.

V
is a right
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
–module with respect to an action
{\rm Ad}
such that the action of the augmentation ideal
J
on
V
is locally nilpotent.

2.

The following compatibility condition holds for the two actions

{\rm Ad}x(yv)={\rm Ad}x^{1}(y){\rm Ad}x^{2}(v),~x\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}],~y\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}],~v\in V,

(5.1.1)

where
\Delta_{s}(x)=x^{1}\otimes x^{2}
,
{\rm Ad}x^{1}(y)
is the adjoint action of
x^{1}\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[B_{+}]
on
y\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}]
.

An element
v\in V
is called a Whittaker vector if

{\rm Ad}xv=\varepsilon_{s}(x)v
for any
x\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
.
The space

{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]}(\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon_{s}},V)={\rm Wh}(V).

(5.1.2)

is called the space of Whittaker vectors of
V
.

Consider the induced
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[G^{*}]
–module
W={\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[G^{*}]\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}]}V
. Using the adjoint action of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[G^{*}]
on itself one can naturally extend the adjoint action of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
from
V
to
W
in such a way that compatibility condition
(\ref{compat})
is satisfied for the natural action of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[G^{*}]
and the adjoint action
\rm Ad
of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
on
W
. As we observed in Section
3.2

\Delta_{s}({\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}])\subset{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[B_{+}]\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
.

We shall require that

3.

For any
x\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
the natural action of the element
(S_{s}\otimes\chi_{\varepsilon}^{s})\Delta_{s}(x)\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[G^{*}]
on
W
coincides with the adjoint action
{\rm Ad}x
of
x
on
W
.

As in the second part of the proof of Proposition
3.2.8
one can check that the last condition implies that
for any
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}]\cap I_{\varepsilon}
and
v\in{\rm Wh}(V)

zv=0
.

Denote by
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}]-{\rm mod}_{{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]}^{\chi_{\varepsilon}^{s}}
the category of finitely generated
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}]
–modules which satisfy conditions (1)–(3) above. Morphisms in the category
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}]-{\rm mod}_{{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]}^{\chi_{\varepsilon}^{s}}
are
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}]
- and
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
–module homomorphisms.
We call
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}]-{\rm mod}_{{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]}^{\chi_{\varepsilon}^{s}}
the category of
({\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}],\chi_{\varepsilon}^{s})
–equivariant modules over
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}]
.

Note that the algebra
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)
naturally acts in the space of Whittaker vectors for any object
V
of the category
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}]-{\rm mod}_{{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]}^{\chi_{\varepsilon}^{s}}
. Indeed, if
w,w^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}]
are two representatives of an element from
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)
then
w-w^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}]\cap I_{\varepsilon}
, and hence for any
v\in{\rm Wh}(V)

wv=w^{\prime}v
. Moreover, by the definition of the algebra
W_{\mathcal{B}}^{s,loc}(G)
and by condition (
5.1.1
) we have

{\rm Ad}x(wv)={\rm Ad}x^{1}(w){\rm Ad}x^{2}(v)={\rm Ad}x^{1}(w)\varepsilon_{s}(x^{2})v={\rm Ad}x(w)v=\varepsilon_{s}(x)wv.

Therefore
wv
is a Whittaker vector independent of the choice of the representative
w
.

For any
\mathbb{C}
–module
R
we denote by
{\rm hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]),R)
the subspace in
{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}],R)
which consists of the linear maps vanishing on some power of the augmentation ideal
J={\rm Ker}~\varepsilon_{s}
of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
,
{\rm hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}],R)=\{f\in{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}],R):f(J^{n})=0~{\rm for~some}~n>0\}
. Note that for every element
f
of
{\rm hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}],R)
one has
f(x)=0
if
x
does not belong to a finite–dimensional subspace of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
, and hence
{\rm hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}],R)={\rm hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}],\mathbb{C})\otimes R
.

Equip the space
{\rm hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}],R)
with the right action of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
induced by the multiplication in
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
from the left. To study the properties of this module we shall need a special filtration on the algebra
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]=U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
.

Recall that the algebra
U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{g})
can be equipped with the DeConcini–Kac filtration such that the associated graded algebra is almost commutative. For
{\bf r},~{\bf t}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{D}
define the height of the element

u_{{\bf r},{\bf t},t}=e^{\bf r}tf^{\bf t}
,
t\in U_{\varepsilon}^{s}({\mathfrak{h}})
as follows
{\rm ht}~(u_{{\bf r},{\bf t},t})=\sum_{i=1}^{D}(t_{i}+r_{i}){\rm ht}~\beta_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
, where
{\rm ht}~\beta_{i}
is the height of the root
\beta_{i}
. Introduce also the degree of
u_{{\bf r},{\bf t},t}
by

d(u_{{\bf r},{\bf t},t})=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{D},t_{D},\ldots,t_{1},{\rm ht}~(u_{{\bf r},{\bf t},t}))\in\mathbb{N}^{2D+1}.

By Lemma
2.7.2
the elements
e^{\bf r}tf^{\bf t}
span
U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{g})
as a linear space.

Equip
\mathbb{N}^{2D+1}
with the total lexicographic order and denote by
(U_{q}^{s}(\mathfrak{g}))_{k}
the span of elements
u_{{\bf r},{\bf t},t}
with
d(u_{{\bf r},{\bf t},t})\leq k
in
U_{q}^{s}(\mathfrak{g})
. Then Proposition 1.7 in
[
23
]
implies that
(U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{g}))_{k}
is a filtration of
U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{g})
such that the associated graded algebra is the complex associative algebra with generators
e_{\alpha},~f_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\Delta_{+}
,
t_{i}^{\pm 1}
,
i=1,\ldots l
subject to the relations

\begin{array}[]{l}t_{i}t_{j}=t_{j}t_{i},~~t_{i}t_{i}^{-1}=t_{i}^{-1}t_{i}=1,~~t_{i}e_{\alpha}t_{i}^{-1}=\varepsilon^{\frac{H_{i}(\alpha)}{d}}e_{\alpha},~~t_{i}f_{\alpha}t_{i}^{-1}=\varepsilon^{-\frac{H_{i}(\alpha)}{d}}f_{j},\\
\\
e_{\alpha}f_{\beta}=\varepsilon^{({1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}^{*}}\alpha,\beta)}f_{\beta}e_{\alpha},\\
\\
e_{\alpha}e_{\beta}=\varepsilon^{(\alpha,\beta)+({1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}^{*}}\alpha,\beta)}e_{\beta}e_{\alpha},~\alpha<\beta,\\
\\
f_{\alpha}f_{\beta}=\varepsilon^{(\alpha,\beta)+({1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}^{*}}\alpha,\beta)}f_{\beta}f_{\alpha},~\alpha<\beta.\end{array}

(5.1.3)

Such algebras are called semi–commutative.

Lemma 5.1.1
.

Let
J={\rm Ker}~\varepsilon_{s}
be the augmentation ideal of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
,
R
a
\mathbb{C}
–module,
{\rm hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}],R)=\{f\in{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}],R):f(J^{p})=0~{\rm for~some}~p>0\}
. Equip
{\rm hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}],R)
with the right action of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
induced by multiplication on
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
from the left. Then the
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
–module
{\rm hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}],R)
is injective.

Proof.

First observe that the algebra
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]\simeq U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
is Notherian and ideal
J
satisfies the so-called weak Artin–Rees property, i.e. for every finitely generated left
U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
–module
M
and its submodule
N
there exists an integer
n>0
such that
J^{n}M\cap N\subset JN
.

Indeed, observe that the algebra
U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
can be equipped with a filtration induced by the De Concini–Kac filtration on the algebra
U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{g})
in such a way that the associated graded algebra is finitely generated and semi–commutative (see (
5.1.3
)).
The fact that
U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
is Notherian follows from the existence of the filtration on it for which the associated graded algebra is semi–commutative and from Theorem 4 in Ch. 5, §3 in
[
53
]
(compare also with Theorem 4.8 in
[
82
]
). The ideal
J
satisfies the weak Artin–Rees property because the subring
U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})+Jt+J^{2}t^{2}+\ldots\subset U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})[t]
, where
t
is a central indeterminate, is Notherian (see
[
84
]
, Ch. 11, §2, Lemma 2.1). The last fact follows from the existence of a filtration on
U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})+Jt+J^{2}t^{2}+\ldots
induced by the filtration on
U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
for which the associated graded algebra is semi–commutative and again from Theorem 4 in Ch. 5, §3 in
[
53
]
.

Finally, the module
{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}],R)
is obviously injective. By Lemma 3.2 in Ch. 3,
[
47
]
the module
{\rm hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}],R)=\{f\in{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}],R):f(J^{p})=0~{\rm for~some}~p>0\}
is also injective since the ideal
J
satisfies the weak Artin–Rees property.

∎

5.2
Skryabin equivalence for equivariant modules over a quantum group

Now we can formulate the main theorem on the structure of the category
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}]-{\rm mod}_{{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]}^{\chi^{s}_{\varepsilon}}
and on the properties of its objects.

Theorem 5.2.1
.

If
[n]_{\varepsilon_{\alpha_{i}}}\neq 0
and
\varepsilon^{2d_{i}}\neq 1
for
i=1,\ldots,l
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
then the functor
E\mapsto Q_{\varepsilon}^{loc}\otimes_{W_{\varepsilon}^{s,loc}(G)}E
, is an equivalence of the category of finitely generated left
W_{\varepsilon}^{s,loc}(G)
–modules and of the category
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}]-{\rm mod}_{{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]}^{\chi^{s}_{\varepsilon}}
. The inverse equivalence is given by the functor
V\mapsto{\rm Wh}(V)
. In particular, the latter functor is exact.

Every module
V\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}]-{\rm mod}_{{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]}^{\chi^{s}_{\varepsilon}}
is isomorphic to
{\rm hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}],\mathbb{C})\otimes{\rm Wh}(V)
as a right
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
–module, where
{\rm hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}],\mathbb{C})
is equipped with the right action of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
induced by the multiplication in
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
from the left.
Q_{\varepsilon}^{loc}
is isomorphic to
{\rm hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}],\mathbb{C})\otimes{W_{\varepsilon}^{s,loc}(G)}
as a
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
–
W_{\varepsilon}^{s,loc}(G)
–bimodule, where the right
W_{\varepsilon}^{s,loc}(G)
–action is induced by the multiplication in
W_{\varepsilon}^{s,loc}(G)
from the right. In particular,
V
is
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[M_{+}]
