. Therefore every element
s^{-1}h_{0}\bar{n}_{s^{-1}}\in s^{-1}H_{0}\overline{N}_{s^{-1}}
can be represented as follows
s^{-1}h_{0}\bar{n}_{s^{-1}}=s^{-1}h_{0}\bar{n}_{s^{-1}}^{+}\bar{n}_{s^{-1}}^{-}
, and conjugating by
\bar{n}_{s^{-1}}^{-}
we obtain that
s^{-1}h_{0}\bar{n}_{s^{-1}}
is conjugate to
\bar{n}_{s^{-1}}^{-}s^{-1}h_{0}\bar{n}_{s^{-1}}^{+}
.

Since the decomposition
s=s^{1}s^{2}
is reduced

s(\overline{N}_{s^{-1}}\cap N_{-})s^{-1}=s(\overline{N}\cap N_{-})s^{-1}\subset M_{+}.

Taking into account that
H_{0}
normalizes
M_{+}
we have
\bar{n}_{s^{-1}}^{-}s^{-1}h_{0}\bar{n}_{s^{-1}}^{+}=s^{-1}h_{0}h_{0}^{-1}s\bar{n}_{s^{-1}}^{-}s^{-1}h_{0}\bar{n}_{s^{-1}}^{+}=s^{-1}h_{0}m_{s}
,
m_{s}=h_{0}^{-1}s\bar{n}_{s^{-1}}^{-}s^{-1}h_{0}\bar{n}_{s^{-1}}^{+}\in M_{+}
. We deduce that all conjugacy classes in the stratum
G_{\mathcal{C}}
intersect the variety
s^{-1}H_{0}M_{+}
.

Recall that by (
3.4.25
) with the roles of
N_{+}
and
N_{-}
swapped we have
v=n^{\prime}sm
for some
n^{\prime}\in N,m\in M_{+}
, where

v\in G
is an element of the form

v=\prod_{i=1}^{l^{\prime}}exp[t_{i}X_{-\gamma_{i}}],

t_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
are non–zero constants depending on the choice of the representative
s\in G
and the product over roots is taken in the normal order (
1.6.3
) associated to
s
. We deduce that

s^{-1}=mv^{-1}n^{\prime}
.

Now let
s^{-1}h_{0}m_{s},h_{0}\in H_{0},m_{s}\in M_{+}
be an element of
s^{-1}H_{0}M_{+}
. Using the previous expression for
s^{-1}
we can write
s^{-1}h_{0}m_{s}=mu^{-1}n^{\prime}h_{0}m_{s}
. Conjugating this element by
m^{-1}
and recalling that
H_{0}
normalizes
N_{+}
we infer that
s^{-1}h_{0}m_{s}
is conjugate to

v^{-1}n^{\prime}h_{0}m_{s}m=v^{-1}h_{0}n=\lambda_{0}(h_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}n,h_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}v),

where
n=h_{0}^{-1}n^{\prime}m_{s}m\in N_{+}
,
h_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\in H_{0}
is any element such that
h_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}h_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}=h_{0}
,
\lambda_{0}
is defined immediately after Proposition
6.1.4
. We deduce that all conjugacy classes in the stratum
G_{\mathcal{C}}
intersect the variety
v^{-1}H_{0}N_{+}
.

By part (iv) of Proposition
6.1.5
we conclude that if
\eta\in{\rm Spec}(Z_{0})
satisfies
\pi\eta\in G_{\mathcal{C}}
then there is a quantum coadjoint transformation
\widetilde{g}
such that
\widetilde{\pi}(\widetilde{g}\eta)=(h_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}n,h_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}v)
for some
n\in N_{+}
,
h_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\in H_{0}
.

Denote
\xi=\widetilde{g}\eta
. From the definition of the map
\widetilde{\pi}
and of the element
v
it follows that

\exp(\xi(x_{\beta_{D}}^{-})X_{-\beta_{D}})\exp(\xi(x_{\beta_{D-1}}^{-})X_{-\beta_{D-1}})\ldots\exp(\xi(x_{\beta_{1}}^{-})X_{-\beta_{1}})=v^{-1}

which implies
\xi((X_{\gamma_{i}}^{-})^{m})=-\frac{t_{i}}{(\varepsilon_{\gamma_{i}}-\varepsilon_{\gamma_{i}}^{-1})^{m}}\neq 0
for
i=1,\ldots,l^{\prime}
and that
\xi((X_{\beta}^{-})^{m})=0
for
\beta\in\Delta_{\mathfrak{m}_{+}}
,
\beta\not\in\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{l^{\prime}}\}
.

By the definition of the elements
f_{\beta}
with
\beta=\sum_{i=1}^{l}m_{i}\alpha_{i}
we have
f_{\beta}=\prod_{i,j=1}^{l}L_{j}^{m_{i}n_{ij}}X_{\beta}^{-}
. Therefore the commutation relations between elements
L_{j}
and
X_{\beta}^{-}
imply that
f_{\beta}^{m}=c_{\beta}\prod_{i,j=1}^{l}L_{j}^{mm_{i}n_{ij}}(X_{\beta}^{-})^{m}
, where
c_{\beta}
are non–zero constants, and hence
\xi(f_{\beta}^{m})=c_{\beta}\prod_{i,j=1}^{l}\xi(L_{j}^{m})^{m_{i}n_{ij}}\xi((X_{\beta}^{-})^{m})
. Since
\xi(L_{j})\neq 0
for
j=1,\ldots,l
,
\xi((X_{\gamma_{i}}^{-})^{m})=-\frac{t_{i}}{(\varepsilon_{\gamma_{i}}-\varepsilon_{\gamma_{i}}^{-1})^{m}}\neq 0
for
i=1,\ldots,l^{\prime}
and
\xi((X_{\beta}^{-})^{m})=0
for
\beta\in\Delta_{\mathfrak{m}_{+}}
,
\beta\not\in\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{l^{\prime}}\}
we deduce

\xi(f_{\gamma_{i}}^{m})=a_{i}\neq 0
for
i=1,\ldots,l^{\prime}
and
\xi(f_{\beta}^{m})=0
for
\beta\in\Delta_{\mathfrak{m}_{+}}
,
\beta\not\in\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{l^{\prime}}\}
. Thus
\xi
satisfies the condition of Propositions
6.2.2
and
6.2.4
. Let
U_{\widetilde{g}\eta}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})=U_{\xi}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})
be the corresponding subalgebra in
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Note that by Theorem
1.5.2
for any
g\in G_{\mathcal{C}}
we have

{\rm dim}~Z_{G}(g)={\rm dim}~\overline{\Sigma}_{s^{-1}},

where
Z_{G}(g)
is the centralizer of
g
in
G
.

By the definition of
\overline{\Sigma}_{s^{-1}}
we also have
{\rm dim}~\overline{\Sigma}_{s^{-1}}=l(s)+2D_{0}+{\rm dim}~{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}^{\perp}
.
Observe also that
{\rm dim}~G=2D+{\rm dim}~\mathfrak{h}
and
{\rm dim}~\mathfrak{h}-{\rm dim}~{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}^{\perp}={\rm dim}~{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}=l^{\prime}
,
and hence from (
1.6.5
) we deduce that
{\rm dim}~\mathfrak{m}_{-}=D-D_{0}-\frac{1}{2}(l(s)-l^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}({\rm dim}~G-{\rm dim}~\overline{\Sigma}_{s^{-1}})=\frac{1}{2}{\rm dim}~\mathcal{O}_{g}
and
{\rm dim}~U_{\eta}^{\widetilde{g}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})={\rm dim}~U_{\widetilde{g}\eta}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})=m^{{\rm dim}~\mathfrak{m}_{-}}=m^{\frac{1}{2}{\rm dim}~\mathcal{O}_{g}}
, where
\mathcal{O}_{g}
is the conjugacy class of any
g\in G_{\mathcal{C}}
.

In particular,
{\rm dim}~U_{\eta}^{\widetilde{g}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})=m^{\frac{1}{2}{\rm dim}~\mathcal{O}_{\pi\eta}}
, where
\mathcal{O}_{\pi\eta}
is the conjugacy class of
\pi\eta\in G_{\mathcal{C}}
.

The remaining statements of this proposition are consequences of Proposition
6.2.4
.

∎

6.3
Skryabin equivalence for quantum groups at roots of unity and the proof of De Concini–Kac–Procesi conjecture

In this section we shall study the
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
–action on finite–dimensional
U_{\eta}(\mathfrak{g})
–modules introduced in the previous section. We shall show that each such module is
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
–cofree. Taking into account that
{\rm dim}~U_{\eta_{1}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})=m^{\frac{1}{2}{\rm dim}~\mathcal{O}_{\pi\eta}}
this will imply the De Concini–Kac–Procesi conjecture.

The main observation with the help of which we shall prove these statements is that the structure of
U_{\eta}(\mathfrak{g})
–modules is similar to that of a root of unity analogue
Q_{\chi}
of the module
Q_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}
, and the results of Propositions
4.6.1
and
4.6.7
can be specialized to
q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}=\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}
and transferred to
Q_{\chi}
and, more generally, to any finite–dimensional
U_{\eta}(\mathfrak{g})
–module using root of unity analogues of the homomorphism
\phi
. In particular, using images of the specializations
B^{\varepsilon}_{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}}
of the elements
B_{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}}
in finite–dimensional
U_{\eta}(\mathfrak{g})
–modules one can construct their
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
–cofree bases.

To realize this program for any given
\eta\in{\rm Spec}(Z_{0})
we assume that
\Delta_{+}^{s}
is the system of positive roots defined for
s
in Theorem
1.5.2
,
\Delta_{+}
the corresponding system of positive roots associated to
s
,
d=2d^{\prime}
, where
d^{\prime}
is defined in Proposition
6.1.1
. Assume that
m
and
d
are coprime.
Fix a quantum coadjoint transformation
\widetilde{g}\in\mathcal{G}
as in Theorem
6.2.6
and denote
\xi=\widetilde{g}\eta\in{\rm Spec}(Z_{0})
. Since according to (
6.1.13
)
\widetilde{g}
gives rise to an isomorphism of the algebras
U_{\eta}(\mathfrak{g})
and
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
it suffices to consider the case of the algebra
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Our first objective is to obtain root of unity analogues of Proposition
4.1.2
and Lemma
4.7.1
. We start by introducing the notions required for the formulations of these statements. For indeterminate
q
these notions were introduced in Section
4.1
.

Let
\chi
be a character of
U_{\xi}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})
defined in Proposition
6.2.2
,
\mathbb{C}_{\chi}
the corresponding representation of
U_{\xi}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})
. Denote by
Q_{\chi}
the induced left
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
–module,
Q_{\chi}=U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes_{U_{\xi}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})}\mathbb{C}_{\chi}
.
Q_{\chi}
can also be naturally regarded as a
U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{g})
–module via the natural projection
U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{g})=U_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Let
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G]
,
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G_{*}]
,
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[G^{*}]
,
B_{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}}^{\varepsilon}
,
{\mathbb{C}}_{1}^{loc}[G]_{\varepsilon}
be the natural specializations at
q^{\frac{1}{rd^{2}}}=\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{rd^{2}}}
of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]
,
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G_{*}]
,
{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathcal{B}}[G^{*}]
,
B_{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}}
,
{\mathbb{C}}_{1}^{loc}[G]
, respectively.

Define the twisted adjoint action of
U_{\varepsilon}^{s,res}(\mathfrak{g})
on
\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon}[G]
by

({\rm Ad}_{0}xf)(w)=f(\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1}({\rm Ad}^{\prime}x(S_{s}\omega_{0}w)))=f((\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})(x^{1})w\omega_{0}(x^{2})),f\in\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon}[G],x,w\in U_{\varepsilon}^{s,res}(\mathfrak{g}).

(6.3.1)

Specializing isomorphism (
3.2.19
) at
q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}=\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}
and twisting it by
\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1}
we obtain a
U_{\varepsilon}^{s,res}(\mathfrak{g})
–module homomorphism

\varphi_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon}[G]\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[{G}_{*}],f\mapsto(id\otimes f)(id\otimes\omega_{0}S_{s}^{-1})(\mathcal{R}^{s}_{21}\mathcal{R}^{s}).

(6.3.2)

Observe that the subalgebra in
U_{\varepsilon}^{s,res}(\mathfrak{g})
generated by
\tilde{f}_{\beta}
,
\beta\in\Delta_{\mathfrak{m}_{+}}
is isomorphic to
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
.
Therefore composing homomorphism (
6.3.2
) with the natural projection
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[{G}_{*}]\subset U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow Q_{\chi}
we obtain
a homomorphism of
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
–modules

\phi_{\xi}:\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon}[G]\rightarrow Q_{\chi},\phi_{\xi}(f)=\varphi_{\varepsilon}(f)1,

(6.3.3)

where
\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon}[G]
is equipped with the restriction of action (
6.3.1
) to
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
and
Q_{\chi}
with the action induced by the adjoint action
\rm Ad
of
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
and
1
is the image of
1\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[G_{*}]
in
Q_{\xi}
under the map
{\mathbb{C}}_{\varepsilon}[G_{*}]\rightarrow Q_{\xi}
.

Similarly, for any finite–dimensional
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
–module
V
and any
w\in V_{\chi}
one can define a
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
–module homomorphism
\phi_{\xi}^{w}:\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon}[G]\rightarrow V
by

\phi_{\xi}^{w}:\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon}[G]\rightarrow V,\phi_{\xi}(f)=\varphi_{\varepsilon}(f)w.

Proposition 6.3.1
.

The specialization
{\overline{I}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\subset\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon}[G]
of the left ideal
{\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}\subset\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
at
q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}=\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}
lies in the kernel of
\phi_{\xi}^{w}
.

Moreover, if
u
is a highest weight vector in the specialization at
q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}=\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}
of a finite rank indecomposable representation
V_{\lambda}
of
U_{\mathcal{B}}^{res}(\mathfrak{g})
of highest weight
\lambda
and such that
(u,u)=1
then for any
f\in\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon}[G]

\phi_{\xi}^{w}(f\otimes(u,\cdot T_{s}^{-1}u))=\varphi_{\varepsilon}({\rm Ad}_{0}(\varepsilon^{-(nd{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+id)\lambda^{\vee}})(f))\phi_{\xi}^{w}((u,\cdot T_{s}^{-1}u)),

(6.3.4)

where
{\rm Ad}_{0}(\varepsilon^{-(nd{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+id)\lambda^{\vee}})(f)
is the adjoint action of the element

\varepsilon^{-(nd{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+id)\lambda^{\vee}}=q^{-(nd{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+id)\lambda^{\vee}}~~({\rm mod}~(q^{\frac{1}{r^{2}d}}-\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{r^{2}d}})),\varepsilon^{-(nd{1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+id)\lambda^{\vee}}\in\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon}[G^{*}]

on
f\in\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon}[G]
.

One can define an action of an operator
\varepsilon^{(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}
on the image of
\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon}[G]
in
V
by the formula

\varepsilon^{(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}\phi_{\xi}^{w}(f)=\varphi_{\varepsilon}({\rm Ad}_{0}(\varepsilon^{-(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}})(f))\phi_{\xi}^{w}((u,\cdot T_{s}^{-1}u)),

(6.3.5)

where
{\rm Ad}_{0}(\varepsilon^{-(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}})(f)
is the adjoint action of the element

\varepsilon^{-(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}=q^{-(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}~~({\rm mod}~(q^{\frac{1}{r^{2}d}}-\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{r^{2}d}})),\varepsilon^{-(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}\in\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon}[G^{*}]

on
f\in\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon}[G]
.

Using this operator formula (
6.3.4
) can be rewritten as follows

\phi_{\xi}^{w}(f\otimes(u,\cdot T_{s}^{-1}u))=\varepsilon^{(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}\phi_{\xi}^{w}({\rm Ad}_{0}(\varepsilon^{(-nd{1+s\over 1-s}s^{-1}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}+s^{-1})\lambda^{\vee}})(f)).

(6.3.6)

The operator
\varepsilon^{(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}
is invertible. More precisely, for some
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
the action of
(\varepsilon^{(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}})^{n_{0}}
coincides with the action of the element

\varepsilon^{n_{0}(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}=q^{n_{0}(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}~~({\rm mod}~(q^{\frac{1}{r^{2}d}}-\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{r^{2}d}})),\varepsilon^{n_{0}(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}\in U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{h}),

and hence
(\varepsilon^{(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}})^{n_{0}m}=\xi(\varepsilon^{n_{0}m(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}})=c_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
.

\phi_{\xi}^{w}
extends to a
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
–module homomorphism
\phi_{\xi}^{w}:\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G]\rightarrow V
,

\phi_{\xi}^{w}(f\otimes\Delta_{\lambda}^{-1})=\varepsilon^{({(1-nd)s^{-1}+(1+nd)s-2\over 1-s}P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}\lambda^{\vee},\lambda^{\vee})}\left(\varepsilon^{(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}\right)^{-1}\phi_{\xi}^{w}({\rm Ad}_{0}(\varepsilon^{(nd{1+s\over 1-s}s^{-1}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}}-s^{-1})\lambda^{\vee}})(f)),

(6.3.7)

and the specialization
{\overline{I}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{loc}\subset\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G]
of
{\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}\subset\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]
at
q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}=\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}
belongs to the kernel of this homomorphism, so

\phi_{\xi}^{w}:\mathbb{C}_{1}^{loc}[G]_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow V.

Proof.

In order to prove this proposition one can apply Proposition
4.1.1
for
\kappa=nd
and
q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}
specialized to
\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}
, and the appropriately modified arguments before Proposition
4.1.2
and from the proof of Lemma
4.7.1
.

Indeed, by the definition of
I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}
with
\kappa=nd
,
k_{i}=c_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,l^{\prime}
for
c_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,l^{\prime}
used in the definition of
\chi
, the specialization of
I_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf k}
at
q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}=\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}
belongs to the annihilator of
w
, and hence by Proposition
4.1.1
the specialization
{\overline{I}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\subset\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon}[G]
of the left ideal
{\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}\subset\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}[G]
at
q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}=\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}
lies in the kernel of
\phi_{\xi}^{w}
.

Formulas (
6.3.4
), (
6.3.5
) and (
6.3.6
) are obtained by specializing the corresponding formulas in Proposition
4.1.1
at
q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}=\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}
with
\kappa=nd
.

The only essential difference is that the operator
\varepsilon^{(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}
is invertible and for some
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
its action coincides with the action of the element

\varepsilon^{n_{0}(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}\in U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{h})
.
This can be justified as follows.

Recall that elements
\gamma_{i}^{\vee}
,
i=1,\ldots,l^{\prime}
form a linear basis of
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}
. Let
\gamma_{i}^{*}
,
i=1,\ldots,l^{\prime}
be the basis of
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}
dual to
\gamma_{i}^{\vee}
,
i=1,\ldots,l^{\prime}
with respect to the restriction of the bilinear form
(\cdot,\cdot)
to
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}
. Since the numbers
(\gamma_{i}^{\vee},\gamma_{j}^{\vee})
are integer each element
\gamma_{i}^{*}
has the form
\gamma_{i}^{*}=\sum_{j=1}^{l^{\prime}}m_{ij}\gamma_{j}^{\vee}
, where
m_{ij}\in\mathbb{Q}
. Therefore
P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}\lambda^{\vee}=\sum_{p=1}^{l^{\prime}}(\lambda^{\vee},\gamma_{p}^{\vee})\gamma_{p}^{*}=\sum_{p,q=1}^{l^{\prime}}(\lambda^{\vee},\gamma_{p}^{\vee})m_{pq}\gamma_{q}^{\vee}
belongs to the rational span of the set of simple coweights
Y_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,l
, and hence
(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}
belongs to the rational span of the set of simple coweights
Y_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,l
as well. We conclude that there exists an integer
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
n_{0}(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}
belongs to the integer span of the set of simple coweights
Y_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,l
, and
q^{n_{0}(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}\in U_{\mathcal{B}}^{s}(\mathfrak{h})
.
So if we define
\varepsilon^{n_{0}(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}=q^{n_{0}(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}
mod
(q^{\frac{1}{r^{2}d}}-\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{r^{2}d}})
then
\varepsilon^{n_{0}(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}\in U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{h})
.

Now using (
6.3.4
) and (
6.3.5
) one immediately verifies that

(\varepsilon^{(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}})^{n_{0}}\phi_{\xi}^{w}(f)=\varphi_{\varepsilon}({\rm Ad}_{0}(\varepsilon^{-n_{0}(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}})(f))\phi_{\xi}^{w}((u,\cdot T_{s}^{-1}u)^{n_{0}})

and that

\phi_{\xi}^{w}((u,\cdot T_{s}^{-1}u)^{n_{0}})=\varepsilon^{n_{0}(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}w.

Recalling the equivariance of
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
with respect to the action of
U_{\varepsilon}^{s,res}(\mathfrak{g})\supset U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{h})
we obtain from the last two identities that

(\varepsilon^{(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}})^{n_{0}}\phi_{\xi}^{w}(f)=

=\varepsilon^{n_{0}(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(f)\varepsilon^{-n_{0}(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}\varepsilon^{n_{0}(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}w=

=\varepsilon^{n_{0}(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(f)w=\varepsilon^{n_{0}(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}\phi_{\xi}^{w}(f).

Finally applying verbatim the arguments from the proof of Lemma
4.7.1
we deduce that
\phi_{\xi}^{w}
extends to a
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
–module homomorphism
\phi_{\xi}^{w}:\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G]\rightarrow V
in such a way that (
6.3.7
) holds, and the specialization
{\overline{I}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{loc}\subset\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon}^{loc}[G]
of
{\overline{I}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{loc}\subset\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{B}}^{loc}[G]
at
q^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}=\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{dr^{2}}}
belongs to the kernel of this homomorphism, so

\phi_{\xi}^{w}:\mathbb{C}_{1}^{loc}[G]_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow V.

Note that due to invertibility of the operator
\varepsilon^{(id+s^{-1})(id-ndP_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}})\lambda^{\vee}}
no localization of
V
, which appears in Lemma
4.7.1
for
Q_{\mathcal{B}}
in the case of indeterminate
q
, is required.

∎

Now we define root of unity counterparts of q-W–algebras.
Let
W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)={\rm End}_{U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})}(Q_{\chi})^{opp}
be the algebra of
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
–endomorphisms of
Q_{\chi}
with the opposite multiplication. The algebra
W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)
is also called a q-W–algebra associated to
s\in W
and to
\xi\in{\rm Spec}(Z_{0})
. Denote by
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})-{\rm mod}
the category of finite–dimensional left
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
–modules and by
W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)-{\rm mod}
the category of finite–dimensional left
W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)
–modules. Observe that if
V\in U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})-{\rm mod}
then the algebra
W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)
naturally acts on the finite–dimensional space
V_{\chi}={\rm Hom}_{U_{\xi}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})}(\mathbb{C}_{\chi},V)={\rm Hom}_{U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})}(Q_{\chi},V)
by compositions of homomorphisms.

The following theorem is a root of unity analogue of the Skryabin equivalence for equivariant modules over quantum groups.
This theorem uncovers some striking similarity between the structure of the category of finite–dimensional representations of
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
and of the category of equivariant modules over a quantum group for generic
\varepsilon
.

Theorem 6.3.2
.

Every module
V\in U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})-{\rm mod}
is isomorphic to
{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-}),V_{\chi})
as a right
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
–module, where the right action of
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
on
{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-}),V_{\chi})
is induced by the multiplication in
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
from the left.
Q_{\chi}
is isomorphic to
{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-}))\otimes W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)
as a
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
–
W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)
–bimodule. In particular,
V
is
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
–injective,
{\rm Ext}^{\bullet}_{U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})}(\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon},V)=V_{\chi}
and the dimension of
V
is divisible by
{\rm dim}~U_{\xi}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})=m^{\frac{1}{2}{\rm dim}~\mathcal{O}_{\pi\xi}}
.

The functor
E\mapsto Q_{\chi}\otimes_{W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)}E
establishes an equivalence of the category of finite–dimensional left
W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)
–modules and the category
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})-{\rm mod}
. The inverse equivalence is given by the functor
V\mapsto V_{\chi}
. In particular, the latter functor is exact, and every finite–dimensional
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
–module is generated by Whittaker vectors.

Proof.

Let
V
be an object in the category
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})-{\rm mod}
.
Fix any linear map
\rho:V\rightarrow V_{\chi}
the restriction of which to
V_{\chi}
is the identity map, and let for any
v\in V

\sigma_{\varepsilon}(v):U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})\rightarrow V_{\chi}
be the
\mathbb{C}
–linear homomorphism given by
\sigma_{\varepsilon}(v)(x)=\rho({\rm Ad}x(v))
, and we have a map
\sigma_{\varepsilon}:V\rightarrow{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-}),V_{\chi})
.

By definition
\sigma_{\varepsilon}
is a homomorphism of right
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
–modules, where the right action of
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
on

{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-}),V_{\chi})

is induced by multiplication in
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
from the left.

We claim that
\sigma_{\varepsilon}
is an isomorphism. Firstly,
\sigma_{\varepsilon}
is injective for otherwise its kernel would contain a non–zero Whittaker vector. Indeed by Lemma
6.2.5
the augmentation ideal of
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
coincides with its Jacobson radical which is nilpotent. Therefore its action on the kernel of
\sigma_{\varepsilon}
is nilpotent, and hence the kernel, if it is non–trivial, must contain a non–zero Whittaker vector annihilated by the augmentation ideal of
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
. But all non–zero Whittaker vectors in
V
belong to
V_{\chi}
and by the definition of
\sigma_{\varepsilon}
their images in
{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-}),V_{\chi})
are non–zero homomorphisms non-vanishing at
1
.

Next we show that
\sigma_{\varepsilon}
is also surjective.
Indeed, for
n_{1},\ldots,n_{c}=1,\ldots m-1
and any
v\in V_{\chi}
the elements
v^{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}}=\prod_{p=1}^{c}\varepsilon_{\beta_{p}}^{-\frac{n_{p}(n_{p}-1)}{2}}\frac{1}{[n_{p}]_{\varepsilon_{\beta_{p}}}!}\phi_{\xi}^{v}(B_{n_{1}\ldots n_{d}}^{\varepsilon})
are well–defined and Proposition
4.6.7
for
k_{1},\ldots,k_{c}=1,\ldots m-1
implies

{\rm Ad}(f_{\beta_{1}}^{k_{1}}\ldots f_{\beta_{c}}^{k_{c}})v^{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}v~~{\rm if}~~n_{p}=k_{p}~~{\rm for}~~p=1,\ldots,c\\
0~~{\rm if}~~k_{i}=n_{i},i=1,\ldots,p-1~~{\rm and}~~k_{p}>n_{p}~~{\rm for~~some}~~p\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\end{array}\right.,

and hence

\sigma_{\varepsilon}(v^{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}})(f_{\beta_{1}}^{k_{1}}\ldots f_{\beta_{c}}^{k_{c}})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}v~~{\rm if}~~n_{p}=k_{p}~~{\rm for}~~p=1,\ldots,c\\
0~~{\rm if}~~k_{i}=n_{i},i=1,\ldots,p-1~~{\rm and}~~k_{p}>n_{p}~~{\rm for~~some}~~p\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\end{array}\right..

(6.3.8)

Observe that the elements
f_{\beta_{1}}^{k_{1}}\ldots f_{\beta_{c}}^{k_{c}}
,
k_{1},\ldots,k_{c}=1,\ldots m-1
form a linear basis of
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
.
Elements of this basis are labeled by elements of the set
\mathbb{N}_{m}^{c}
, where
\mathbb{N}_{m}=\{0,1,\ldots,m-1\}
. Introduce the lexicographic order on this set, so that
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{c})>(n_{1},\ldots,n_{c})
if
k_{i}=n_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,p-1
and
k_{p}>n_{p}
for some
p\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
.

Note that for any
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{c})\in\mathbb{N}_{m}^{c}
the number of elements
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{c})\in\mathbb{N}^{c}
such that
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{c})>(n_{1},\ldots,n_{c})
is finite.

Now let
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{c})\in\mathbb{N}_{m}^{c}
,
v\in V_{\chi}
. If for
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{c})\in\mathbb{N}_{m}^{c}
such that
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{c})\geq(n_{1},\ldots,n_{c})
we denote

\sigma_{\varepsilon}(v_{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}}^{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}})=f_{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}},

where for
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{c})\geq(n_{1},\ldots,n_{c})

v_{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}}\in V_{\chi}
are defined by induction starting from
v_{k_{1}\ldots k_{c}}=v
as follows

v_{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}}=-\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{c})\geq(n_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,n_{c}^{\prime})>(n_{1},\ldots,n_{c})}f_{n_{1}^{\prime}\ldots n_{c}^{\prime}}(f_{\beta_{1}}^{n_{1}}\ldots f_{\beta_{c}}^{n_{c}}),

(6.3.9)

then using (
6.3.8
) one obtains

f_{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}}(f_{\beta_{1}}^{n_{1}^{\prime}}\ldots f_{\beta_{c}}^{n_{c}^{\prime}})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}v_{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}}~~{\rm if}~~(n_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,n_{c}^{\prime})=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{c}),\\
0~~{\rm if}~~(n_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,n_{c}^{\prime})>(n_{1},\ldots,n_{c})\end{array}\right..

From this property and from (
6.3.9
) one immediately checks that if we define

f^{k_{1}\ldots k_{c}}_{v}=\sum_{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{c})\geq(n_{1},\ldots,n_{c})}f_{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}}

then for any
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{c})\in\mathbb{N}_{m}^{c}

f^{k_{1}\ldots k_{c}}_{v}(f_{\beta_{1}}^{n_{1}}\ldots f_{\beta_{c}}^{n_{c}})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}v~~{\rm if}~~(n_{1},\ldots,n_{c})=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{c}),\\
0~~{\rm if}~~(n_{1},\ldots,n_{c})\neq(k_{1},\ldots,k_{c})\end{array}\right..

(6.3.10)

Since the elements
f_{\beta_{1}}^{k_{1}}\ldots f_{\beta_{c}}^{k_{c}}
,
k_{1},\ldots,k_{c}=1,\ldots m-1
form a linear basis of
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
(
6.3.10
) implies that the elements
f^{k_{1}\ldots k_{c}}_{v}
with
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{c})\in\mathbb{N}_{m}^{c}
,
v\in V_{\chi}
generate
{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-}),V_{\chi})
, and hence the elements
\sigma_{\varepsilon}(v^{n_{1}\ldots n_{c}})
with
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{c})\in\mathbb{N}_{m}^{c}
,
v\in V_{\chi}
generate
{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-}),V_{\chi})
as well. Therefore
\sigma_{\varepsilon}
is surjective, and the first part of the theorem is proved.

Similarly to the case of generic
\varepsilon
(see last part of the proof of Theorem
5.2.1
) one shows that
Q_{\chi}
is isomorphic to
{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-}))\otimes W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)
as a
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
–
W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)
–bimodule.

Let
E
be a finite–dimensional
W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)
–module. Using the isomorphism
Q_{\chi}\simeq{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-}))\otimes W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)
of
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
–
W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)
–bimodules and the linear space isomorphism
W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)={\rm End}_{U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})}(Q_{\chi})^{opp}={\rm Hom}_{U_{\xi}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})}(\mathbb{C}_{\chi},Q_{\chi})=(Q_{\chi})_{\chi}
, one immediately deduces similarly to the case of generic
\varepsilon
in the proof of Theorem
5.2.1
that

(Q_{\chi}\otimes_{W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)}E)_{\chi}=E
. Therefore to prove the second statement of the theorem it suffices to check that for any
V\in U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})-{\rm mod}
the canonical map
f:Q_{\chi}\otimes_{W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)}V_{\chi}\rightarrow V
is an isomorphism.

Indeed,
f
is injective because otherwise by Proposition
6.2.4
its kernel would contain a non–zero Whittaker vector with respect to
\chi
. But all Whittaker vectors of
Q_{\chi}\otimes_{W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)}V_{\chi}
belong to the subspace
1\otimes V_{\chi}
, and the restriction of
f
to
1\otimes V_{\chi}
induces an isomorphism of the spaces of Whittaker vectors of
Q_{\chi}\otimes_{W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)}V_{\chi}
and of
V
.

In order to prove that
f
is surjective we consider the exact sequence

0\rightarrow Q_{\chi}\otimes_{W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)}V_{\chi}\rightarrow V\rightarrow W\rightarrow 0,

where
W
is the cokernel of
f
, and the corresponding long exact sequence of cohomology,

0\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{0}_{U_{\eta_{1}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})}(\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon},Q_{\chi}\otimes_{W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)}V_{\chi})\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{0}_{U_{\eta_{1}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})}(\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon},V)\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{0}_{U_{\eta_{1}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})}(\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon},W)\rightarrow

\rightarrow{\rm Ext}^{1}_{U_{\eta_{1}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})}(\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon},Q_{\chi}\otimes_{W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)}V_{\chi})\rightarrow\ldots.

Now recall that
f
induces an isomorphism of the spaces of Whittaker vectors of
Q_{\chi}\otimes_{W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)}V_{\chi}
and of
V
. As we proved above the finite–dimensional
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
–module
Q_{\chi}\otimes_{W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)}V_{\chi}
is injective over
U_{\eta_{1}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})
, and hence
{\rm Ext}^{1}_{U_{\eta_{1}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})}(\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon},Q_{\chi}\otimes_{W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)}V_{\chi})=0
. Therefore the initial part of the long exact cohomology sequence takes the form

0\rightarrow V_{\chi}\rightarrow V_{\chi}\rightarrow W_{\chi}\rightarrow 0,

where the second map in the last sequence is an isomorphism. Using the last exact sequence we deduce that
W_{\chi}=0
. But if
W
were non–trivial it would contain a non–zero Whittaker vector by Proposition
6.2.4
. Thus
W=0
, and
f
is surjective. This completes the proof of the theorem.

∎

By the previous theorem every module
V\in U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})-{\rm mod}
is isomorphic to

{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-}),V_{\chi})={\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-}),\mathbb{C})\otimes V_{\chi}

as a right
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
–module. In fact, one can show that the algebra
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
is Frobenius, i.e. its left regular representation is isomorphic to the dual of the right regular representation and its right regular representation is isomorphic to the dual of the left regular representation. Thus as a right
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
–module
V
is isomorphic to
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})\otimes V_{\chi}
, where the right action of
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
on
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})\otimes V_{\chi}
is induced by the multiplication in
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
from the right. In particular,
V
is
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
–free.

More generally, we have the following proposition.

Proposition 6.3.3
.

For any character
\eta:Z_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
the algebra
U_{\eta}({\mathfrak{g}})
and its subalgebra
U_{\eta}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})
are Frobenius algebras.

Proof.

The proof of this proposition is parallel to the proof of a similar statement for Lie algebras over fields of prime characteristic (see Proposition 1.2 in
[
38
]
) and for the restricted form of the quantum group in
[
67
]
. We shall only briefly outline the main steps of the proof for
U_{\eta}({\mathfrak{g}})
. The proof for
U_{\eta}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})
is similar.

The key ingredient of the proof is the De Concini-Kac filtration on
U_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{g})\simeq U_{\varepsilon}^{s}(\mathfrak{g})
defined as in Section
5.1
.
For
{\bf r},~{\bf t}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{D}
introduce the element

u_{{\bf r},{\bf t},t}=e^{\bf r}tf^{\bf t}
,
t\in U_{\varepsilon}({\mathfrak{h}})
, where we use the notation of Lemma
2.7.2
. Here the generators
f_{\alpha},e_{\alpha},\alpha\in\Delta_{+}
and the ordered products of them are defined with the help of the normal ordering of
\Delta_{+}
associated to
s
. Define also the height of the element

u_{{\bf r},{\bf t},t}
as follows
{\rm ht}~(u_{{\bf r},{\bf t},t})=\sum_{i=1}^{D}(t_{i}+r_{i}){\rm ht}~\beta_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
, where
{\rm ht}~\beta_{i}
is the height of the root
\beta_{i}
. Introduce also the degree of
u_{{\bf r},{\bf t},t}
by

d(u_{{\bf r},{\bf t},t})=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{D},t_{D},\ldots,t_{1},{\rm ht}~(u_{{\bf r},{\bf t},t}))\in\mathbb{N}^{2D+1}.

Equip
\mathbb{N}^{2D+1}
with the total lexicographic order and for
k\in\mathbb{N}^{2D+1}
denote by
(U_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{g}))_{k}
the span of elements
u_{{\bf r},{\bf t},t}
with
d(u_{{\bf r},{\bf t},t})\leq k
in
U_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{g})
. Then Proposition 1.7 in
[
23
]
implies that
(U_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{g}))_{k}
is a filtration of
U_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{g})
such that the associated graded algebra is the associative algebra over
\mathbb{C}
with generators
e_{\alpha},~f_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\Delta_{+}
,
L_{i}^{\pm 1}
,
i=1,\ldots l
subject to the relations

\begin{array}[]{l}L_{i}L_{j}=L_{j}L_{i},~~L_{i}L_{i}^{-1}=L_{i}^{-1}L_{i}=1,~~L_{i}e_{\alpha}L_{i}^{-1}=\varepsilon^{\alpha(Y_{i})}e_{\alpha},~~L_{i}f_{\alpha}L_{i}^{-1}=\varepsilon^{-\alpha(Y_{i})}f_{\alpha},\\
\\
e_{\alpha}f_{\beta}=\varepsilon^{nd({1+s\over 1-s}P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime*}}\alpha,\beta)}f_{\beta}e_{\alpha},\\
\\
e_{\alpha}e_{\beta}=\varepsilon^{(\alpha,\beta)+nd({1+s\over 1-s}P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime*}}\alpha,\beta)}e_{\beta}e_{\alpha},~\alpha<\beta,\\
\\
f_{\alpha}f_{\beta}=\varepsilon^{(\alpha,\beta)+nd({1+s\over 1-s}P_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime*}}\alpha,\beta)}f_{\beta}f_{\alpha},~\alpha<\beta.\end{array}

(6.3.11)

Such algebras are called semi–commutative.

By Theorem 61.3 in
[
30
]
it suffices to show that there is a non–degenerate bilinear form
B_{\eta}:U_{\eta}({\mathfrak{g}})\times U_{\eta}({\mathfrak{g}})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
which is associative in the sense that

B_{\eta}(ab,c)=B_{\eta}(a,bc),~~a,b,c\in U_{\eta}({\mathfrak{g}}).

Consider the free
Z_{0}
–basis of
U_{\varepsilon}({\mathfrak{g}})
introduced in part (ii) of Proposition
6.1.3
. This basis consists of the monomials
x_{I}=f^{\bf r}L^{\bf s}e^{\bf t}
,
I=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{D},s_{1},\ldots,s_{l},t_{1},\ldots,t_{D})
for which
0\leq r_{k},t_{k},s_{i}<m
for
i=1,\ldots,l
,
k=1,\ldots,D
. Set
I^{\prime}=(m-1-r_{1},\ldots,m-1-r_{D},m-1-s_{1},\ldots,m-1-s_{l},m-1-t_{1},\ldots,m-1-t_{D})
and
P=(m-1,\ldots,m-1)
.

Let
\Phi:U_{\varepsilon}({\mathfrak{g}})\rightarrow Z_{0}
be the
Z_{0}
–linear map defined on the basis
x_{I}
of monomials by

\Phi(x_{I})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&I=P\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{array}\right..

Let
x=\sum_{I}c_{I}x_{I},c_{I}\in Z_{0}
be an element of
U_{\varepsilon}({\mathfrak{g}})
, and
c_{K}\neq 0
a coefficient such that
d(x_{K})
is maximal possible with
c_{K}\neq 0
in the sum defining
x
.

Using the definition of the De Concini–Kac filtration and commutation relations (
6.3.11
) one can check that
\Phi(xx_{K^{\prime}})=a_{x}c_{K}
, where
a_{x}
is a nonzero complex number (see
[
67
]
, proof of Theorem 2.2, Assertion I for details).

Therefore the bilinear form
B_{\eta}:U_{\eta}({\mathfrak{g}})\times U_{\eta}({\mathfrak{g}})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
associated to the associative
Z_{0}
–bilinear pairing
B:U_{\varepsilon}({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{Z_{0}}U_{\varepsilon}({\mathfrak{g}})\rightarrow Z_{0}
,
B(x,y)=\Phi(xy)
is non–degenerate and associative. This completes the proof.

∎

We restate the results of the discussion before the previous proposition as its corollary.

Corollary 6.3.4
.

As a right
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
–module, every module
V\in U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})-{\rm mod}
is isomorphic to
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})\otimes V_{\chi}
, where the right action of
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
on
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})\otimes V_{\chi}
is induced by the multiplication in
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
from the right. In particular,
V
is
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
–free.

6.4
Properties of q-W–algebras associated to quantum groups at roots of unity

In conclusion we study some further properties of q-W–algebras at roots of unity and of the module
Q_{\chi}
. We keep the notation introduced in the previous section. First we prove the following lemma.

Lemma 6.4.1
.

The left
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
–module
Q_{\chi}
is projective in the category
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})-{\rm mod}
.

Proof.

We have to show that the functor
{\rm Hom}_{U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})}(Q_{\chi},\cdot)
is exact. Let
V^{\bullet}
be an exact complex of finite–dimensional
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
–modules. Since by the previous theorem any object
V
of
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})-{\rm mod}
is isomorphic to
{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-}),V_{\chi})
as a right
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
–module we have

V^{\bullet}\simeq{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-}),\overline{V}^{\bullet}),

where
\overline{V}^{\bullet}
is an exact complex of vector spaces and the action of
U_{\eta_{1}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})
on
{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-}),\overline{V}^{\bullet})
is induced by multiplication from the left on
U_{\eta_{1}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})
.

Now by the Frobenius reciprocity we have obvious isomorphisms of complexes,

\displaystyle{\rm Hom}_{U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})}(Q_{\chi},V^{\bullet})\simeq{\rm Hom}_{U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})}(Q_{\chi},V^{\bullet})={\rm Hom}_{U_{\xi}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})}(\mathbb{C}_{\chi},V^{\bullet})\simeq

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Hom}_{U_{\eta_{1}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})}(\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon},{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-}),\overline{V}^{\bullet}))\simeq{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(U_{\eta_{1}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})\otimes_{U_{\eta_{1}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})}\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon},\overline{V}^{\bullet})=\overline{V}^{\bullet},

where the last complex is exact, and we used the fact that by Lemma
6.2.3
for any finite–dimensional
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
–module
V
one has
{\rm Hom}_{U_{\xi}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})}(\mathbb{C}_{\chi},V)\simeq{\rm Hom}_{U_{\eta_{1}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})}(\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon},V)
. We conclude that the functor
{\rm Hom}_{U_{\xi}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})}(Q_{\chi},\cdot)
is exact.

∎

The properties of q-W–algebras at roots of unity are summarized in the following proposition.

Proposition 6.4.2
.

Denote
b=m^{{\rm dim}~\mathfrak{m}_{-}}=m^{\frac{1}{2}{\rm dim}~\mathcal{O}_{\pi\xi}}
. Then
Q_{\chi}^{b}\simeq U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
as left
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
–modules,
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})\simeq{\rm Mat}_{b}(W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G))
as algebras and
Q_{\chi}\simeq(W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)^{opp})^{b}
as right
W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)
–modules.

Proof.

Let
E_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,C
be the simple finite–dimensional modules over the finite–dimensional algebra
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
. Denote by
P_{i}
the projective cover of
E_{i}
. Since by Theorem
6.3.2
the dimension of
E_{i}
is divisible by
b
we have
{\rm dim}~E_{i}=br_{i}
,
r_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
, where
r_{i}
is the rank of
E_{i}
over
U_{\eta_{1}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})
equal to the dimension of the space of Whittaker vectors in
E_{i}
. By Proposition 2.1 in
[
87
]

U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})={\rm Mat}_{b}({\rm End}_{U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})}(P)^{opp}),

where
P=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{C}P_{i}^{r_{i}}
. Therefore to prove the second statement of the proposition it suffices to show that
P\simeq Q_{\chi}
. Since by the previous lemma
Q_{\chi}
is projective we only need to verify that

r_{i}={\rm dim}~{\rm Hom}_{U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})}(P,E_{i})={\rm dim}~{\rm Hom}_{U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})}(Q_{\chi},E_{i}).

Indeed, by the Frobenius reciprocity we have

{\rm dim}~{\rm Hom}_{U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})}(Q_{\chi},E_{i})={\rm dim}~{\rm Hom}_{U_{\xi}({\mathfrak{m}}_{-})}(\mathbb{C}_{\chi},E_{i})=r_{i}.

This proves the second statement of the proposition. From Proposition 2.1 in
[
87
]
we also deduce that
P^{b}\simeq U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
as left
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
–modules. Together with the isomorphism
P\simeq Q_{\chi}
this gives the first statement of the proposition.

Using results of Section 6.4 in
[
85
]
and the fact that
Q_{\chi}
is projective one can find an idempotent
e\in U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
such that
Q_{\chi}\simeq U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})e
as modules and
(W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G))^{opp}\simeq eU_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})e
as algebras.

By the first two statements of this proposition one can also find idempotents
e=e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{b}\in U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
such that
e_{1}+\ldots+e_{b}=1
,
e_{i}e_{j}=0
if
i\neq j
and
e_{i}U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})\simeq eU_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
as right
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
–modules. Therefore
e_{i}U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})e\simeq eU_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})e
as right
eU_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})e
–modules, and

Q_{\chi}\simeq U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})e=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{b}e_{i}U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})e\simeq(eU_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})e)^{b}\simeq(W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)^{opp})^{b}

as right
W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)
–modules.
This completes the proof of the proposition

∎

Corollary 6.4.3
.

The algebra
W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)
is finite–dimensional, and
{\rm dim}~W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)=m^{{\rm dim}~\Sigma_{s}}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.4.5

2{\rm dim}~\mathfrak{m}_{-}+{\rm dim}~\Sigma_{s}={\rm dim}~G
. Therefore by the definition of
Q_{\chi}
we have
{\rm dim}~Q_{\chi}=m^{{\rm dim}~G-{\rm dim}~\mathfrak{m}_{-}}=m^{{\rm dim}~\mathfrak{m}_{-}+{\rm dim}~\Sigma_{s}}
. Finally from the last statement of the previous theorem one obtains that
{\rm dim}~W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G)={\rm dim}~Q_{\chi}/m^{{\rm dim}~\mathfrak{m}_{-}}=m^{{\rm dim}~\Sigma_{s}}
.

∎

Using Proposition
6.1.1
we deduce from Proposition
6.4.2
the following statement on the structure of the algebra
U_{\eta}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Corollary 6.4.4
.

Let
\eta\in{\rm Spec}(Z_{0})
be an element such that
\pi\eta\in G_{\mathcal{C}}
,
\mathcal{C}\in C(W)
and
s^{-1}\in\mathcal{C}
,
d=2d^{\prime}
, where
d^{\prime}
is defined in Proposition
6.1.1
. Assume that
m
and
d
are coprime.

Then
U_{\eta}(\mathfrak{g})\simeq{\rm Mat}_{b}(W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G))
, where
\xi\in{\rm Spec}Z_{0}
is chosen as in Proposition
6.4.2
, and
b=m^{\frac{1}{2}{\rm dim}~\mathcal{O}_{\pi\eta}}
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a sheaf of algebras over
{\rm Spec}Z_{0}
the stalk of which over
\eta\in{\rm Spec}Z_{0}
is
U_{\eta}(\mathfrak{g})
. Assume that the conditions imposed on
m
are satisfied for all Weyl group conjugacy classes in
C(W)
.
Then the sheaf
\mathcal{L}
is isomorphic to a sheaf the stalk of which over any
\eta\in{\rm Spec}Z_{0}
with
\pi\eta\in G^{0}\cap G_{\mathcal{C}}
,
\mathcal{C}\in C(W)
is
{\rm Mat}_{b}(W^{s}_{\varepsilon,\xi}(G))
, where
\xi\in{\rm Spec}Z_{0}
is chosen as in Proposition
6.4.2
,
s^{-1}\in\mathcal{C}
,
b=m^{\frac{1}{2}{\rm dim}~\mathcal{O}_{\pi\eta}}
.

6.5
Bibliographic comments

The study of representations of quantum groups at roots of unity was initiated in
[
23
]
, where the quantum coadjoint action was defined as well. This action was studied in detail in
[
25
]
where the De Concini–Kac–Procesi conjecture on the dimensions of irreducible representations of quantum groups at roots of unity was formulated.

The results on quantum groups at roots of unity stated in Section
6.1
can be found in
[
23
]
and
[
25
]
.
Proposition
6.1.1
first appeared in Appendix A to
[
108
]
.
The statements of Proposition
6.1.2
can be found in
[
23
]
, Corollary 3.3,
[
25
]
, Theorems 3.5, 7.6 and Proposition 4.5.
Proposition
6.1.4
is Corollary 4.7 in
[
25
]
, and the statements of Proposition
6.1.5
appear in Propositions 3.4, 3.5,
[
23
]
, and in Proposition 6.1 and Theorem 6.6 in
[
25
]
.
Finite–dimensional quotients
U_{\eta}(\mathfrak{g})
were introduced in
[
24
]
.

The notions of Whittaker vectors for representations of quantum groups at roots of unity, of the algebras
U_{\eta_{1}}(\mathfrak{m}_{-})
, and of their actions on finite–dimensional representations of
{U}_{\eta}(\mathfrak{g})
were introduced in
[
107
]
, and the exposition in Section
6.2
follows
[
107
]
as well.

In the representation theory of Lie algebras in prime characteristic there is a conjecture similar to the De Concini–Kac–Procesi conjecture. It is called the Kac–Weisfeiler conjecture. Our proof of the the De Concini–Kac–Procesi conjecture is conceptually similar to the proof of the Kac–Weisfeiler conjecture given in
[
110
]
which is in turn a straightforward prime characteristic generalization of the proof of the Skryabin equivalence for reductive Lie algebras over algebraically closed fields of zero characteristic suggested in the Appendix to
[
86
]
. All these proofs go back to the original Kostant’s idea on the proof of the classification theorem for Whittaker representations of complex semisimple Lie algebras in
[
65
]
, the proof of the Skryabin equivalence in
[
86
]
being a significantly refined and simplified version of the proof of the main Theorem 3.3 in
[
65
]
.

The properties of q-W–algeras at roots of unity are similar to those of W–algebras associated to semisimple Lie algebras in prime characteristic proved in
[
87
]
,
Proposition
6.4.2
being an analogue of Theorem 2.3 in
[
87
]
.

Appendix

Appendix 1. Normal orderings of root systems compatible with involutions in Weyl groups

By Theorem A in
[
91
]
every involution
w
in the Weyl group
W
of the pair
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
is the longest element of the Weyl group of a Levi subalgebra in
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to some system of positive roots, and
w
acts by multiplication by
-1
in the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}_{w}\subset\mathfrak{h}
of the semisimple part
\mathfrak{m}_{w}
of that Levi subalgebra. By Lemma 5 in
[
18
]
the involution
w
can also be expressed as a product of
{\rm dim}~\mathfrak{h}_{w}
reflections from the Weyl group of the pair
(\mathfrak{m}_{w},\mathfrak{h}_{w})
, with respect to mutually orthogonal roots,
w=s_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots s_{\gamma_{n}}
, and the roots
{\gamma_{1}},\ldots,{\gamma_{n}}
span the subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}_{w}
.

If
w
is the longest element in the Weyl group of the pair
(\mathfrak{m}_{w},\mathfrak{h}_{w})
with respect to some system of positive roots, where
\mathfrak{m}_{w}
is a simple Lie algebra and
\mathfrak{h}_{w}
is a Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{m}_{w}
, then
w
is an involution acting by multiplication by
-1
in
\mathfrak{h}_{w}
if and only if
\mathfrak{m}_{w}
is of one of the following types:
A_{1},B_{l},C_{l},D_{2n},E_{7},E_{8},F_{4},G_{2}
.

Fix a system of positive roots
\Delta_{+}(\mathfrak{m}_{w},\mathfrak{h}_{w})
of the pair
(\mathfrak{m}_{w},\mathfrak{h}_{w})
. Let
w=s_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots s_{\gamma_{n}}
be a representation of
w
as a product of
{\rm dim}~\mathfrak{h}_{w}
reflections from the Weyl group of the pair
(\mathfrak{m}_{w},\mathfrak{h}_{w})
, with respect to mutually orthogonal positive roots. A normal ordering of
\Delta_{+}(\mathfrak{m}_{w},\mathfrak{h}_{w})
is called compatible with the decomposition
w=s_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots s_{\gamma_{n}}
if it is of the following form

\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{\frac{p-n}{2}},\gamma_{1},\beta_{\frac{p-n}{2}+2}^{1},\ldots,\beta_{\frac{p-n}{2}+n_{1}}^{1},\gamma_{2},\beta_{\frac{p-n}{2}+n_{1}+2}^{1}\ldots,\beta_{\frac{p-n}{2}+n_{2}}^{1},\gamma_{3},\ldots,\gamma_{n},

where
p
is the number of positive roots, and for any two positive roots
\alpha,\beta\in\Delta_{+}(\mathfrak{m}_{w},\mathfrak{h}_{w})
such that
\gamma_{1}\leq\alpha<\beta
the sum
\alpha+\beta
cannot be represented as a linear combination
\sum_{k=1}^{q}c_{k}\gamma_{i_{k}}
, where
c_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha<\gamma_{i_{1}}<\ldots<\gamma_{i_{k}}<\beta
.

Existence of such compatible normal orderings is checked straightforwardly for all simple Lie algebras of types
A_{1},B_{l},C_{l},D_{2n},E_{7},E_{8},F_{4}
and
G_{2}
. In case
A_{1}
this is obvious since there is only one positive root. In the other cases normal orderings defined by the properties described below for each of the types
B_{l},C_{l},D_{2n},E_{7},E_{8},F_{4},G_{2}
exist and are compatible with decompositions of nontrivial involutions in Weyl group.
We use the Bourbaki notation for the systems of positive and simple roots (see
[
10
]
).

•

\textstyle{{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\alpha_{2}}

Simple roots:
\alpha_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{8})-\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{3}+\varepsilon_{4}+\varepsilon_{5}+\varepsilon_{6}+\varepsilon_{7})
,

\alpha_{2}=\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2},\alpha_{3}=\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{1},

\alpha_{4}=\varepsilon_{3}-\varepsilon_{2},\alpha_{5}=\varepsilon_{4}-\varepsilon_{3},\alpha_{6}=\varepsilon_{5}-\varepsilon_{4},\alpha_{7}=\varepsilon_{6}-\varepsilon_{5},\alpha_{8}=\varepsilon_{7}-\varepsilon_{6}
.

Positive roots:
\pm\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}

(1\leq i<j\leq 8)
,

\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{8}+\sum_{i=1}^{7}(-1)^{\nu(i)}\varepsilon_{i})
with
\sum_{i=1}^{7}{\nu(i)}
even.

The longest element of the Weyl group expressed as a product of
{\rm dim}~\mathfrak{h}_{w}
reflections with respect to mutually orthogonal roots:

w=s_{\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{1}}s_{\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{1}}s_{\varepsilon_{4}-\varepsilon_{3}}s_{\varepsilon_{4}+\varepsilon_{3}}s_{\varepsilon_{6}-\varepsilon_{5}}s_{\varepsilon_{6}+\varepsilon_{5}}s_{\varepsilon_{8}-\varepsilon_{7}}s_{\varepsilon_{8}+\varepsilon_{7}}.

Normal ordering of
\Delta_{+}(\mathfrak{m}_{w},\mathfrak{h}_{w})
compatible with expression

w=s_{\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{1}}s_{\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{1}}s_{\varepsilon_{4}-\varepsilon_{3}}s_{\varepsilon_{4}+\varepsilon_{3}}s_{\varepsilon_{6}-\varepsilon_{5}}s_{\varepsilon_{6}+\varepsilon_{5}}s_{\varepsilon_{8}-\varepsilon_{7}}s_{\varepsilon_{8}-\varepsilon_{7}}:

\displaystyle\alpha_{1},\varepsilon_{3}-\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{5}-\varepsilon_{4},\varepsilon_{7}-\varepsilon_{6},\ldots,\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{4}-\varepsilon_{3},\varepsilon_{6}-\varepsilon_{5},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{8}-\varepsilon_{7},\ldots,\varepsilon_{8}+\varepsilon_{7},\ldots,\varepsilon_{6}+\varepsilon_{5},\ldots,\varepsilon_{4}+\varepsilon_{3},\ldots,\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{1},

where the roots
\pm\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}

(1\leq i<j\leq 8)
forming the subsystem
\Delta_{+}(D_{8})\subset\Delta_{+}(E_{8})
are placed as in case of the compatible normal ordering of the system
\Delta_{+}(D_{8})
, the roots
\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}

(1\leq i<j\leq 8)
are situated to the right from
\varepsilon_{8}+\varepsilon_{7}
; the positive roots which do not belong to the subsystem
\Delta_{+}(D_{8})\subset\Delta_{+}(E_{8})
can be split into two groups: the roots from the first group contain
\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{8}+\varepsilon_{7})
in their decompositions with respect to the basis
\varepsilon_{i},i=1,\ldots,8
, and the roots from the second group contain
\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{8}-\varepsilon_{7})
in their decompositions with respect to the basis
\varepsilon_{i},i=1,\ldots,8
; a half of the roots from the first group are situated to the left from
\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{1}
and the other half of those roots are situated to the right from
\varepsilon_{8}+\varepsilon_{7}
; a half of the roots from the second group are situated to the left from
\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{1}
and the other half of those roots are situated to the right from
\varepsilon_{8}-\varepsilon_{7}
.

Appendix 2. Transversal slices for simple exceptional algebraic groups.

In this appendix, for simple exceptional algebraic groups we present the data related to the varieties
\Sigma_{s,{\bf k}}
defined in Theorem
1.5.2
.
Let
G_{\bf k}
be a connected simple algebraic group of an exceptional type over an algebraically closed field
{\bf k}
of characteristic good for
G_{\bf k}
, and
\mathcal{O}\in\widehat{\underline{\mathcal{N}}}(G_{\bf k})
. Let
H_{\bf k}
be a maximal torus of
G_{\bf k}
,
W
the Weyl group of the pair
(G_{\bf k},H_{\bf k})
, and
s\in W
an element from the conjugacy class
\Psi^{W}(\mathcal{O})
.

Let
\Delta
be the root system of the pair
(G_{\bf k},H_{\bf k})
and
\Delta_{+}^{s}
the system of positive roots in
\Delta
associated to
s
and defined in Section
1.2
with the help of decomposition (
1.2.14
) where the subspaces
\mathfrak{h}_{i}
are ordered in such a way that in sum (
1.2.14
)
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
is the linear subspace of
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{R}}
fixed by the action of
s
, the one–dimensional subspaces
\mathfrak{h}_{i}
on which
s^{1}
acts by multiplication by
-1
are immediately preceding
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
in (
1.2.14
), and if
\mathfrak{h}_{i}=\mathfrak{h}_{\lambda}^{k}
,
\mathfrak{h}_{j}=\mathfrak{h}_{\mu}^{l}
and
0\leq\lambda<\mu<1
then
i<j
. We also use a decomposition
s=s^{1}s^{2}
for which the direct sum
\bigoplus_{k=0,i_{k}>0}^{r}\mathfrak{h}_{i_{k}}
of the one–dimensional subspaces
\mathfrak{h}_{i_{k}}
on which
s^{1}
acts by multiplication by
-1
is trivial. Such decomposition always exists. As a consequence condition (
1.6.2
) is satisfied.
Let
\Sigma_{s,{\bf k}}
be the corresponding variety defined in the beginning of Section
1.5
.

Then straightforward calculation shows that

{\rm dim}~Z_{G_{p}}(n)={\rm dim}~\Sigma_{s,{\bf k}}

for any
n\in\mathcal{O}\in\underline{\mathcal{N}}(G_{p})\subset\widehat{\underline{\mathcal{N}}}(G)
. The numbers
{\rm dim}~Z_{G_{p}}(n)
can be found in
[
68
]
, Chapter 22 (note, however, that the notation in
[
68
]
for some classes is different from ours; we follow
[
80
,
113
]
). The numbers
{\rm dim}~\Sigma_{s,{\bf k}}
are contained in the tables below. These two numbers coincide in all cases. The tables below contain also the following information for each
\mathcal{O}\in\widehat{\underline{\mathcal{N}}}(G_{\bf k})
:

– The Weyl group conjugacy class
\Psi^{W}(\mathcal{O})
which can be found in
[
80
]
;

– The two involutions
s^{1}
and
s^{2}
in the decomposition
s=s^{1}s^{2}\in\Psi^{W}(\mathcal{O})
; they are represented by sets of natural numbers which are the numbers of roots appearing in decompositions
s^{1}=s_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots s_{\gamma_{n}}
,
s^{2}=s_{\gamma_{n+1}}\ldots s_{\gamma_{l^{\prime}}}
, where the system of positive roots
\Delta_{+}^{s}
is chosen as in Theorem
1.5.2
, and the numeration of positive roots is given in Appendix 3;

– The dimension of the fixed point space
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
for the action of
s
on
\mathfrak{h}
;

– The number
|\Delta_{0}|
of roots fixed by
s
;

– The type of the root system
\Delta_{0}
fixed by
s
;

– The Dynkin diagram
\Gamma_{0}^{s}
of
\Delta_{0}
, where the numbers at the vertices of
\Gamma_{0}^{s}
are the numbers of simple roots in
\Delta_{+}^{s}
which appear in
\Gamma_{0}^{s}
; the numeration of simple roots is given in Appendix 3;

– The length
l(s)
of
s
with respect to the system of simple roots in
\Delta_{+}^{s}
;

–
{\rm dim}~\Sigma_{s,{\bf k}}={\rm dim}~\mathfrak{h}_{0}+|\Delta_{0}|+l(s)
;

– The lowest common multiple
d^{\prime}
of the denominators of the numbers
\frac{1}{d_{j}}\left({1+s\over 1-s}P_{{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}^{*}}\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}\right)
, where
i,j=1,\ldots,l
;

Appendix 3. Irreducible root systems of exceptional types.

In this Appendix we give the lists of positive roots in irreducible root systems of exceptional types. All simple roots are numbered as shown at the Dynkin diagrams. The other roots in each list are given in terms of their coordinates with respect to the basis of simple roots. The coordinates are indicated in the brackets ( ). Each set of coordinates is preceded by the number of the corresponding root. These numbers are used to indicate roots which appear in the columns
s^{1}
,
s^{2}
and
\Gamma_{0}^{s}
in the tables in Appendix 2.
